The fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period
Paul Boisseau

Abstract
We state and prove the spectral expansion of the theta series attached to the Rankin–Selberg spherical variety ( GL n + 1 × GL n ) / GL n (\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n})/\mathrm{GL}_{n} . This is a key result towards the fine spectral expansion of the Jacquet–Rallis trace formula. Our expansion is written in terms of regularized Rankin–Selberg periods for non-tempered automorphic representations, which we show compute special values of L L -functions. The proof relies on shifts of contours of integration à la Langlands. We also establish two technical but crucial results on bounds and singularities for discrete Eisenstein series of GL n \mathrm{GL}_{n} in the positive Weyl chamber.



1
Introduction

1.1
Motivations

1.1.1
Spectral decomposition of the Rankin–Selberg spherical variety

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and let
F
be a number field with ring of adeles
\mathbb{A}
. For any algebraic group
\mathbb{G}
over
F
, set
[\mathbb{G}]:=\mathbb{G}(F)\backslash\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{A})
. Set
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
and
H=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, both considered as algebraic groups over
F
. Embed
H
as a subgroup of
G
using the map
h\mapsto\left(h,\begin{pmatrix}h&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)
, and consider the affine
G
-spherical
Rankin–Selberg
variety
X=G/H
. For any Schwartz function
\Phi
on
X(\mathbb{A})
we may form the theta series

\Theta^{X}_{\Phi}(g)=\sum_{x\in X(F)}\Phi(g^{-1}.x),\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

The series
\Theta^{X}_{\Phi}
form a
G(\mathbb{A})
-invariant subspace of the space of smooth functions on
[G]
. Our goal is to describe this representation in terms of the automorphic spectrum of
G
.

We may reformulate the problem as follows. Because
X(\mathbb{A})=G(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
, any Schwartz function on
X(\mathbb{A})
can be obtained from a Schwartz function
f
on
G(\mathbb{A})
via the integral

\Phi(x)=\int_{H(\mathbb{A})}f(gh)dh,

where
dh
is a Haar-measure on
H(\mathbb{A})
and
g
is any representative of
x
in
G(\mathbb{A})
. We can consider the automorphic kernel

K_{f}(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in G(F)}f(x^{-1}\gamma y),\quad x,y\in G(\mathbb{A}),

(1.1)

and form the Rankin–Selberg automorphic kernel

J^{H}(g,f)=\int_{[H]}K_{f}(g,h)dh,\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}),

where
[H]
is equipped with the quotient measure. Because
X(F)=G(F)\backslash H(F)
, we have
\Theta^{X}_{\Phi}(g)=J^{H}(g,f)
and we now need to write the spectral decomposition of the distribution
f\mapsto J^{H}(g,f)
.

1.1.2
The Jacquet–Rallis relative trace formula

The motivation to study the distribution
J^{H}(g,f)
stems from the Jacquet–Rallis relative trace formula introduced in
[
32
]
. More precisely, let
E
be a quadratic extension of
F
, and write
G_{E}
and
H_{E}
for the restriction of scalars
\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}(\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1})
and
\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}GL_{n}
respectively. We have the distribution
J^{H_{E}}(g,f)
. Inside
G_{E}
lies the subgroup
G^{\prime}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
. The integral along
[G^{\prime}]
is the
Flicker–Rallis
studied in
[
22
]
. We can write any
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
as
g^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{n},g^{\prime}_{n+1})
. Let
\eta
be the quadratic character associated to
E/F
by class field theory. For
f
a Schwartz function on
G_{E}(\mathbb{A})
, the Jacquet–Rallis relative trace formula for general linear groups introduced in
[
54
]
is the regularized version of the integral

\int_{[G^{\prime}]}J^{H_{E}}(g^{\prime},f)\eta(\det(g^{\prime}_{n}))^{n+1}\eta(\det(g^{\prime}_{n+1}))^{n}dg^{\prime}.

(1.2)

It plays a significant role in recent works on the global Gan–Gross–Prasad and Ichino–Ikeda conjectures for unitary groups, in particular
[
53
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
11
]
and
[
12
]
. To any cuspidal datum
\chi
of
G_{E}
we can attach a distribution
J^{H_{E}}_{\chi}(g,f)
obtained by integrating the
K_{\chi}
part of the kernel. A key result obtained in
[
11
]
is the spectral decomposition of
J^{H_{E}}_{\chi}(g,f)
for certain
regular
cuspidal data
\chi
. Combined with an analog result for the Flicker–Rallis period, the authors of
[
11
]
are able to derive the regular part of the spectral expansion of the Jacquet–Rallis trace formula. This turns out to be sufficient to prove the conjecture of
[
23
]
for cuspidal generic representations of unitary groups. However, to tackle the non-tempered version of the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture introduced in
[
24
]
it is necessary to understand all residual and Eisenstein contributions to the trace formula.

In this regard, our paper provides the fine spectral expansion of the distribution
J^{H_{E}}_{\chi}(g,f)
with no restriction on cuspidal data. This is an important step towards the fine spectral expansion of the trace formula itself. In
[
19
]
, Chaudouard proved the corresponding result for the Flicker–Rallis distributions
J^{G^{\prime}}(g,f)
obtained by considering the symmetric space
G_{E}(\mathbb{A})/G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})
. It now remains to combine the two expansions to derive that of (
1.2
), which we will do in a future work.

1.2
The fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period

1.2.1
Preliminary notations

Before stating the main result of this paper, we fix some notations. For every place
v
of
F
,
F_{v}
is the localization of
F
at
v
. We say that a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
is semi-standard if it contains the torus of diagonal matrices, and standard if it contains the Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices. In this last case, it admits a standard Levi decomposition
P=M_{P}N_{P}
, where
N_{P}
is the unipotent radical of
P
. Let
\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
be the set of discrete irreducible automorphic representations of
M_{P}
, with trivial central character on the central subgroup
A_{P}^{\infty}
(see §
3.2.1
). If
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
, we can write its decomposition into local components
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
. We also have the space
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
of automorphic forms on
A_{P}^{\infty}M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
induced from
\pi
(see §
3.4.2
). Let
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
be the complex vector space of unramified characters of
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
, and let
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
be its real subspace of unitary characters. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have a map
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}
that identifies
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
with the induction
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
of
\pi\otimes\lambda
. A Schwartz function
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
acts on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
and thus on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
by transporting the structure, and we denote this action by
I_{P}(f,\lambda)
. We finally write
E(\varphi,\lambda)
for the Eisenstein series induced from
\varphi_{\lambda}
(see §
3.4.6
).

1.2.2
Relevant inducing data

Let
k\geq 1
. By
[
45
]
, any discrete automorphic representation
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{k})
is obtained by taking residues of Eisenstein series of automorphic forms in an induction
\mathcal{A}_{P,\sigma^{\boxtimes d}}(\mathrm{GL}_{k})
, where
P
is a standard parabolic subgroup with
M_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{d}
,
\sigma
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{r}
and
r
and
d
are integers with
rd=k
. We then write
\pi=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
. By
[
36
]
, inductions of discrete automorphic representations exhaust the spectral decomposition of
L^{2}([\mathrm{GL}_{k}])
. We will refer to these representations as being of
Arthur type
.

By analogy with the local notion of derivatives introduced in
[
9
]
, we define the automorphic derivative of
\pi=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
to be

\pi^{-}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d-1)\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r(d-1)}).

Note that if
\pi
is cuspidal, i.e. if
d=1
, then
\pi^{-}
is the trivial representation of the trivial group. We also write
\pi^{\vee}
for the dual of
\pi
, and we easily check that
\pi^{-,\vee}=\pi^{\vee,-}
.

Our spectral expansion of
J^{H}
will be indexed by a set
\Pi_{H}
of
relevant inducing data
. This is the set of triples
(I,P,\pi)
satisfying the following desiderata.

•

I
is a tuple of non-negative integers
(n_{+},n_{1},n_{2},n_{-})
such that
n_{2}^{-}:=n-n_{+}-n_{1}-n_{-}
and
n_{1}^{-}:=n+1-n_{+}-n_{2}-n_{-}
are non-negative. We then let
P_{I}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with standard Levi subgroup

M_{I}:=\left(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{2}^{-}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}\right)\times\left(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}^{-}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{2}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}\right).

(1.3)

•

P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
included in
P_{I}
.

•

\pi
is a discrete automorphic representation of
M_{P}
which, with respect to (
1.3
), decomposes as

\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i}\right)\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i}^{\vee}\right),

(1.4)

where
m_{+},m_{1},m_{2}
and
m_{-}
are non-negative integers, and all the
\pi_{\ldots}
are discrete automorphic representations of some general linear groups.

Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. We associate to this triple three additional pieces of data.

•

Let
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
be the subspace of unramified characters
\lambda
of
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
such that
(I,P,\pi\otimes\lambda)
belongs to
\Pi_{H}
if we lift the requirement that the restriction of the central character of
\pi\otimes\lambda
to
A_{P}^{\infty}
is trivial. We refer to (
6.7
) for an explicit description of
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in coordinates. We denote by
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
its subset of unitary characters.

•

With respect to the basis coming from the lattice of algebraic characters in
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{I},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
(see §
3.1.1
), let
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
be the element in
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{I},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with coordinates

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=((1/4,0,0,-1/4),(1/4,0,0,-1/4)).

Note that
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
does not belong to
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
unless
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
, in which case it is zero.

•

Let
W(\pi)
be the subset of the Weyl group of
G
defined in §
6.1.4
. It has the property that, for any
w\in W(\pi)
, if we write
w.P
for the unique standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with standard Levi subgroup
wM_{P}w^{-1}
, and
w.\pi
for the discrete automorphic representation of
wM_{P}w^{-1}
obtained by conjugation, we have
(I,w.P,w.\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
.

Note that
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
,
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
and
W(\pi)
really depend on
(I,P,\pi)
, but we only highlight the relation to
\pi
to ease notations.

Example 1.1
.

Take
I=(0,n,n+1,0)
. Then
P_{I}
is
G
. It follows that triples
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
are in bijection with couples
(P,\pi)
where
P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
M_{P}
. Indeed, with the notation of (
1.4
),
\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}
and
\pi_{1,j}^{-,\vee}
for
1\leq i\leq m_{2}
and
1\leq j\leq m_{1}
are representations of the trivial group. Moreover,
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
,
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=0
and
W(\pi)
is the subset
W(P)
of Weyl elements permutating the blocks of
M_{P}
.

Example 1.2
.

Take
I=(0,0,n+1,0)
. Then
P_{I}
is again
G
. However, if
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
, then
\pi
is of the form
\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\right)\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}\right)
. This is only possible if
m_{2}=1
and
\pi_{2,1}=\mathrm{Speh}(\chi,n+1)
for
\chi
an automorphic character of
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
, so that
\pi
is the character
\chi^{\vee}\circ\det\boxtimes\chi\circ\det
of
G
. By a remark of
[
24
, Section 9]
, all residual representations of
G
which admit a
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant linear form are of this shape. In this case,
P=G
,
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is the diagonal subspace
(\lambda,-\lambda)
in
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
,
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=0
and
W(\pi)
is trivial.

Example 1.3
.

We now give a non-Arthur example coming from
\Pi_{H}
. Take
I=(n,0,1,0)
so that
P_{I}
has standard Levi
M_{I}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times(\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1})
. Then for
(I,P,\pi)
to belong to
\Pi_{H}
,
P
has to be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with
M_{P}
of the form
M_{+}\times(M_{+}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1})
, and
\pi
to be a discrete automorphic representation of
M_{P}
which decomposes as
\pi_{+}\boxtimes(\pi_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes\chi)
accordingly, where
\pi_{+}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{+})
and
\chi
is an automorphic character of
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
. Relatively to
P\subset P_{I}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is the subspace
(\lambda,(-\lambda,\mu))
where
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{M_{+},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
\mu\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{GL}_{1},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
,
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=(1/4,(1/4,0))
. Finally, if we set
P_{+}=P\cap H
, then
W(\pi)=W(P_{+})\subset H\subset G
.

1.2.3
Regularized periods

We now give some motivation for the definition of
\Pi_{H}
. For
\varphi
an automorphic form on
G
, consider the (a priori non-convergent)
Rankin–Selberg
period

\mathcal{P}_{H}(\varphi)=\int_{[H]}\varphi(h)dh.

If
\varphi
belongs to a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
G
(so that the integral converges), a celebrated theorem of
[
31
]
states that
\mathcal{P}_{H}
vanishes if an only if the central value of the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L(1/2,\pi)
does. In
[
24
]
, Gan, Gross and Prasad conjectured that the restriction of (a suitable regularization of)
\mathcal{P}_{H}
to an arbitrary automorphic representation
\pi
of
G
of Arthur type should vanish unless the Arthur parameter of
\pi
was
relevant
and a special value of a certain quotient of
L
-functions was non-zero. If we specify the definition of
\Pi_{H}
to the case
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
, then these relevant representations singled out by
[
24
]
are exactly the inductions
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
with
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
. They are those expected to appear in the spectral expansion of
J^{H}
. However, it turns out that they are not enough to fully describe it and that additional representations are needed. They are those of the form
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
for
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
and
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. These representations are not of Arthur type as soon as
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\neq 0
.

Our first result is the definition of a regularization of
\mathcal{P}_{H}
on these inductions
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
. By
[
45
]
, there exist
P_{\pi}
a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
,
\sigma_{\pi}
a cuspidal automorphic representation of
M_{P_{\pi}}
and
\nu_{\pi}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}}^{*}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
is spanned by residues of Eisenstein series induced from
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
at
-\nu_{\pi}
. We denote this map by
E^{P,*}(\cdot,0)
. For
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
, we can form the Eisenstein series
E(\phi,\lambda)
and further take its Rankin–Selberg Zeta integral
Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
. It is a meromorphic function in
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
by
[
29
]
. If we identify
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\nu_{\pi}
as an affine subspace of
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we see that it is contained in a finite union of singularities of
Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
which are all affine hyperplanes. By taking iterated residues, we obtain a meromorphic function
\mathrm{Res}\;Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
on
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\nu_{\pi}
. A priori, this construction depends on the order of the residues taken. In the following theorem, by "for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
" in general position we mean that it lies outside of a countable union of affine hyperplanes.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. The following assertions hold.

•

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position, the residue
\mathrm{Res}\;Z(E(\phi,\lambda-\nu_{\pi}))
is independent of the order and factors through
E^{P,*}(\cdot,\lambda-\nu_{\pi}):\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{\pi}}(G)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\cdot,\lambda)
the resulting
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant linear form on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
.

•

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
, the map
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
is meromorphic, and for
\lambda
in general position the map
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
is continuous.

•

For
\varphi=E^{P,*}(\phi,\lambda-\nu_{\pi})\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
with
\phi=\otimes\phi_{v}
and
\lambda
in general position, there exists a finite set of places
\mathtt{S}
such that

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{L}(\pi,\lambda)\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}Z_{\sigma_{\pi},v}^{\natural}(\phi_{v},\lambda-\nu_{\pi}),

where
\mathcal{L}(\pi,\lambda)
is the quotient of Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions described in §
6.1.6
, and the linear forms
Z_{\sigma_{\pi},v}^{\natural}(\phi_{v},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})
are residues of local Zeta integrals built on the inductions
I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}(\sigma_{\pi,v}\otimes(\lambda-\nu_{\pi}))
(see §
5.3.1
).

Example 1.5
.

If
I=(0,n,n+1,0)
and
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
as in Example
1.1
(so that
\pi
is cuspidal), then
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=Z(E(\varphi,\lambda))
. If now
I=(0,0,n+1,0)
as in Example
1.2
, so that
P=G
and
\pi
is a character of
G
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
is simply evaluation at
1
(up to constant). Note that for any
(\lambda,-\lambda)\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
the character
\pi_{(\lambda,-\lambda)}
remains
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant. Finally, take
I=(n,0,1,0)
as in Example
1.3
. We use the notation from there. The representation
\mathcal{A}_{P_{+},\pi_{+}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
is equipped with the Petersson inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{n}
. If
\varphi=\varphi_{n}\otimes\varphi_{n+1}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
, then
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{+},\pi_{+}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
and
g_{n}\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})\mapsto\varphi_{n+1}(g_{n})
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{P_{+},\pi_{+}^{\vee}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})\otimes\left|\det\right|^{1/2}
. It follows that for any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, the linear form

\varphi=\varphi_{n}\otimes\varphi_{n+1}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}}(G)\mapsto\langle\varphi_{n}\otimes\overline{\varphi}_{n+1}\rangle_{n}

is non-zero and
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant. This is our map
\mathcal{P}_{\pi,\lambda}
(up to constant).

In
[
16
]
, we built these periods in the Arthur case, i.e. when
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
, and proved the corresponding version of Theorem
1.4
. The procedure we use in the current paper for the case of general
(I,P,\pi)
is the same, and the main idea is to realize
Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
as a regularization of a truncated period using
[
29
]
and
[
55
]
. The approach of
[
16
]
generalizes quite easily to prove the first and third points of Theorem
1.4
. In
[
16
, Theorem 1.2]
, we additionally studied the vanishing of the period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
. We showed that the local linear forms
Z_{\sigma_{\pi},v}^{\natural}
are always non-zero for
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
, and therefore that
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
vanishes if an only if
\mathcal{L}(\pi,\lambda)
does. This proved the non-tempered Gan–Gross-Prasad conjecture from
[
24
]
. In this text, we will not deal directly with the local factors
Z^{\natural}_{\sigma_{\pi},v}
, and in particular we will not settle the question of their non-vanishing. We leave this question to a future work. Finally, we emphasize that an alternative description of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
using parabolic descent is given in Proposition
5.10
.

We give two conceptual explanations for the appearance of
\Pi_{H}
in the spectral decomposition of
J^{H}
. First, in
[
6
]
Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis and Venkatesh have attached to the spherical variety
X=G/H
a hyperspherical hamiltonian variety
M
(in this case, the contangent bundle
T^{*}X
) and a
\hat{G}(\mathbb{C})=G(\mathbb{C})
-dual variety
M^{\vee}
which here is
T^{*}\mathrm{St}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{St}_{n+1}
the cotangent bundle of the tensor product of the standard representations. According to
[
6
, Conjecture 14.3.5]
, if
\Pi
is an automorphic representation of
G
with Arthur parameter
\Psi
, the (regularized) period
\mathcal{P}_{H}
should be zero on
\Pi
as soon as the set of fixed points for the action of the hypothetical Langlands dual group of
F
via
\Psi
on a Slodowy slice
M^{\vee}_{\mathrm{slice}}
of
M^{\vee}
(which depends on
\Psi
) is empty. Using the reformulation of
[
24
, Section 4]
, one can check that if
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
, then the action of the Arthur parameter of the induced representation
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
on
M^{\vee}_{\mathrm{slice}}
indeed admits fixed points. The second reason comes from local theory. It has recently been checked in
[
47
]
that, over
p
-adic fields, the unitary
H
-distinguished representations of
G
are exactly the local counterparts of the
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
, that is inductions of Speh representations that satisfy the same combinatorial condition as §
1.2.2
. This is consistent with
[
6
]
, and provides further evidence that the linear forms
Z_{\sigma_{\pi},v}^{\natural}
should be non-zero.

1.2.4
The fine spectral expansion

We can finally describe the spectral expansion of
J^{H}
. Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. We write
\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}
for the Hilbert basis of
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
for the Petersson innner product defined in §
3.4.8
. For a Schwartz function
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
,
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position, we can consider the relative character

J_{(I,P,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}}E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\varphi,\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\overline{\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})}.

(1.5)

The regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})
may have poles in the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
, but they are compensated by zeros of the Eisenstein series (which are regular in this region). Their product therefore defines an holomorphic function on
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
which is moreover of rapid decay. The sum in (
1.5
) is absolutely convergent and the map
f\mapsto J_{(I,P,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)
is continuous. All these properties are proved in Lemma
7.2
. Finally, we have functional equations (Corollary
6.5
)

J^{H}_{(I,w.P,w.\pi)}(g,f,w\lambda)=J^{H}_{(I,P,\pi)}(g,f,\lambda),\quad w\in W(\pi).

We now equip
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
with the Haar measure described in §
7.1.1
, which depends on that of
H(\mathbb{A})
. We write the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
be a Schwartz function, let
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
. Then we have

J^{H}(g,f)=\sum_{(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}J_{(I,P,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)d\lambda,

(1.6)

where this double integral is absolutely convergent.

The maps
\lambda\mapsto J_{(I,P,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)
are meromorphic and therefore the contour in the integral (
1.6
) may be shifted. In particular, one can replace
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
by any element in
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{I}}^{*}
of the form
((t,0,0,-s),(1/2-t,0,0,-1/2+s))
,
0<t,s<1/2
. Moving the contour further away might result in poles of Eisenstein series.

1.3
About Theorem
1.6

Although the statement of Theorem
1.6
seems analogous at first glance to fine spectral expansions for other relative trace formulae, in particular those of
[
38
]
and
[
19
]
, we emphasize that our case presents an additional difficulty. Namely, the strategy in
[
38
]
and
[
19
]
is to start from the spectral expansion of the kernel function
K_{f}
from
[
1
]
, which follows from the spectral expansion of
L^{2}([G])
of
[
36
]
, and then to make it commute with the considered automorphic period. This involves serious analytic obstacles which are overcomed by using truncation in the spirit of
[
30
]
. A key feature of these proofs is that the contours of integration never leave a neighborhood of the unitary axis
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. In our case, this approach proves to be impracticable as to make the different truncated integrals converge one has to shift parts of the spectral expansion of
K_{f}
off the unitary axis. This basically amounts to reversing the proof of
[
36
]
and, as one can expect, produces very intricate residual contributions. This specificity of the Rankin–Selberg period is reflected in the expansion (
1.6
) of Theorem
1.6
by the appearance of the
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
shifts. In contrast, the formulae in
[
38
]
and
[
19
]
only involve unitary terms.

Our strategy to prove Theorem
1.6
does not start from the decomposition of
L^{2}([G])
. Instead, we reproduce the argument of
[
36
]
in the case of our Rankin–Selberg period, which means that we proceed by shifting contours of from integrals along unramified characters with very positive real parts back to the unitary axis. However, we emphasize that we do use
[
36
]
later in the proof as a black box to simplify some computations of residual contributions. The miracle in our proof is that, after applying this trick and choosing the contours carefully, all the residues we gain along the way actually contribute to the expansion of Theorem
1.6
. This is in sharp contrast with
[
36
]
where intricate compensations occur (see e.g.
[
35
, Section 4]
).

We now present the main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.6
and the required technical inputs. We will also explicitly write our argument for the simple
\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}
example in Section
2
.

1.3.1
Step 1: coarse unfolding of the Rankin–Selberg integral

The first step does not involve shift of contours and is the subject of §
7.2
. For fixed
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
, set
F(g^{\prime})=K_{f}(g,g^{\prime})
for
g^{\prime}\in[G]
. Then
F
is a Schwartz function on
[G]
(see §
3.3.5
). For every integer
0\leq r\leq n
, let
P_{r}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with standard Levi factor
M_{r}:=(\mathrm{GL}_{r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n-r})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1-r})
. Let
F_{P_{r}}
be the constant term of
F
along
P_{r}
. Let
K_{H}
be the standard maximal compact subgroup of
H(\mathbb{A})
and write
R
for its action by right translations on
F
. By applying a classical Rankin–Selberg unfolding argument, we arrive at an expression of the form

\int_{[H]}F(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\int_{K_{H}}\left(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}\otimes Z_{n-r}(\cdot,0)\right)(R(k)F_{P_{r}})dk.

(1.7)

Here we mean that we regard
F_{P_{r}}
as a function on
[M_{r}]
to which we apply the inner-product obtained by integrating on the diagonal
[\mathrm{GL}_{r}]
and the Rankin–Selberg Zeta integral relative to
\mathrm{GL}_{n-r}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n-r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n-r+1}
, evaluated at zero. We refer to Proposition
7.8
for a precise statement.

Let
0\leq r\leq n
. We now compute the spectral expansion of the linear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}\otimes Z_{n-r}(\cdot,0)
which we view as defined on a certain space of functions on the Levi
[M_{r}]
. For
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{r}
, we can directly use the spectral expansion of
[
36
]
. For the Zeta integral, it is not too difficult to write that of
Z_{n-r}(\cdot,s)
for
\Re(s)
large enough. This boils down to the fact that this map is continuous for functions of fixed moderate growth (see Lemma
7.7
). Moreover,
Z_{n-r}
will kill all non-generic (hence here non-cuspidal) contributions. Thanks to the adjunction between constant terms and Eisenstein series and the description of the periods
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
by parabolic descent (Proposition
5.10
), we can induce our expansion back to
G
. The final result in the language of Theorem
1.6
, is that for any
\Re(s)
large enough

\int_{[H]}F(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-s\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}}E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)\overline{\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})}d\lambda,

(1.8)

where
I_{r}=(r,n-r,n+1-r,0)
and
\underline{z}_{r}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{r}}^{*}
is the element with coordinates
((0,1),(0,1))
. Note that this imposes that the representations
\pi_{1,i}
and
\pi_{2,j}
in (
1.4
) are cuspidal. The appearance of
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
boils down to a computation of modular characters.

1.3.2
Step 2: residues of Eisenstein series

To end the proof of Theorem
1.6
, we want to shift the contour of integration in (
1.8
) to the regions
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. This has to be done in two main steps, the first being to go to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-1/2\underline{z}_{r}
. This is done in §
7.3
.

To begin with, we need to ensure that our integrand is meromorphic and of rapid decay in vertical strips in our region of integration. This requires a majorization of the Eisenstein series
E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)
for
\Re(\lambda)
in a neighborhood of the positive Weyl chamber. This intermediate result is the content of Theorem
4.25
. It is an extension of
[
19
, Theorem 3.9.2.1]
which derived such a bound for
\lambda
in a neighborhood of the imaginary axis
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, following the strategy of
[
40
]
. The proof relies on deep results including bounds towards the Ramanujan conjecture from
[
41
]
and zero-free regions for automorphic
L
functions from
[
17
]
and
[
40
]
which allow us to control Eisenstein series slightly to the left of the imaginary axis.

We now have to understand the singularities of meromorphic functions

\lambda\mapsto E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)\overline{\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})},

When moving the contour to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-1/2\underline{z}_{r}
, we can show that all the poles we encounter come from the Eisenstein series
E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)
. We determine their possible singularities for
\Re(\lambda)
in a neighborhood of the positive Weyl chamber in Theorem
4.17
. We emphasize that the situation for discrete Eisenstein series is far more complicated than for those induced from cuspidal automorphic forms as intricate compensations of poles can occur in their constant terms. We refer to
[
28
]
where this phenomenon was studied in details for unramified forms on split reductive groups. For
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, it turns out that Theorem
4.17
was already contained (up to some mild reformulation) in
[
45
]
. The answer is that, in the language of
[
9
]
, singularities arise when segments of
\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
and
\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma^{\prime},d^{\prime})
are linked (see §
4.4.1
). This useful fact seems to be ignored by later references dealing with the subject (e.g.
[
27
]
or
[
26
]
).

In any case, we now know which singularities we cross during our shift of contours. The next step is to describe the representations spanned by the corresponding residues of
E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)
. In general, this is a hard question (see e.g.
[
27
]
and
[
26
]
), but for the singularities we consider it is straightforward. More precisely, they arise from links between segments associated to
\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d-1)
and
\sigma
, for some cuspidal representation
\sigma
. The resulting residues is a twist of
\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
by
[
45
]
(see Lemma
7.16
).

The outcome of the second step is an expansion of the form

\int_{[H]}F(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H,r}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-1/2\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}}E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda)\varphi,\lambda)\overline{\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})}d\lambda,

(1.9)

where
\Pi_{H,r}
is a certain subset of
\Pi_{H}
. We refer to Proposition
7.11
for a precise statement.

1.3.3
Step 3: residues of regularized periods

We finally move the contour of integration in (
1.9
) to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. This is the content of §
7.4
. The singularities that we now cross are those of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})}
. By Theorem
1.4
, these regularized periods are built by taking residues of Rankin–Selberg Zeta integrals
Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
of Eisenstein series induced from cuspidal automorphic representations. Using this description, we see that residues of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
are also iterated residues of
Z(E(\phi,\lambda))
, and therefore can be written as some
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\prime}}
for another triple
(I^{\prime},P^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
. Therefore, the bookeeping needed to keep track of the residual contributions in this third step is less involved than in the second one. These additional terms are always twisted by a non-zero
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. Once this last wave of shifts of contours is finished, Theorem
1.6
is proved.

1.4
Outline of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents our argument in the simple example
G=\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}
. In Section
3
, we fix some notations and prove some preliminary results pertaining to automorphic forms and spaces of functions on automorphic quotients. In Section
4
, we recall the main results on the classification of discrete automorphic forms on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
from
[
45
]
. In particular, we study the poles of global intertwining operators
M(w,\lambda)
and discrete Eisenstein series
E(\varphi,\lambda)
for
\Re(\lambda)
in a neighborhood of the positive Weyl chamber (Proposition
4.11
and Theorem
4.17
). We finally extend the results of
[
19
]
to bound Eisenstein series in this region (Theorem
4.25
). We then proceed in Section
5
to recall the framework of
[
16
]
on regularized Rankin–Selberg periods, following
[
29
]
and
[
55
]
. In particular, we describe them using parabolic descent in Proposition
5.10
, and use this to find their singularities in Proposition
5.11
and bound them in Proposition
5.12
. We finally compute their residues in Proposition
5.13
. In Section
6
, we extend the construction of the regularized linear forms
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
of
[
16
]
to the general case of triples
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. We prove Theorem
1.4
in §
6.1.6
. We also present in §
6.2
a different set of
increasing
inducing data
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
which appears naturally when computing the spectral expansion. Finally, in Section
7
we prove Theorem
1.6
. The argument is divided in the three steps of §
7.2
, §
7.3
and §
7.4
presented above. In §
7.5
we group together all the contributions to end the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

and financial support.

2
The
\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-case

We now explain our shifts of contours of integration for the simple example
G=\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and
H=\mathrm{GL}_{1}
. In that case, the result is certainly not new (see e.g.
[
33
]
for a closely related computation). We refer to the main text for some technical analytic bounds that we use. We also ignore the question of normalizing the measures, for which we refer to §
7.1.1
.

We will use the notation from Section
1
. We take
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
,
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
and set
F(g^{\prime})=K_{f}(g,g^{\prime})
for
g^{\prime}\in[G]
. The unfolding expansion for the Rankin–Selberg integral from (
1.7
) reads

\int_{[H]}F(h)dh=Z_{2}(F,0)+\int_{[\mathrm{GL}_{1}]}F_{B}(h)dh,

(2.1)

where
B
is the Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices in
G
. Here
Z_{2}(F,0)
is the Zeta integral from
[
31
]
. It is the integral of a Whittaker coefficient of
F
along
H(\mathbb{A})
(see (
7.10
)). Let
B_{2}
be the Borel subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, so that
B=\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times B_{2}
. We now write the expansion of each term separately.

2.1
The main contribution

We start with
Z_{2}(F,0)
. For
s\in\mathbb{C}
, note that
F_{-s}:=F(h)\left|h\right|^{-s}
is still of rapid decay, and that
Z_{2}(F,0)=Z_{2}(F_{-s},s)
. We now apply the spectral expansion of
[
36
]
to
F_{-s}
. It reads

\displaystyle F_{-s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi_{1}}\sum_{\pi}\sum_{\varphi\in\chi_{1}\boxtimes\pi}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}}\langle F,\varphi_{\lambda-\overline{s}}\rangle_{G}\varphi_{\lambda}d\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\chi_{1}}\sum_{\chi_{2}}\sum_{\varphi\in\chi_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{A}_{B_{2},\chi_{2}}(\mathrm{GL}_{2})}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}}\langle F,E(\varphi,\lambda-\overline{s})\rangle_{G}E(\varphi,\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle+\sum_{\chi_{1},\chi_{1}^{\prime}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}}\langle F,(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{1}^{\prime})_{\lambda-\overline{s}}\rangle_{G}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{1}^{\prime})_{\lambda}d\lambda.

The notations we use are as follows.
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{1}^{\prime}
range along automorphic characters of
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
(and we write again
\chi_{1}^{\prime}
for
\chi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\det
), and
\chi_{2}
along automorphic characters of
(\mathrm{GL}_{1})^{2}
. These characters are trivial on
A_{\mathrm{GL}_{1}}^{\infty}
and
A_{(\mathrm{GL}_{1})^{2}}^{\infty}
respectively.
\pi
ranges along cuspidal representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
(with trivial central character on
A_{\mathrm{GL}_{2}}^{\infty}
).
\varphi
ranges along orthonormal bases for the Petersson inner products.
s
is the element in
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
corresponding to the character
(g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto\left|g_{1}\right|^{s/2}\left|\det g_{2}\right|^{s/2}
. Finally
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{G}
is the inner product given by integrating along
[G]
. By Corollary
4.31
, this expression is absolutely convergent in some space of function with large fixed growth
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
independent of
s
, and by
[
11
, Lemma 7.1.1.1]

Z_{2}(\cdot,s)
defines a continuous linear form on this space for
\Re(s)
large enough. As characters of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
are not generic, we arrive at

Z_{2}(F,0)=\sum_{\chi_{1},\pi,\varphi}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}+s}\langle F,\varphi_{-\overline{\lambda}}\rangle_{G}Z_{2}(\varphi_{\lambda})d\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\varphi}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}+s}\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda))d\lambda,

(2.2)

where we write
Z_{2}(\varphi_{\lambda})
and
Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda))
for
Z_{2}(\varphi_{\lambda},0)
and
Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda),0)
, both Zeta integral being absolutely convergent for our
\lambda
. This ends the manipulations of step 1 from §
1.3.1
for this contribution. We now shift the contour in each integral.

2.1.1
Cuspidal contributions

We start with the first term in (
2.2
). We fix
\chi_{1},\pi
and
\varphi
. There is no Eisenstein series here, so that we may skip step 2 an go directly to step 3 from §
1.3.3
. The only poles come from
Z_{2}(\varphi_{\lambda})
. If we write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we know that for factorizable
\varphi
(which we can arrange all our elements in the orthonormal bases to be), we have a finite set of places
\mathtt{S}
of
F
such that

Z_{2}(\varphi_{\lambda})=L(1/2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2},\chi_{1}\times\pi)\times\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}Z^{\natural}_{v}(\varphi_{v,\lambda}).

The global
L
-functions and normalized local Zeta integrals are regular by
[
31
]
as we only look at the central direction. In that case, we can shift the contour to
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
with no issue. In the language of §
1.2.2
, this corresponds to the case
(I,G,\chi\boxtimes\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
with
I=(0,1,2,0)
from Example
1.1
.

2.1.2
Continuous contributions

We now deal with the second contribution in (
2.2
), and fix
\chi_{1}
,
\chi_{2}
and
\varphi
. We further write
\chi_{2}=\chi_{2}^{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}^{2}
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},(\lambda_{2}^{1},\lambda_{2}^{2}))
. The factorization is

Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda))=\frac{L(1/2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1},\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}^{1})L(1/2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{2},\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}^{2})}{L(1+\lambda_{2}^{1}-\lambda_{2}^{2},\chi_{2}^{1}\times\chi_{2}^{2,\vee})}\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}\times Z^{\natural}_{v}(\varphi_{v,\lambda}).

(2.3)

If
\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}^{1,\vee}
and
\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}^{2,\vee}
, we shift the contour in the central direction of
s
. The Eisenstein series and the Zeta integral remain regular and neither step 2 nor 3 are needed. We obtain an integral on
i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}
. This corresponds to the contribution
(I,B,\chi\boxtimes(\chi_{1}^{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}^{2}))\in\Pi_{H}
with
I=(0,1,2,0)
from Example
1.1
.

We now assume that
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}^{1,\vee}
but
\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}^{2,\vee}
. As before, the Eisenstein series remains regular. However, we now have a simple pole at
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2
coming from (
2.3
). Set
I=(0,0,1,1)
and let
\chi
be the character
\chi_{1}\boxtimes(\chi_{2}^{2}\boxtimes\chi_{2}^{1})
of
\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times(\mathrm{GL}_{1})^{2}
. Then
(I,P,\chi)\in\Pi_{H}
. Let
w
be the non-trivial element in the Weyl group of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
. Then if we apply
w
, the hyperplane
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2
becomes
\mathfrak{a}_{\chi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}
. For
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{B,\chi}(G)
, by Proposition
5.10
(see also Example
1.5
) we have the simple description

\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(\phi,\mu)=\int_{K_{H}}\phi_{\mu}(wk)dk=\phi(w),\quad\mu\in\mathfrak{a}_{\chi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}.

(2.4)

Note that for
\mu
in this subspace, this indeeds define a
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant linear form on
\mathcal{A}_{B,\chi,\mu}(G)
. We claim that we have the equality

\underset{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2}{\mathrm{Res}}\;Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda))=\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi,w\lambda).

(2.5)

This result is proved in Proposition
5.13
.

Remark 2.1
.

If we specify (
2.5
) to the special case where all the characters and
\varphi
are assumed to be unramified and if
F=\mathbb{Q}
(so that there is no local factor in (
2.3
) by
[
18
]
and
[
49
]
), we are simply saying that the residue of the quotient of
L
-functions in (
2.3
) is equal, up to a volume term, to the global factor of
M(w,\lambda)
.

By the functional equation of Eisenstein series and a change of variable, we arrive at

\sum_{\varphi}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}+s}\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}Z_{2}(E(\varphi,\lambda))d\lambda=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{B,\chi}(G)}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\chi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}+e}\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(\varphi,\lambda)d\lambda,

(2.6)

where
\mathfrak{a}_{\chi}^{*}
is the subspace
\{(\lambda_{1},(\lambda_{2}^{1},-\lambda_{1}))\}
,
\underline{\rho}_{\chi}=(-1/4,(0,-1/4))
and
e=(0,(1/4,0))
. We now want to shift in the
\lambda_{2}^{1}
variable to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\chi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}
(i.e. to
\Re(\lambda_{2}^{1})=0
). By the descrition of (
2.4
),
\mathcal{P}_{\chi}
is clearly regular, and our contour will keep
-\overline{\lambda}
in the positive Weyl chamber without crossing the pole of the Eisenstein series (which would occur for
\Re(\lambda_{2}^{1})=3/4
). Therefore, we can indeed do this shift of contour. We therefore get two contributions attached to
(I,B,\chi)
with
I=(0,1,2,0)
and
(0,0,1,1)
. The case
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}^{2,\vee}
but
\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}^{1,\vee}
is the same using the functional equation.

We now move to the most difficult case where
\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}^{1,\vee}=\chi_{2}^{2,\vee}
. We a priori have a pole of order two in the numerator of (
2.4
) when the hyperplanes
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2
and
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=1/2
cross. If we assume a weak version of the generalized Riemann hypothesis, we get that
E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})
is regular and that
L(1+\lambda_{2}^{1}-\lambda_{2}^{2},\chi_{2}^{1}\times\chi_{2}^{2,\vee})
has no zero for
\Re(\lambda_{2}^{1})-\Re(\lambda_{2}^{2})\geq-\varepsilon
for a small
\varepsilon
. We could take advantage of this as follows. First, we move the contour in (
2.6
) to the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}+(1/4,(1/4+\varepsilon,1/4+\varepsilon))
. We then shift the contour in the
\lambda_{2}^{1}
variable to the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}+(1/4,(1/4-\varepsilon,1/4+\varepsilon))
, thus getting the residue along
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2
. But we are now exactly in the situation of (
2.6
) and can proceed from there, the key point is that the hyperplane
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{2}=1/2
is no longer singular for our residue. Finally, we shift the main contribution in the
\lambda_{2}^{2}
variable to go to
i\mathfrak{a}_{B}^{*}+(1/4,(1/4-\varepsilon,1/4-\varepsilon))
catching the additional residue along
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{1}=1/2
. We may now conclude as in the
\chi_{1}\neq\chi_{2}^{2,\vee}
.

If we don’t assume some variant of the generalized Riemann hypothesis, we can still use the zero free regions from
[
17
]
. Because we know that the integrand in (
2.6
) is of rapid decay by Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
6.7
, we may cut its tail to focus on the region
\left|\Im(\lambda_{2}^{1})\right|,\left|\Im(\lambda_{2}^{2})\right|\leq T
for
T
large. We can now assume that the product of rectangles in the variables
\lambda_{2}^{1}
and
\lambda_{2}^{2}
centered in
(1/4,(1/4,1/4))
with real length
2\varepsilon
and imaginary height
T
is contained in a zero free region, and therefore do the same manipulations as before. This is the method we use in the core of the text (see Lemma
7.18
and Lemma
7.19
). It is inspired by
[
38
]
.

2.1.3
Final result

Putting everything together, we arrive at

Z_{2}(F,0)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}\\
I=(0,1,2,0)\\
\text{or }I=(0,1,0,1)\end{subarray}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}J_{(I,P,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)d\lambda,

(2.7)

where
\left|W(\pi)\right|
is
2
if
I=(0,1,2,0)
, and
1
otherwise.

2.2
The constant term contribution

We now deal with the second term in (
2.1
) which is the integral of
F_{B}
along
[\mathrm{GL}_{1}]
. Let
T=\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times(\mathrm{GL}_{1})^{2}
be the maximal torus of diagonal matrices. The function
F_{B}
is not of rapid decay on
[T]
, but if we fix any
N>0
then there exists
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(s)
large enough so that

(t_{1},(t_{2}^{1},t_{2}^{2}))\in[T]\mapsto F_{B}(t_{1},(t_{2}^{1},t_{2}^{2}))\left|\det g_{2}^{2}\right|^{s}

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{-N}([T])
, i.e. decreases at least as fast as
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert^{-N}_{T}
(see Lemma
7.6
). Using the same trick as before, we can write the spectral expansion of
F_{B}
as a function on
[T]
using
[
36
]
, and further upgrade it to a spectral expansion depending on
F
by the adjunction between constant terms and Eisenstein series. By repackaging things as in (
2.2
) and taking into account the modular characters, we arrive at

\int_{[\mathrm{GL}_{1}]}F_{B}(h)dh=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I,P,\chi)\in\Pi_{H}\\
I=(1,0,1,0)\end{subarray}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\chi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}+se_{2}^{1}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\chi}(G)}\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(\varphi,\lambda)d\lambda,

(2.8)

where
\underline{\rho}_{\chi}=(1/4,(1/4,0))
,
e_{2}^{1}=(0,(0,1))
, the condition
I=(1,0,1,0)
implies that
\chi
is a character of
[T]
of the form
\chi_{1}\boxtimes(\chi_{1}^{\vee}\boxtimes\chi_{2})
,
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}=(\lambda_{1},(-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))
, and finally

\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\int_{K_{H}}\varphi_{\lambda}(k)dk=\varphi(1),\quad\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\chi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\chi}.

(2.9)

We now shift in the variable
\lambda_{2}^{1}
to bring the region of integration from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+se_{2}^{1}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. Given (
2.9
), the only possible singularity comes from
E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})
and occurs at
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
if
\chi_{2}=\chi_{1}^{\vee}
. If we write
\eta=\chi_{1}\boxtimes(\chi_{1}^{\vee}\circ\det)
, then the residue spans the character
\eta_{-\overline{\lambda}}
(where we project
-\overline{\lambda}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
). By Lemma
7.16
, we have the adjunction for
\lambda
in the singular hyperplane

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\chi}(G)}\langle F,E^{*}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\mathcal{P}_{\chi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\langle F,\eta_{-\overline{\lambda}}\rangle_{G}\mathcal{P}_{\eta}(1,\lambda),

(2.10)

where
E^{*}
is the residue of the Eisenstein series and
\mathcal{P}_{\eta}(1,\lambda)
is simply constant equal to
1
. In particular, this expression is now holomorphic. This case corresponds to
((0,0,2,0),G,\eta)\in\Pi_{H}
from Example
1.2
, so that
\mathfrak{a}_{\eta}=(\lambda_{1},(-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{1}))
and
\underline{\rho}_{\eta}=0
. We can shift the contour in the integral of the residue to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\eta}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\eta}
.

If we add up the main contribution of (
2.8
) with (
2.10
), we obtain the formula

\int_{[\mathrm{GL}_{1}]}F_{B}(h)dh=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I,B,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}\\
I=(1,0,1,0)\\
\text{or }I=(0,0,0,2)\end{subarray}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}J_{(I,B,\pi)}^{H}(g,f,\lambda)d\lambda,

(2.11)

where
\left|W(\pi)\right|
is always
1
here. Theorem
1.6
now follows from putting (
2.7
) and (
2.11
) together.

3
Preliminaries on automorphic forms

3.1
General notation

Let
F
be a field of characteristic zero. All algebraic groups are defined over
F
.

3.1.1
Reductive groups, parabolic subgroups, characters

Let
G
be a connected reductive group. Let
Z_{G}
be the center of
G
. Let
N_{G}
be the unipotent radical of
G
and let
X^{*}(G)
be the group of
F
-algebraic characters of
G
. Set
\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{G}=X^{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{G}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(G),\mathbb{R})
. Let

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\times\mathfrak{a}_{G}\to\mathbb{R}

(3.1)

be the canonical pairing.

Let
P_{0}
be a minimal parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
M_{0}
be a Levi factor of
P_{0}
. We say that a parabolic subgroup of
G
is standard (resp. semi-standard) if it contains
P_{0}
(resp. if it contains
M_{0}
). If
P
is a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, we will denote by
N_{P}
its unipotent radical and by
M_{P}
its unique Levi factor containing
M_{0}
, which is said to be semi-standard. We have a decomposition
P=M_{P}N_{P}
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}(M_{P})
the set of semi-standard parabolic subgroups of
G
with semi-standard Levi
M_{P}
.

Let
A_{G}
be the maximal central
F
-split torus of
G
. If
P
is a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, set
A_{P}=A_{M_{P}}
. We set
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P_{0}}^{*}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\mathfrak{a}_{P_{0}}
and
A_{0}=A_{P_{0}}
.

Let
P\subset Q
be semi-standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. The restriction maps
X^{*}(Q)\to X^{*}(P)
and
X^{*}(A_{P})\to X^{*}(A_{Q})
induce dual decompositions
\mathfrak{a}_{P}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{Q}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}
. In particular, we have projections
\mathfrak{a}_{0}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
denoted by
X\mapsto X_{P}^{Q}
which only depend on the Levi factors
M_{P}
and
M_{Q}
. If
Q=G
, we omit the exponent
G
in the previous notation.

Set
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{Q}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{Q,*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. We still denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the pairing obtained by extension of scalars. We have decompositions
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{Q}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q},\quad\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{Q,*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
, where
i^{2}=-1
. We denote by
\Re
and
\Im
the real and imaginary parts associated to these decompositions, and by
\overline{\lambda}
the complex conjugate of any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{Q,*}
.

3.1.2
Roots, coroots, weights

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
\Delta_{0}^{P}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{P,*}
(resp.
\Sigma_{0}^{P}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{P,*}
) be the set of simple roots (resp. of roots) of
A_{0}
in
M_{P}\cap P_{0}
. If
P=G
, we write
\Delta_{0}
and
\Sigma_{0}
. Let
\Delta_{P}
(resp.
\Sigma_{P}
) be the image of
\Delta_{0}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}
(resp.
\Sigma_{0}\setminus\Sigma_{0}^{P}
) by the projection
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. More generally, for
P\subset Q
let
\Delta_{P}^{Q}
(resp.
\Sigma_{P}^{Q}
) be the projection of
\Delta_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Delta_{0}^{P}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
(resp.
\Sigma_{0}^{Q}\setminus\Sigma_{0}^{P}
). Let
\Delta_{P}^{Q,\vee}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
be the set of simple coroots. If
\alpha\in\Delta_{P}^{Q}
, we denote by
\alpha^{\vee}
the associated coroot. By duality, let
\hat{\Delta}_{P}^{Q}
be the set of simple weights. Set

\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*,+}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\;|\;\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>0,\;\forall\alpha\in\Delta_{P}^{Q}\right\}.

If
Q=G
, we drop the exponent. We denote by
\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*,+}}
the closure of these open subsets in
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
respectively. If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
, we write
\lambda>0
if
\lambda
is a nonnegative linear combination of the simple roots
\Delta_{P}
.

We say that a functional
\Lambda
on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is an affine linear form if it is of the form
\Lambda(\lambda)=\langle\lambda,\gamma^{\vee}\rangle-a
for
\gamma^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}
and
a\in\mathbb{C}
. We call its set of zeros an affine hyperplane. If
\gamma^{\vee}
is a coroot, then it is an affine root hyperplane. By "
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position", we mean that
\lambda
lies outside of a countable union of affine hyperplanes.

3.1.3
Weyl group

Let
W
be the Weyl group of
(G,A_{0})
, which is by definition the quotient of the normalizer
N_{G(F)}(A_{0}(F))
by the centralizer
Z_{G(F)}(A_{0}(F))
. It acts on
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
and by duality on
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{*}
. If
w\in W
, we write again
w
for a representative in
G(F)
.

Let
P=M_{P}N_{P}
and
Q=M_{Q}N_{Q}
be two standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. Let
{}_{Q}W_{P}
be the set of
w\in W
such that
M_{P}\cap w^{-1}P_{0}w=M_{P}\cap P_{0}
and
M_{Q}\cap wP_{0}w^{-1}=M_{Q}\cap P_{0}
.

Let
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
. Set
P_{w}=(M_{P}\cap w^{-1}Qw)N_{P}
. By
[
48
, Lemme V.4.6.]
,
P_{w}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
included in
P
, with standard Levi factor
M_{P}\cap w^{-1}M_{Q}w
. In the same way,
Q_{w}=(M_{Q}\cap wPw^{-1})N_{Q}
is standard parabolic subgroup of
G
included in
Q
, with standard Levi factor
M_{Q}\cap wM_{P}w^{-1}
. Note that
w\Sigma_{P_{w}}^{P}\subset\Sigma_{Q_{w}}
and
w^{-1}\Sigma_{Q_{w}}^{Q}\subset\Sigma_{P_{w}}
. Set

\displaystyle W(P;Q)

\displaystyle=\{w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}\;|\;P_{w}=P\}=\{w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}\;|\;M_{P}\subset w^{-1}M_{Q}w\},

\displaystyle W(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\{w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}\;|\;M_{P}=w^{-1}M_{Q}w\}.

Note that
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
implies
w\in W(P_{w},Q_{w})
. Set

W(P)=\bigcup_{Q}W(P,Q).

Write
w_{P}
for the longest element in
W(P)
.

If
R
is another standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, we write
{}_{Q}W^{R}_{P}
(resp.
W^{R}(P;Q)
and
W^{R}(P,Q)
) for
{}_{Q\cap M_{R}}W_{P\cap M_{R}}
(resp.
W(P\cap M_{R};Q\cap M_{R})
and
W(P\cap M_{R},Q\cap M_{R})
) relatively to the reductive group
M_{R}
.

3.2
Automorphic quotients and Haar measures

We now assume that
F
is a number field. Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
.

3.2.1
Automorphic quotients

Let
\mathbb{A}
be the adele ring of
F
, let
\mathbb{A}_{f}
be its ring of finite adeles. Set
F_{\infty}=F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
. Let
V_{F}
be the set of places of
F
and let
V_{F,\infty}\subset V_{F}
be the subset of Archimedean places. For
v\in V_{F}
, let
F_{v}
be the completion of
F
at
v
. If
v
is non-Archimedean, let
q_{v}
be the cardinality of the residual field of
F_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v}
be its ring of integers. Let
\left|\cdot\right|
be the absolute value
\mathbb{A}^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
given by taking the product of the normalized absolute values
\left|\cdot\right|_{v}
on each
F_{v}
.

Let
P=M_{P}N_{P}
be a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Set

[G]_{P}=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}).

Let
A_{P,\mathbb{Q}}
be the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split subtorus of the Weil restriction
\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}A_{P}
, and let
A_{P}^{\infty}
be the neutral component of
A_{P,\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R})
. Set

[G]_{P,0}=A_{P}^{\infty}M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}).

If
P=G
, we simply write
[G]
and
[G]_{0}
for
[G]_{G}
and
[G]_{G,0}
respectively.

Let
P
be a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. There is a canonical morphism
H_{P}:P(\mathbb{A})\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
such that
\langle\chi,H_{P}(g)\rangle=\log\left|\chi(g)\right|
for any
g\in P(\mathbb{A})
and
\chi\in X^{*}(P)
. The kernel of
H_{P}
is denoted by
P(\mathbb{A})^{1}
. We extend it to
H_{P}:G(\mathbb{A})\to\mathfrak{a}_{P}
which satisfies: for any
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
we have
H_{P}(g)=H_{P}(p)
whenever
g\in pK
with
p\in P(\mathbb{A})
. If
P=P_{0}
, we write
H_{0}=H_{P_{0}}
.

We set

[G]_{P}^{1}=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})\backslash P(\mathbb{A})^{1}K.

If
P=G
, we simply write
[G]^{1}
.

Let
K=\prod_{v\in V_{F}}K_{v}\subset G(\mathbb{A})
be a "good" maximal compact subgroup in good position relative to
M_{0}
. We write
K=K_{\infty}K^{\infty}
where
K_{\infty}=\prod_{v\in V_{F,\infty}}K_{v}
and
K^{\infty}=\prod_{v\in V_{F}\setminus V_{F,\infty}}K_{v}
. By a
level

J
of
G
, we mean an open-compact subgroup
J
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

3.2.2
Modular characters

If
P\subset Q
are semi-standard parabolic subgroups of
G
, let
\rho_{P}^{Q}
be the unique element in
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q,*}
such that for every
m\in M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
we have

\left|\det(\mathrm{Ad}_{P}^{Q}(m))\right|=\exp(\langle 2\rho_{P}^{Q},H_{P}(m)\rangle),

where
\mathrm{Ad}_{P}^{Q}
is the adjoint action of
M_{P}
on the Lie algebra of
M_{Q}\cap N_{P}
. For every
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
, we then set

\delta_{P}^{Q}(g):=\exp(\langle 2\rho_{P}^{Q},H_{P}(g)\rangle).

In particular, when restricted to
P(\mathbb{A})\cap M_{Q}(\mathbb{A})
it coincides with the restriction of the modular character of the latter. If
Q=G
, we omit the superscript.

3.2.3
Haar measures

We take a Haar measure
dg
on
G(\mathbb{A})
, with factorization
dg=\prod_{v}dg_{v}
where for all place
v
,
dg_{v}
is a Haar measure on
G(F_{v})
. This implicitly implies that for almost all place
v
the volume of
K_{v}
is
1
.

Let
P
be a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. We equip
\mathfrak{a}_{P}
with the Haar measure that gives covolume
1
to the lattice
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{*}(P),\mathbb{Z})
. We equip
A_{P}^{\infty}
with the Haar measure compatible with the isomorphism
A_{P}^{\infty}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{P}
induced by
H_{P}
. If
P\subset Q
, we equip
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{Q}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}/\mathfrak{a}_{Q}
with the quotient measure.

For each
v\in V_{F}
, we give
K_{v}
the invariant probability measure. This yields a product measure on
K
. If
N
is an unipotent group, we give
N(\mathbb{A})
the Haar measure whose quotient by the counting measure on
N(F)
gives
[N]
volume
1
. We equip
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
with the unique Haar measure such that

\int_{G(\mathbb{A})}f(g)dg=\int_{N_{P}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{M_{P}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{K}f(nmk)\exp(-\langle 2\rho_{P},H_{P}(m)\rangle)dkdmdn

(3.2)

for every continuous and compactly supported function
f
on
G(\mathbb{A})
. We equip
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})^{1}
with the Haar measure compatible with the isomorphism
M_{P}(\mathbb{A})^{1}\times A_{P}^{\infty}\to M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
.

We give
[G]_{P}
the quotient of our measure on
G(\mathbb{A})
by the product of the counting measure on
M_{P}(F)
with our measure on
N_{P}(\mathbb{A})
. Moreover, note that the action of
a\in A_{P}^{\infty}
by left translation on
[G]_{P}
multiplies the measure by
\delta_{P}^{-1}(a)
. By taking the quotient of the measure on
[G]_{P}
by that of
A_{P}^{\infty}
, we obtain a "semi-invariant" measure on
[G]_{P,0}
.

3.3
Functions on automorphic quotients

We keep the assumption that
F
is a number field and that
G
is connected reductive over
F
.

Let
X
be a set, let
f
and
g
be two positive functions on
X
. We write

f(x)\ll g(x),\quad x\in X,

if there exists
C>0
such that
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
for all
x\in X
.

3.3.1
Smooth functions

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}
be the Lie algebra of
G(F_{\infty})
, let
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
be the enveloping algebra of its complexification and let
\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
be the center of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
. If we only care about
G(F_{v})
for a single Archimedean place
v
of
F
, we will write
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{v,\infty})
instead.

By a level
J
we mean a normal open compact subgroup of
K^{\infty}
. If
V
is a representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
, we denote by
V^{J}
its subspace of vectors fixed by
J
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space. We say that a function
\varphi:G(\mathbb{A})\to V
is smooth if it is right-invariant by some level
J
and if for every
g_{f}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, the function
g_{\infty}\in G(F_{\infty})\mapsto\varphi(g_{f}g_{\infty})
is smooth in the usual sense (i.e. belongs to
C^{\infty}(G(F_{\infty}))
). We write
R
(resp.
L
) for the actions by right-translation (resp. left-translation) of
G(\mathbb{A})
and
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
on such smooth functions.

3.3.2
Heights

We take an embedding
\iota:G\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n}
for some integer
n>0
. We define a height
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
on
G(\mathbb{A})
by

\left\lVert g\right\rVert=\prod_{v}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq n}(\left|\iota(g)_{i,j}\right|_{v},\left|\iota(g^{-1})_{i,j}\right|_{v}),\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

If we choose another embedding
\iota^{\prime}
yielding
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert^{\prime}
, then there exists
r>0
such that
\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{1/{r}}\ll\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{\prime}\ll\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{r}
for
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
. By
[
11
, Equation (2.4.1.1)]
, we have the formula

\left\lVert gh\right\rVert\ll\left\lVert g\right\rVert\left\lVert h\right\rVert,\quad g,h\in G(\mathbb{A}).

(3.3)

If
P
is a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, for any
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
we define

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{P}=\inf_{\delta\in M_{P}(F)N_{P}(\mathbb{A})}\left\lVert\delta g\right\rVert.

The heights satisfy the following properties.

Lemma 3.1
.

The following assertions hold.

•

There exists
N>0
such that
g\mapsto\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{G}^{-N}
is absolutely integrable on
[G]
.

•

For every
M>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{U}\subset[G]
such that
g\notin\mathcal{U}
implies
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{G}\geq M
.

Proof.

The first assertion is
[
14
, Proposition A.1.1.(vi)]
. For the second, by
[
11
, Section 2.4.3]
we are reduced to proving the fact on a Siegel domain of
G
(see
[
11
, Section 2.2.13]
), and hence for
g\in A_{0}^{\infty}
. But there we can express
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{G}
using characters by
[
11
, Section 2.4.3]
, and hence easily conclude.
∎

3.3.3
Schwartz functions

For every compact subset
C
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and every level
J
, let
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J)
be the space of smooth functions
f:G(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{C}
such that

•

f
is biinvariant by
J
and is supported on
G(F_{\infty})\times C
;

•

for every integer
r\geq 1
and
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
we have

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{r,X,Y}:=\sup_{g\in G(\mathbb{A})}\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{r}\left|(R(X)L(Y)f)(g)\right|<\infty.

We equip
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J)
with the family of semi-norms
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{r,X,Y}
, and let
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
be the locally convex topological direct limit of the spaces
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}),C,J)
over the pairs
(C,J)
. It is the space of Schwartz functions on
G(\mathbb{A})
, and it is an algebra for the convolution product
*
. For any level
J
, we denote by
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
its subalgebra of
J
-biinvariant functions.

3.3.4
Petersson innner–product

We fix a semi-standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
for the reminder of this section. We have the Hilbert space
L^{2}([G]_{P})
of square-integrable functions on
[G]_{P}
. We will also consider
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})
the space of functions on
[G]_{P}
that transform by
\delta_{P}^{1/2}
under left-translation by
A_{P}^{\infty}
and such that the Petersson-norm

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}^{2}=\langle\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}:=\int_{[G]_{P,0}}\left|\varphi(g)\right|^{2}dg,

is finite. If
J
is a level
G
, we write
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{\infty,J}
for the space of
J
-invariant functions
\varphi
in
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})
such that the orbit map
g\mapsto g.\varphi
is smooth. The space
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{\infty,J}
is given the topology induced by the family of semi-norms
\left\lVert X.\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
for
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
. Then
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{\infty}=\bigcup_{J}L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{\infty,J}
is given the locally convex direct limit topology.

3.3.5
Spaces of functions

For all
N\in\mathbb{R}
,
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and any smooth function
\varphi:[G]_{P}\to\mathbb{C}
we define

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{N,X}=\sup_{x\in[G]_{P}}\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{P}^{N}\left|(R(X)\varphi)(x)\right|.

If
X=1
, we simply write
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{N}
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})
be the space of smooth functions
\varphi:[G]_{P}\to\mathbb{C}
such that for every
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
we have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X}<\infty
. For every level
J
, we equip
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})^{J}
with the topology of Fréchet space induced by the family of semi-norms
(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{-N,X})_{X}
. Set

\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})=\bigcup_{N>0}\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P}).

This is the space of
functions of uniform moderate growth on
[G]_{P}
. It is equipped with a natural topology of locally Fréchet space.

Let
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
be the space of smooth functions
\varphi:[G]_{P}\to\mathbb{C}
such that for every
N\geq 0
and
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
we have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{N,X}<\infty
. For every level
J
, we equip
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})^{J}
with the Fréchet topology induced by the family of semi-norms
(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{N,X})_{N,X}
. The space
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
is the
Schwartz space
of
[G]_{P}
.

By
[
11
, Section 2.5.10]
,
\mathcal{S}([G]_{P})
is dense in
\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})
. It is in general not dense in
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})
, but we have the following weaker result.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the closure of
\mathcal{S}([G])
in
\mathcal{T}_{N+1}([G])
contains
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
.

Proof.

By the Dixmier–Malliavin theorem of
[
20
]
, it is enough to show that the statement holds for the topology induced by the sole norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{-N-1}
. If
[G]
is compact, this is automatic. In general, we have the following fact: for every
M>0
there exists a compact set
\mathcal{U}\subset[G]
such that
g\notin\mathcal{U}
implies
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{G}\geq M
. By Lemma
3.1
it is enough to approximate elements in
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
by functions in
\mathcal{S}([G])
on compact sets. But it follows from an easy adaptation of
[
42
, Theorem 8.4]
that this can be done using Poincaré series of Schwartz functions in
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
.
∎

We will also make use of some non-smooth variants of the above spaces. For every
N\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathcal{T}_{N}^{0}([G]_{P})
be the space of complex Radon measures
\varphi
on
[G]_{P}
such that

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{1,N}:=\int_{[G]_{P}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{-N}\left|\varphi(g)\right|<\infty.

We equip
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}([G]_{P})
with the topology associated to the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,N}
so that it is Banach, and let
\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P})
be the locally convex direct limit of the spaces
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{N}([G]_{P})
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{00}([G]_{P})
be the space of continuous measurable complex-valued functions on
[G]_{P}
such that for every
N>0
we have
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{N}<\infty
. We have a pairing

\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle_{P}=\int_{[G]_{P}}\varphi(g)\overline{\psi}(g),\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{00}([G]_{P}),\quad\psi\in\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P}).

(3.4)

It identifies the topological dual of
\mathcal{S}^{00}([G]_{P})
with
\mathcal{T}^{0}([G]_{P})
(see
[
11
, Section 2.5.9]
).

3.4
Automorphic representations

We keep the assumption that
F
is a number field and that
G
is reductive over
F
.

3.4.1
Automorphic forms

Let
P
be a semi-standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. We define the space of automorphic forms
\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
to be the subspace of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
-finite functions in
\mathcal{T}([G]_{P})
.

For any ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
of finite codimension, we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathcal{J}}(G)
the subspace of
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
such that
R(z)\varphi=0
for every
z\in\mathcal{J}
. By
[
11
, Section 2.7.1]
, there exists
N\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathcal{J}}(G)\subset\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})
. We give
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathcal{J}}(G)
the induced topology. It is independent from
N
by
[
11
, Lemma 2.5.4.1]
and by the open mapping theorem. Then
\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)=\bigcup_{\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathcal{J}}(G)
is given the locally convex direct limit topology.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{P}^{0}(G)
be the subspace of
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
such that

\varphi(ag)=\exp(\langle\rho_{P},H_{P}(a)\rangle)\varphi(g)

for every
a\in A_{P}^{\infty}
and
g\in[G]_{P}
. If
P=G
we simply write
\mathcal{A}(G)
and
\mathcal{A}^{0}(G)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)\subset\mathcal{A}_{P}^{0}(G)
be the subspace of
\varphi
such that the Petersson norm
\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
is finite. The spaces
\mathcal{A}_{P}^{0}(G)
and
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
are given the subspace topology.

Remark 3.3
.

In contrast with most references, we follow
[
11
]
and do not ask that our automorphic forms are
K_{\infty}
-finite. By
[
39
]
, the main results on the analytic extensions of Eisenstein series and intertwining operators in the
K_{\infty}
-finite case propagate to the smooth case.

3.4.2
Discrete automorphic representations

We define a discrete automorphic representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
to be a topologically irreducible subrepresentation of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
. Let
\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
be the set of such representations. For
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
, let
\mathcal{A}_{\pi}(G)
be the
\pi
-isotypic component of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
. Note that
\pi
always has trivial central character on
A_{G}^{\infty}
.

For
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
, let
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
be the subspace of
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
such that for all
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
the map
m\in[M_{P}]\mapsto\delta_{P}(m)^{-1/2}\varphi(mg)
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{\pi}(M_{P})
. It is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathcal{J}}(G)
for some ideal of finite codimension
\mathcal{J}
and we give it the induced topology. For any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, set
\pi_{\lambda}=\pi\otimes\exp(\langle\lambda,H_{M_{P}}(\cdot)\rangle)
and for
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
define

\varphi_{\lambda}(g)=\exp(\langle\lambda,H_{P}(g)\rangle)\varphi(g).

The map
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}
identifies
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
with a subspace of
\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
. We denote by
I_{P}(\lambda)
the actions of
G(\mathbb{A})
and
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
we obtain on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
by transporting those on
\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. By
[
21
]
, it decomposes as
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
. For every place
v
, we write
I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
for the smooth parabolic induction of
\pi_{v}
for
G(F_{v})
.

3.4.3
Topologies on spaces of automorphic forms

Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
for
M_{P}
some standard Levi of
G
. Because
\pi
is discrete, we have another choice of topology on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
by realizing it as a subspace of
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{\infty}
. The following lemma explains how to compare these two topologies. We only state it for
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, although it should hold for any reductive groups. More precisely, the potential issue lies within the first assertion where information on the exponents of discrete automorphic forms is used.

Lemma 3.4
.

Assume that
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
and let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
J
be a level. Then for any
N>0
sufficiently large there exist
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for all
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X_{i}}.

In particular,
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
is included in the space of smooth vectors
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{J,\infty}
, and if we endow it with the induced topology we have a closed embedding
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}\subset\mathcal{T}_{N}([G]_{P})^{J}
(where
N
can be chosen independently of
\pi
).

Conversely, without assuming that
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, for any
N>0
large enough and any
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
, there exist
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for any
\varphi\in L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{J,\infty}
we have

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert R(Y_{i})\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}.

In fact, up to constant we can take
Y_{i}=\Delta^{i}
where
\Delta
is the Laplace–Beltrami operator defined in (
3.12
) below.

Proof.

The first assertion is
[
19
, Lemma 3.1.2.1]
, the second is the Sobolev inequality (
[
10
, §3.4, Key Lemma]
, see also
[
19
, Lemma 3.8.1.1]
). The last part is a consequence of
[
7
, Proposition 3.5]
.
∎

3.4.4
Constant terms and cuspidal representations

For
Q
a standard parabolic subgroup and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
, we have a constant term
\varphi_{Q}
defined by

\varphi_{Q}(g)=\int_{[N_{Q}]}\varphi(ng)dn,\quad g\in[G]_{Q}.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\subset\mathcal{A}^{0}_{P}(G)
be the subspace of
\varphi
such that
\varphi_{Q}=0
for all
Q\subsetneq P
. Let
\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
be the set of topologically irreducible subrepresentations of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
, where we equip this space with the subspace topology from
\mathcal{A}(G)
. It is a subset of
\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(G)
.

3.4.5
Intertwining operators

Let
P
and
Q
be standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
w\in W(P,Q)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
such that
\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle
is large enough for any
\alpha\in\Delta_{P}
such that
w\alpha<0
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
, consider the absolutely convergent integral

(M(w,\lambda)\varphi)_{w\lambda}(g)=\int_{(N_{Q}\cap wN_{P}w^{-1})(\mathbb{A})\backslash N_{Q}(\mathbb{A})}\varphi_{\lambda}(w^{-1}ng)dn,\quad g\in[G]_{Q}.

By
[
36
]
and
[
8
]
, it admits a meromorphic continuation to
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
if
\varphi
is
K_{\infty}
-finite. By
[
39
]
, this holds for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
and defines a continuous intertwining operator for any regular
\lambda

M(w,\lambda):\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)\to\mathcal{A}_{Q,w\pi}(G).

(3.5)

By
[
8
, Theorem 2.3]
, the singularities of
M(w,\lambda)
are located along affine root hyperplanes.

Let
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
and
P^{\prime}\subset P
such that
w\in W(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
. Then we have for
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)

\left(M(w,\lambda)\varphi\right)_{Q^{\prime}}=M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P^{\prime}}.

(3.6)

Moreover, if
R
is another standard parabolic and if
w_{1}\in W(P,Q)
and
w_{2}\in W(Q,R)
, by
[
8
, Theorem 2.3.5]
we have the functional equation

M(w_{2},w_{1}\lambda)M(w_{1},\lambda)\varphi=M(w_{2}w_{1},\lambda)\varphi.

(3.7)

3.4.6
Eisenstein series

Let
P\subset Q
be standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. For any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we define

E^{Q}(g,\varphi,\lambda)=\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash Q(F)}\varphi_{\lambda}(\gamma g)=\sum_{\gamma\in M_{Q}\cap P(F)\backslash M_{Q}(F)}\varphi_{\lambda}(\gamma g),\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

(3.8)

This sum is absolutely convergent for
\Re(\lambda)
in a suitable cone. If
\varphi
is
K_{\infty}
-finite, it admits once again a meromorphic continuation to
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
by
[
36
]
and
[
8
]
, and this holds for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
by
[
39
]
. If
Q=G
, we simply write
E(g,\varphi,\lambda)
. By
[
8
, Theorem 2.3]
, the singularities of
E^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)
are located along affine root hyperplanes.

For regular
\lambda
, let
E_{Q}^{G}(\varphi,\lambda)
be the constant term of
E(\varphi,\lambda)
along
Q
. By
[
8
, Lemma 6.10]
, we have

E_{Q}^{G}(\varphi,\lambda)=\sum_{w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}}E^{Q}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w\lambda).

(3.9)

We have the following easy relation between intertwining operators and Eisenstein series.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{disc}}(G)
. Let
Q,Q^{\prime}
be two standard parabolic subgroups of
G
such that
P\subset Q
. Let
w\in W(Q,Q^{\prime})
. Then for regular
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we have

M(w,\lambda)E^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)=E^{Q^{\prime}}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi,w\lambda).

(3.10)

Proof.

This holds in the region of absolute convergence, and for
\lambda
in general position by analytic continuation.
∎

3.4.7
Cuspidal components and residual automorphic forms

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}^{0}(G)
. As cuspidal automorphic forms are of rapid decay (
[
46
, Section I.2.18.]
), for every
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
the pairing
\langle\varphi,\varphi_{0}\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
makes sense. By
[
46
, Section I.2.18]
, there exists a unique
\varphi^{\mathrm{cusp}}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
such that for all
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
we have
\langle\varphi,\varphi_{0}\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\varphi^{\mathrm{cusp}},\varphi_{0}\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
. This definition is then generalized to any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
that is finite under the action by left translation of
A_{P}^{\infty}
(see
[
46
, Section I.3.4]
). The tuple
(\varphi_{Q}^{\mathrm{cusp}})_{Q\subset P}
(where
\varphi_{Q}^{\mathrm{cusp}}
is the cuspidal component of the constant term
\varphi_{Q}
) is called the family of cuspidal components of
\varphi
.

If
\varphi^{\mathrm{cusp}}=0
, then we say that
\varphi
is
residual
. For any regular
\lambda
we have
E(\varphi,\lambda)\in\mathcal{A}(G)
. If this Eisenstein series is proper (that is if
P\neq G
), then
E(\varphi,\lambda)
is residual

3.4.8
Pairs, triples and bases

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. For any level
J
, let
\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})^{J}
(resp.
\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})^{J}
) be the subset of
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
(resp.
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})
) such that
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}\neq\{0\}
. Let
e_{J}
the measure supported on
J
of volume
1
. Then the projection
R(e_{J})
sends
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
to
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
.

Let
\widehat{K}_{\infty}
be the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible unitary representations of
K_{\infty}
. For any
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau}
be
\tau
-isotypic component of
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. For any level
J
, set
