\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}=\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau}\cap\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}.

Following
[
19
, Section 3.2.3]
, we call
J
-pair any
(P,\pi)
where
P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})^{J}
. If
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
, we call
\tau
-pair any
(P,\pi)
with
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau}\neq\{0\}
. We call a
J
-triple any
(P,\pi,\tau)
where
(P,\pi)
is a
J
-pair and
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
with the additional requirement
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}\neq\{0\}
. Note that this subspace is always of finite dimension. Let
\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(\tau,J)
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}
with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
. We then define
\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)
to be the union over
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
of the
\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(\tau,J)
.
If
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
, let
e_{\tau}
be the measure supported on
K_{\infty}
given by
e_{\tau}(k)=\deg(\tau)\mathrm{trace}(\tau(k))dk
, where
dk
is the probability Haar measure on
K_{\infty}
. Then the projection
e_{\tau}
sends
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
to
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau}
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
, set

f_{\tau}:=f*e_{\tau}.

(3.11)

3.4.9
Numerical invariants

We borrow some notation from
[
19
, Section 3.2.2]
. Set

\Delta=\mathrm{Id}-\Omega_{G}+2\Omega_{K},

(3.12)

where
\Omega_{G}
and
\Omega_{K_{\infty}}
are the Casimir operator of
G
and
K_{\infty}
respectively associated to the standard Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}
corresponding to the trace.

For any
\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}
, let
\lambda_{\tau}
be the Casimir eigenvalue of
\tau
. Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
\pi_{\infty}
be an irreducible unitary representation of
M_{P}(F_{\infty})
and let
\lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}
be the Casimir eigenvalue of
\pi_{\infty}
. Set

\Lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{M_{P}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2}},

where
\tau
is a minimal
K_{\infty}\cap M_{P}(F_{\infty})
-type of
\pi_{\infty}
, i.e. whose infinitesimal character has minimal norm, and

\Lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{G}=\min_{\tau}\sqrt{\lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2}},

where the minimum is taken over minimal
K_{\infty}
-types of
\Ind_{P(F_{\infty})}^{G(F_{\infty})}\pi_{\infty}
.

If
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
with Archimedean component
\pi_{\infty}
, set

\lambda_{\pi}=\lambda_{\pi_{\infty}},\quad\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}=\Lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{M_{P}},\quad\Lambda_{\pi}=\Lambda_{\pi_{\infty}}^{G}.

A key property on the Casimir eigenvalues is that, if
(P,\pi,\tau)
is a
J
-triple, then by
[
44
, Lemma 6.1]
we have
\lambda_{\tau}\geq\lambda_{\pi}
.

3.4.10
\mathcal{R}
-regions

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. We define for
k>0
,
c>0
and
C>0

\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\forall\alpha\in\Sigma_{P},\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-c(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left|\langle\Im(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\right|)^{-k}\right\},

(3.13)

and

\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}^{C}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\forall\alpha\in\Sigma_{P},\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-c(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left|\langle\Im(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\right|)^{-k},\quad\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C\right\}.

(3.14)

We also define

\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<c(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left\lVert\Im(\lambda)\right\rVert)^{-k}\right\},

(3.15)

These definitions are inspired by
[
40
, Section 3]
. The two differences are that our sets are subsets of
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
rather than
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{G,*}
, and that in the two
\mathcal{R}
sets we allow
\Re(\lambda)
to grow large in the positive direction.

Let
Q
be another standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
. Set

\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}(w)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Sigma_{P_{w}}\\
w\alpha<0\end{subarray}}\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{w}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-c(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left|\langle\Im(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\right|)^{-k}\right\},

(3.16)

and

\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}^{C}(w)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Sigma_{P_{w}}\\
w\alpha<0\end{subarray}}\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{w}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-c(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left|\langle\Im(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\right|)^{-k},\quad\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C\right\}.

(3.17)

These regions contain
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c}^{C}
respectively.

3.5
Pseudo-Eisenstein series and spectral decompositions

We keep the notation from the previous section. We now present some generalities on pseudo-Eisenstein series and state Langlands spectral decomposition theorem for the scalar product.

3.5.1
Pseudo-Eisenstein series

Let
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*})
be the Paley–Wiener space of functions on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
obtained as Fourier transforms of compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathfrak{a}_{P}
. If
\mathcal{V}
is a finite-dimensional subspace of
K_{\infty}
-finite functions in
\mathcal{A}_{P,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
, we define
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P,\mathcal{V}}
to be the space of
\mathcal{V}
-valued entire functions on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
of Paley–Wiener type. We write
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P}
for the direct sum of all the
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P,\mathcal{V}}
. For
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P}
and any
\kappa\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, consider

F_{\Phi}(g)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\\
\Re(\lambda)=\kappa\end{subarray}}\Phi(\lambda)(g)\exp(\langle\lambda,H_{P}(g)\rangle)d\lambda,\quad g\in G(\mathbb{A}).

(3.18)

It is independent of the choice of
\kappa
. We define the pseudo-Eisenstein series associated to
\Phi
by

E(g,F_{\Phi})=\sum_{\gamma\in P(F)\backslash G(F)}F_{\Phi}(\gamma g),\quad g\in[G].

where this sum is actually over a finite set which depends on
g
by
[
1
, Lemma 5.1]
. This pseudo-Eisenstein series is rapidly decreasing. Moreover, by
[
46
, Section II.1.11]
we have

E(g,F_{\Phi})=\int_{\Re(\lambda)=\kappa}E(g,\Phi(\lambda),\lambda)d\lambda,\quad g\in[G],

(3.19)

for any
\kappa
in the region of the absolute convergence of Eisenstein series.

Lemma 3.6
.

The vector space

\bigoplus_{P_{0}\subset P}\bigoplus_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})}E\left(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P,\pi}\right)

(3.20)

is dense in
\mathcal{S}([G])
.

Proof.

The space in (
3.20
) is stable by the action of the left
K_{\infty}
-finite functions in
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
. The latter form a dense subspace
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
(see
[
1
, 931]
). By the Dixmier–Malliavin theorem of
[
20
]
(see also
[
11
, Section 2.5.3]
), it is therefore enough to show that (
3.20
) is dense in
\mathcal{S}^{00}([G])
. Let
l\in\mathcal{S}^{00}([G])^{*}
the topological dual of this space and assume that it is zero when restricted to (
3.20
). Using the pairing (
3.4
), we can identify it with an element
\varphi
in some
\mathcal{T}_{N}^{0}([G])
. Let
\delta_{n}
be a Dirac sequence in
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
, so that
R(\delta_{n})l
converges weakly to
l
. Then all the
R(\delta_{n})l
correspond to smooth elements
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}^{0}([G])\cap\mathcal{T}([G])
. Moreover, by the same procedure as in
[
25
, Lemma 4.4.3]
, we can choose the
\delta_{n}
to be
K_{\infty}
-finite. It follows that all the
R(\delta_{n})l
are zero on (
3.20
). By the adjunction between constant terms and Eisenstein series from
[
11
, Equation (2.5.13.12)]
, this implies that the
\varphi_{n}
are orthogonal to all the spaces
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}_{P,\pi}
. By
[
46
, Theorem II.1.12]
they must be zero, so that
l
is as well. This concludes the proof. Note that
[
46
, Theorem II.1.12]
is written for the space
L^{2}([G])
but also holds for
\mathcal{T}([G])
(see
[
46
, Proposition I.3.4]
).
∎

3.5.2
Langlands’ spectral decomposition theorem

We now state the version of Langlands’ spectral theorem from
[
36
]
for pseudo–Eisenstein series.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
J
be a level. For every
\Phi\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}^{J}
and
\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{W}^{J}
we have

\langle E(F_{\Phi}),E(F_{\Phi^{\prime}})\rangle_{G}\\
=\sum_{P_{0}\subset P}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{P})\right|}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle E(F_{\Phi^{\prime}}),E(\varphi,\lambda)\rangle_{G}\langle E(\varphi,\lambda),E(F_{\Phi^{\prime}})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(3.21)

Note that the sums in (
3.21
) are finite. In Theorem
4.25
, we will show that the Eisenstein series involved in (
3.21
) form an integrable family in some space
\mathcal{T}_{N}([G])
. This will let us extend Theorem
3.7
to functions of rapid enough decay in Proposition
4.30
.

4
Discrete Eisenstein series on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}

In this chapter, the group
G
is
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
for some
n\geq 1
. We will use the following conventions. We choose
P_{0}
to be the standard Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices, and
M_{0}=T_{0}
to be the diagonal maximal torus. The group
K
is the standard maximal compact subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
.

If
P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, its standard Levi factor is of the form
M_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{m}}
for some integers
n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}
. With this notation, we associate to
P
the tuple
\underline{n}(P):=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{m})
. This completely characterizes
P
among the standard parabolic subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. We will often write
M_{P}=\prod\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
, where we implicitly assume that the product is taken in the order
i=1,\ldots,m
. We identify
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
with
\mathbb{R}^{m}
by sending the canonical basis
(e_{i}^{*})
of
X^{*}(P)
to the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We will write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m})
with respect to this basis.

If
w\in W
, we take the representative of
w
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(F)
prescribed by
[
34
, Section 2]
. If
P=M_{P}N_{P}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, we have an embedding of
W(M_{P})
the Weyl group of
M_{P}
inside
W
. Write
M_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{m}}
. We have an identification (of sets)
W(P)\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{m}
such that, if
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
, we have

\sigma M_{P}\sigma^{-1}=M_{n_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}}\times\ldots\times\ldots M_{n_{\sigma^{-1}(m)}}.

We will often identify a
w\in W(P)
with an element in
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
. We will write
w.P
for the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
with standard Levi factor
wM_{P}w^{-1}
. We say that
w\in W
acts by permutation on the blocks of
M_{P}
(or simply acts by blocks on
P
) if it belongs to
W(P)
.

4.1
Discrete automorphic forms for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}

4.1.1
The classification of
[
45
]

Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
. There exist integers
r,d\geq 1
with
n=rd
and
\sigma\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r})
such that any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
is obtained as the residue of an Eisenstein series built from a
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma^{\boxtimes d}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
where
P_{\pi}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is the standard parabolic subgroup of Levi factor
\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{d}
. More precisely, define

\nu_{\pi}=-\rho_{P_{\pi}}/r,\quad\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma^{\boxtimes d}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P_{\pi}}),

(4.1)

and set for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}

L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}}}\left(\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle-1\right).

(4.2)

Note that
L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(-\nu_{\pi})=0
. We introduce a minus sign in (
4.1
) to follow the convention of
[
19
]
. For every
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
, denote by
E^{*}(g,\phi,\cdot)
the map
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)E(g,\phi,\lambda)
. It is holomorphic in a neighborhood of
-\nu_{\pi}
. By
[
45
]
we have

\varphi(g)=E^{*}(g,\phi,-\nu_{\pi}).

(4.3)

As
\pi
is the unique irreducible quotient of
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
, it deserves to be called a Speh representation and we write
\pi=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
.

Let
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
. Define
r^{\prime}
,
d^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{\prime}
,
P_{\pi^{\prime}}
as in §
4.1.1
for
\pi^{\prime}
. By
[
46
]
, the completed Rankin–Selberg
L
function
L(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})
exists and satisfies

L(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\prod_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}L\left(s+\frac{d-2i+1}{2}+\frac{d^{\prime}-2j+1}{2},\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}\right).

(4.4)

4.1.2
The induced case

We now deal with representations induced from the discrete spectrum of a Levi subgroup, i.e. with representations of Arthur type of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. Let
P=M_{P}N_{P}
be a standard parabolic subgroup. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Write
M_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{m}}
and
\pi=\pi_{1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\pi_{m}
accordingly. By §
4.1.1
, there exist integers
r_{i},d_{i}\geq 1
with
n_{i}=r_{i}d_{i}
and some representations
\sigma_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r_{i}})
such that any
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pi_{i}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}})
is obtained as the residue of an Eisenstein series built from a
\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi_{i}},\sigma_{i}^{\boxtimes d_{i}}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}})
where
P_{\pi_{i}}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
is the standard parabolic subgroup of Levi factor
\mathrm{GL}_{r_{i}}^{d_{i}}
. Set
P_{\pi}=(P_{\pi_{1}}\times\ldots\times P_{\pi_{m}})N_{P}
and
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma_{1}^{\boxtimes d_{1}}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\sigma_{m}^{\boxtimes d_{m}}
which is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
M_{P_{\pi}}
.

Note that any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
is residual unless
\pi
is cuspidal (i.e.
P=P_{\pi}
). Indeed, this follows from the fact that Eisenstein series are orthogonal to cusp forms (in the sense of §
3.4.6
) and that we may compute the residue under the Petersson inner-product by
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
(see also
[
15
, Lemma 9.4.2.1]
for a closely related argument).

Let
Q
and
R
be a parabolic of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
such that
P_{\pi}\subset Q\subset P
. Write
Q\cap M_{P}=Q_{1}\times\ldots\times Q_{m}
. We have a decomposition
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{P,*}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{i}}^{\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}},*}
. Set

\nu_{Q,\pi}=\left(-\rho_{Q_{i}}^{\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}}/r_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq r}

(4.5)

written accordingly. If the context is clear, we omit the subscript
\pi
. If
Q=P_{\pi}
, we will write
\nu_{\pi}
.

By construction, if
P_{\pi}\subset Q\subset P
, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
we have
\varphi_{Q,-\nu_{Q}}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}^{0}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
(see Lemma
4.3
). Moreover, note that
\varphi_{Q}=0
unless
P_{\pi}\subset Q
. If
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
such that
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
, then we set
\varphi_{w}:=\varphi_{P_{w}}
and
\nu_{w}:=\nu_{P_{w}}
.

Set

L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}}^{P}}\left(\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle-1\right).

(4.6)

For every
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
and
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
denote by
E^{P,*}(g,\phi,\cdot)
the partial residues of Eisenstein series
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)E^{P}(g,\phi,\lambda)
. It is holomorphic in a neighborhood of
-\nu_{\pi}
. By exactness of induction, for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
there exists
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
such that
\varphi=E^{P,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi})
. We write
\pi=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{i},d_{i})
.

Finally, let
w^{*}_{\pi}
be the longest element in
W^{P}(P_{\pi},P_{\pi})
, i.e. the unique element
w
in this set such that
w(P_{\pi}\cap M_{P})w^{-1}
is opposed to
P_{\pi}\cap M_{P}
. Note that it acts by identity on
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
and that
w_{\pi}^{*}\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma_{\pi}
and
w_{\pi}^{*}\nu_{\pi}=-\nu_{\pi}
.

4.2
Normalization of intertwining operators

In this section, let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
Q
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. Let
w\in W(P,Q)
and take a
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
in general position. Denote by
M_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
the restriction of
M(w,\lambda)
(defined in (
3.5
)) to the subspace
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)\subset\mathcal{A}_{P}(G)
. Following
[
3
]
, we normalize
M_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
as

M_{\pi}(w,\lambda)=n_{\pi}(w,\lambda)N_{\pi}(w,\lambda).

(4.7)

Here
n_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
is a meromorphic function in
\lambda
referred to as "the scalar factor", and
N_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
is the so-called "normalized operator". If the context is clear, we will remove the subscript
\pi
. We describe these objects below. The goal of this section is to recall the main properties of
M_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
. We then explain how this operator appears in the constant term of discrete automorphic forms.

4.2.1
The scalar factor

Write
M_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{m}}
and
\pi=\pi_{1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\pi_{m}
. Let
\beta\in\Sigma_{P}
be the positive root of
P
associated to the two blocks
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{j}}
, with
1\leq i<j\leq m
. Set

n_{\pi}(\beta,s)=\frac{L(1-s,\pi_{i}^{\vee}\times\pi_{j})}{L(1+s,\pi_{i}\times\pi_{j}^{\vee})},\quad\text{and}\quad n_{\pi}(w,\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Sigma_{P}\\
w\beta<0\end{subarray}}n_{\pi}(\beta,\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle).

(4.8)

By (
4.4
), the scalar factor can be expressed in terms of cuspidal Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions. We write
L_{\infty}
(resp.
L^{\infty}
) for the Archimedean parts of the
L
-functions (resp. the finite part).

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{r_{1}}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{r_{2}}
respectively. Set
\delta_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}=1
if
\sigma_{1}\simeq\sigma_{2}
and
0
otherwise.

1.

Poles: the function

(s(1-s))^{\delta_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}L(s,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee})

is entire of order one.

2.

Functional equation: we have

L(s,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee})=\epsilon(s,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee})L(1-s,\sigma_{1}^{\vee}\times\sigma_{2}),

with
\epsilon(s,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee})=\epsilon_{0}q_{\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee}}^{\frac{1}{2}-s}
where
\epsilon_{0}
is a complex number of modulus
1
and
q_{\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee}}\in\mathbb{N}
is the arithmetic conductor of
\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee}
.

3.

Archimedean factors: we have

L_{\infty}(s,\sigma_{1}\times\sigma_{2}^{\vee})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}(s-\alpha_{j}),

where
m=r_{1}r_{2}[F:\mathbb{Q}]
,
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}(s)=\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s)
(the usual
\Gamma
function) and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
are complex numbers such that for every
j

1-\Re(\alpha_{j})>\frac{1}{r_{1}^{2}+1}+\frac{1}{r_{2}^{2}+1}.

(4.9)

4.

Zero free region: There exists
k
such that for every level
J
there exist
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
and every
i,j
, the meromorphic
s\mapsto L^{\infty}(s,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})
doesn’t have any zero in the region
\Re(s)\geq 1-c_{J}\left(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}+\left|s\right|\right)^{-k}
, where
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma_{1}^{\boxtimes d_{1}}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\sigma_{m}^{\boxtimes d_{m}}
is the cuspidal representation of
M_{P_{\pi}}
defined in §
4.1.2
.

Proof.

1. and 2. are proved in
[
31
]
. The bound (
4.9
) in 3. is
[
43
, Proposition 3.3]
which is based on
[
41
]
. 4. is
[
40
, Proposition 3.5]
which quotes
[
17
]
. More precisely,
[
40
, Proposition 3.5]
writes the zero-free region in terms of the analytic conductor of
\sigma_{\pi}
. But as noted in
[
19
, Section 3.4.7]
, the latter is bounded in terms of
\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}
as the level
J
is fixed. Therefore, we conclude that this zero-free region holds.
∎

4.2.2
Local normalized intertwining operators

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Assume that
\phi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\phi_{v}
is factorizable, so that for all place
v
we have
\phi_{v}\in I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
. Let
S\subset V_{F}
be a finite set of places such that
\phi_{v}
is unramified if
v\notin\mathtt{S}
. By
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
, we have a factorization

N_{\pi}(w,\lambda)\phi=\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)\phi_{v}

(4.10)

where the
N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)
are meromorphic local intertwining operators
I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v,\lambda}\to I_{Q}^{G}w\pi_{v,\lambda}
. The product notation of (
4.10
) means that
N_{\pi}(w,\lambda)\phi
is factorizable and that for
v\notin S
the local component
N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)\phi_{v}
is the unique unramified vector in
I_{Q}^{G}w\pi_{\lambda}
such that
\phi_{v}(1)=N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)\phi_{v}(1)
.

Let
v\in V_{F}
. The normalized operator
N_{\pi_{v}}
is defined in term of a local
L
-function, so that it makes sense for any smooth irreducible unitary representation
\pi_{v}
of
M_{P}(F_{v})
(which is not necessarily a local constituent of some
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
). We recall the classification of such representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
for
N\geq 1
. For any discrete series
\delta
of some
\mathrm{GL}_{r}(F_{v})
and any
d\geq 1
, let
\mathrm{Speh}(\delta,d)
be the unique irreducible quotient of the parabolic induction
\delta_{\frac{d-1}{2}}\times\ldots\times\delta_{\frac{1-d}{2}}
. By
[
50
, Theorem A(ii)]
in the non-Archimedean case, and
[
51
]
in the Archimedean case, for any smooth irreducible unitary representation
\tau
of
\mathrm{GL}_{N}(F_{v})
there exist discrete series
\delta_{1}\ldots\delta_{k}
of some
\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(F_{v})
, integers
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
and real numbers
-1/2<\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{k}<1/2
such that

\tau\simeq\mathrm{Speh}(\delta_{1},d_{1})_{\nu_{1}}\times\ldots\times\mathrm{Speh}(\delta_{k},d_{k})_{\nu_{k}}.

Set

e(\tau)=2\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}-\left|\nu_{i}\right|\;\big|\;1\leq i\leq k\right\}.

If now
\pi_{v}=\pi_{v,1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\pi_{v,m}
is a smooth irreducible unitary representation of
M_{P}(F_{v})
, set
e(\pi_{v})=\min e(\pi_{v,i})
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\pi_{v}
be a smooth irreducible and unitary representation of
M_{P}(F_{v})
.

1.

For each
\phi_{v}\in I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
the vector
N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)\phi_{v}
is a rational function in
\lambda
if
v
is Archimedean, and in
q_{v}^{-\lambda}
if
v
is non-Archimedean. More precisely, it is a rational function in the variables
\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle
(resp.
q_{v}^{-\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle}
) for the
\alpha\in\Delta_{P}
such that
w\alpha<0
in the Archimedean case (resp. the non-Archimedean case).

2.

If
w_{1}\in W(P,Q)
and
w_{2}\in W(Q,R)
we have

N_{w_{1}\pi_{v}}(w_{2},w_{1}\lambda)N_{\pi_{v}}(w_{1},\lambda)=N_{\pi_{v}}(w_{2}w_{1},\lambda).

3.

The operator
N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)
is holomorphic in the region

\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Sigma_{P}\\
w\alpha<0\end{subarray}}\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;|\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-e(\pi_{v})\right\}.

4.

If
\pi_{v}
is the local component of some
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
, then

e(\pi_{v})>\frac{2}{1+n^{2}}.

Proof.

1., 2. are contained in
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
. By decomposing
N_{\pi_{v}}(w,\lambda)
as a product of rank one intertwining operators, 3. is
[
45
, Proposition I.10]
. 4. is proved for local components of cuspidal representations in
[
43
, Proposition 3.3]
, and for residual representations in
[
43
, Proposition 3.5]
.
∎

4.2.3
Constant terms of discrete automorphic forms

We now fix a standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. We have
P_{\pi}
and
\sigma_{\pi}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P_{\pi}})
as in §
4.1.2
. For any
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
consider the regularized operator

M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},\lambda)\phi:=L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)M(w_{\pi}^{*},\lambda)\phi.

(4.11)

It follows from the factorization of the global intertwining operators (
4.7
), from the localization of the poles of the global
L
-factors in Theorem
4.1
, and from the regularity of the local intertwining operators in Theorem
4.2
that there exists a constant
c_{\pi}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we have

M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi}+\lambda)\phi=c_{\pi}N_{\sigma_{\pi}}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi}+\lambda)\phi.

(4.12)

By Theorem
4.2
, we see that
M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi}+\lambda)\phi
is regular on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Morever, by
[
16
, Corollary 3.3]

M^{*}(w_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})
also realizes the quotient map
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi}}(G)\to\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
.

We now use the notation of §
4.1.2
. Let
P_{\pi}\subset Q\subset P
. For each
1\leq i\leq m
there exist integers
d_{i,1},\ldots,d_{i,m_{i}}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}d_{i,j}=d_{i}
and
M_{Q}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathrm{GL}_{r_{i}d_{i,j}}
. For each pair
(i,j)
, set
n_{i,j}=r_{i}d_{i,j}
and
P_{i,j}=P_{\pi_{i}}\cap\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i,j}}
. Let
\pi_{i,j}
be the discrete representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i,j}}
spanned by the residues of Eisenstein series built from
\phi_{i,j}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{i,j},\sigma_{i}^{\boxtimes d_{i,j}}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i,j}})
. Set
\pi_{Q}=\boxtimes_{i}\boxtimes_{j}\pi_{i,j}
which is a discrete representation of
M_{Q}
. The following is
[
16
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
we have
\varphi_{Q,-\nu_{Q}}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q,\pi_{Q}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
. In particular,
\varphi_{Q,-\nu_{Q}}
is residual unless
Q=P_{\pi}
. Moreover, in that case, assume that
\varphi=E^{P,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi})
for
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(\mathrm{GL}_{n})
. Then we have

\varphi_{P_{\pi}}=M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi})\phi.

(4.13)

We note that our choices of measures yield an adjunction between residues of Eisenstein series and constant terms.

Proposition 4.4
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi}}(G)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
we have

\langle E^{P,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi}),\varphi\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\phi,\varphi_{P_{\pi}}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}.

Proof.

By the Iwasawa decomposition, it is enough to prove that the proposition holds if
P=G
. Write
M_{P_{\pi}}=\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{d}
and
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma^{\boxtimes d}
where
\sigma\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r})
. Choose
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi}}
such that
E^{*}(\phi^{\prime})=\varphi
. Let
\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}
be Arthur’s truncation operator from
[
2
]
. Here
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{G}
is a truncation parameter, and we say that it is sufficiently positive if we have
\langle\alpha,T\rangle>M
for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{0}
. This also naturally defines a notion of
\lim\limits_{T\to\infty}
. By the Maaß–Selberg relation of
[
19
, Theorem 3.1.3.1]
, for
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{G,*}
in general position and
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{G}
sufficiently positive we have

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}E(\phi,\lambda),E(\phi^{\prime},\overline{\lambda^{\prime}})\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{w,w^{\prime}\in W(P_{\pi},P_{\pi})}\langle M(w,\lambda)\phi,M(w^{\prime},\overline{\lambda^{\prime}})\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}\frac{\exp(\langle w\lambda+w^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime},T_{P_{\pi}}\rangle)}{\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art}}(w\lambda+w^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime})},

(4.14)

where for any standard parabolic subgroup
Q
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
we write
\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{G}/\mathbb{Z}(\Delta_{Q}^{\vee}))
be the covolume of the lattice generated by
\Delta_{Q}^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{G}
and we set

\theta_{Q}^{\mathrm{Art}}(\lambda)=\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{G}/\mathbb{Z}(\Delta_{Q}^{\vee}))^{-1}\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{Q}}\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle.

We compute residues of this expression in two ways. Set

f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}):=L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda^{\prime})\langle\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}E(\phi,\lambda),E(\phi^{\prime},\overline{\lambda^{\prime}})\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}}.

First, by the continuity of the truncation operator from
[
55
, Theorem 3.9]
and of Eisenstein series from
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
, we have

f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(-\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})=\langle\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}E^{*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi}),E^{*}(\phi^{\prime},-\nu_{\pi})\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}E^{*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi}),\varphi\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}}.

By another application of the Maaß–Selberg relation, this is

f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(-\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})=\sum_{Q\supset P_{\pi}}\langle E^{*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi})_{Q},\varphi_{Q}\rangle_{Q,\mathrm{Art}}\frac{\exp(\langle 2\nu_{Q},T_{Q}\rangle)}{\theta_{Q}^{\mathrm{Art}}(2\nu_{Q})},

which is well defined as for all
P_{\pi}\subset Q
and
\alpha\in\Delta_{Q}
we have
\langle 2\nu_{Q},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle<0
. In particular, by taking the limit as
T\to\infty
we obtain

\lim_{T\to\infty}f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(-\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})=\langle E^{*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi}),\varphi\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}}.

(4.15)

We now start from the RHS of (
4.2.3
). For regular
\lambda
we have

f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(\lambda,-\nu_{\pi})=L_{\pi,\mathrm{res}}(\lambda)\sum_{w\in W(P_{\pi},P_{\pi})}\langle M(w,\lambda)\phi,M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi})\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}\frac{\exp(\langle w\lambda+\nu_{\pi},T_{P_{\pi}}\rangle)}{\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art}}(w\lambda+\nu_{\pi})}.

Let
w\in W(P_{\pi},P_{\pi})
. In a neighborhood of
-\nu_{\pi}
, the poles of
\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art}}(w\lambda+\nu_{\pi})^{-1}
are simple and along the affine hyperplanes
\langle\lambda,w^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=\langle-\nu_{\pi},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle=1
for
\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}}
. In particular, they are distinct from the poles of
M(w,\lambda)
at
-\nu_{\pi}
which are of the form
\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle=1
with
\beta^{\vee}\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}}
and
w\beta<0
. We may therefore compute the residues under the sum to obtain

f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(-\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})=\sum_{w\in W(P_{\pi},P_{\pi})}\langle M^{*}(w,-\nu_{\pi})\phi,M^{*}(w_{\pi}^{*},-\nu_{\pi})\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}\frac{\exp(\langle-w\nu_{\pi}+\nu_{\pi},T_{P_{\pi}}\rangle)}{\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art},*}(-w\nu_{\pi}+\nu_{\pi})},

where
M^{*}(w,-\nu_{\pi})
and
\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art},*}(-w\nu_{\pi}+\nu_{\pi})
are the appropriate regularizations. But we see that
\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle-w\nu_{\pi}+\nu_{\pi},T_{P_{\pi}}\rangle=-\infty
unless
w=1
, so that by Lemma
4.3

\lim_{T\to\infty}f_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}^{T}(-\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi})=\frac{\langle\phi,\varphi_{P_{\pi}}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}}{\theta_{P_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{Art},*}(0)}=\langle\phi,\varphi_{P_{\pi}}\rangle_{P_{\pi},\mathrm{Pet}}\times\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}}^{G}/\mathbb{Z}(\Delta_{P_{\pi}}^{\vee})).

(4.16)

By going back to our choices of measures in §
3.2.3
and of coordinates, we see that
\mathrm{vol}(\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}}^{G}/\mathbb{Z}(\Delta_{P_{\pi}}^{\vee}))
is equal to
1
. The proposition now follows from (
4.15
) and (
4.16
).
∎

4.3
Regularity of intertwining operators

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
Q
be another standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and take
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
such that
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
. Write
\pi_{w}
for the representation
\pi_{P_{w}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P_{w}})
defined in Lemma
4.3
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. As before, set
\nu_{w}=\nu_{P_{w}}
and
\varphi_{w}=\varphi_{P_{w}}
. By Lemma
4.3
, we have the normalization

M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}=n_{\pi_{w}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{w})N_{\pi_{w}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{w})\varphi_{w,-\nu_{w}}

(4.17)

The goal of this section is to determine the poles of
M(w,\lambda)
in a neighborhood of
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*,+}
of the form
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
(see (
3.13
)).

4.3.1
Regularity of scalar factors

Lemma 4.5
.

There exist
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
there exists a product of affine root linear forms
L_{\pi,w}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}]
such that the product

\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)n_{\pi_{w}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{P_{w}}).

is regular in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
.

Remark 4.6
.

As the proof shows, the set
\{L_{\pi,w}\}
, where
w
and
\pi
range as in §
4.3
, is finite. Moreover, if
\pi
is cuspidal we may take

L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
w(i)>w(j)\\
\pi_{i}\simeq\pi_{j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}-1).

We do not give a precise description of
L_{\pi,w}
for general
\pi
, as it will be easily computable in the cases we are interested in.

Proof.

Using the description of
L
-factors for discrete automorphic representations given in (
4.4
), we see that we have the equality

n_{\pi_{w}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{w})=n_{\sigma_{\pi}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi}).

(4.18)

Therefore we may assume that
P_{w}=P_{\pi}
. We use the notation of §
4.1.2
. For each
i
, set
D_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}d_{j}
. Then
w
acts by permuting the blocks of
M_{P_{\pi}}
so that we may identify it with an element of
\mathfrak{S}_{D_{m}}
. Let
1\leq i<j\leq m
. For each
1\leq a\leq d_{i}
, let
b_{a}
be the greatest integer such that
1\leq b_{a}\leq d_{j}
and
w(b_{a}+D_{j-1})<w(a+D_{i-1})
(if no such integer exists, set
b_{a}=0
). Set

\displaystyle n_{i,j}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\prod_{a=1}^{d_{i}}\prod_{b=1}^{b_{a}}\frac{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a-b+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a-(b-1)+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{a=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a-b_{a}+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}.

(4.19)

There is another description of
n_{i,j}
: if
1\leq b\leq d_{j}
, let
a_{b}
be the smallest integer such that
1\leq a_{b}\leq d_{i}
and
w(b+D_{j-1})<w(a_{b}+D_{i-1})
(if no such integer exists, set
a_{b}=d_{i}+1
). Then we have

\displaystyle n_{i,j}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\prod_{b=1}^{d_{j}}\prod_{a=a_{b}}^{d_{i}}\frac{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a-b+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+(a+1)-b+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{b=1}^{d_{j}}\frac{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a_{b}-b+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+1-b+\frac{d_{j}+d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}.

(4.20)

Then by (
4.8
) and the functional equation of
L
-functions (Theorem
4.1
), we have

n_{\sigma}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})=\epsilon(\lambda)\prod_{i<j}n_{i,j}(\lambda),

(4.21)

where
\epsilon
is some product of
\epsilon
-factors which are entire.

Take
k
and
c_{J}
given by Theorem
4.1
. If
d_{j}\geq d_{i}
, for every
a\geq 1
we see that the function
L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a+\frac{d_{j}-d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)
doesn’t have any zero in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
by Theorem
4.1
. If
d_{j}\leq d_{i}
, we see that for every
b\leq d_{j}
it is
L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+1-b+\frac{d_{j}+d_{i}}{2},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)
which doesn’t have any zero in
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. By (
4.21
), the only poles of
n_{\sigma_{\pi}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})
come from poles of
L(\cdot,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})
. By Theorem
4.1
, they are all simple and located along a finite collection of affine root hyperplanes (even as
\pi
varies), so that there exists
L_{\pi,w}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}]
such that
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)n_{\sigma_{\pi}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})
is regular on
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
as claimed.
∎

We also note that the polynomial
L_{\pi,w}
can be ignored in a neighborhood of
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
if
w\in W(P)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Assume that
w\in W(P,Q)
. There exist
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
the map

\lambda\mapsto n_{\pi}(w,\lambda).

is regular in the region
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
(see (
3.15
)).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
, we only have to show that
n_{\sigma_{\pi}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})
doesn’t have any poles in the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. We keep the notation from the previous proof. Assume that
w
switches the blocks
i
and
j
of
M_{P}
with
i<j
, and that
d_{j}\geq d_{i}
. By (
4.3.1
), the associated factor is

n_{i,j}(\lambda)=\prod_{a=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+\frac{d_{i}-d_{j}}{2}-a+1,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+\frac{d_{i}+d_{j}}{2}-a+1,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee}\right)}.

By Theorem
4.1
, the only possible pole of
n_{i,j}
on
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
is located along the hyperplane
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
and only occurs if the terms associated to
\frac{d_{i}-d_{j}}{2}-a+1\in\{0,1\}
appear in the product. For the
0
case, we obtain
a=1+\frac{d_{i}-d_{j}}{2}
which is only possible if
d_{i}=d_{j}
. This term is compensated in the denominator by the term corresponding to
\frac{d_{i}+d_{j}}{2}-a+1=1
, i.e.
a=d_{i}
. For the
1
case, we get
a=\frac{d_{i}-d_{j}}{2}
which is excluded. If
d_{i}\geq d_{j}
, we use the same procedure with (
4.3.1
) instead, which concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.2
Regularity of local normalized operators

We keep the data defined at the top of §
4.3
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For every place
v
the composition

N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w^{*}_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})

(4.22)

is holomorphic in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;|\;\forall\alpha\in\Sigma_{P},\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-e(\sigma_{\pi,v})\right\}.

(4.23)

More precisely, let
\lambda
be an element in the region
(
4.23
)
. Let
w^{\prime}\in W(P_{\pi})
be an element such that, if we set
P_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}.P_{\pi}
, for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}^{\prime}}
we have
\langle(w^{\prime})^{-1}(\lambda-\nu_{\pi}),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-e(\sigma_{\pi,v})
. Set
\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}\lambda
and
\nu_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}\nu_{\pi}
. Then we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P_{\pi}^{\prime}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime},v}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,\lambda-\nu_{\pi},v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v,\lambda}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{w.P}^{G}w\pi_{v,\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(ww_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w_{\pi}^{*},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(ww_{\pi}^{*},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w,\lambda)}

where all the arrows are holomorphic in a neighborhood of
\lambda
,
N_{\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime},v}(w_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})
is surjective and we identify
I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
and
I_{wP}^{G}(w.\pi_{v})
as subrepresentations of
I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,\nu_{\pi},v}
and
I_{w.P_{\pi}}^{G}w\sigma_{\pi,\nu_{\pi},v}
respectively.

Proof.

This is
[
45
, Proposition I.11]
. As the terminology there differs from ours, some comments are in order. Our element
w_{\pi}^{*}
is denoted
w
, and our
w
is
\sigma
. The condition
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
implies condition (b) of
[
45
, Proposition I.11]
. The parameter
\lambda
is
\underline{s}
. That
\lambda
belongs to the region (
4.23
) is condition (c). The representation
\sigma_{v}
is denoted
\pi
and is assumed to be generic (which is the case here as
\sigma_{v}
is the local component of a cuspidal representation). Finally, the element
w^{\prime}
from
[
45
, Proposition I.11]
is the one that we use.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

We keep the notation from Lemma
4.8
. For every place
v
, the composition
N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w^{*}_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})
is holomorphic in the region

\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Sigma_{P_{w}}\\
w\alpha<0\end{subarray}}\left\{\mu\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{w}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\langle\Re(\mu),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-e(\sigma_{\pi,v})\right\}.

Proof.

If
w\in W(P)
(so that
\nu_{w}=0
and
P_{w}=P
), this follows from Lemma
4.8
by decomposing
w
as a product of adjacent transpositions. In general, decompose
w_{\pi}^{*}=w_{\pi_{w}}^{*}w^{\pi_{w}}
. Then we have and
w^{\pi_{w}}\nu_{\pi}=-\nu_{w}+\nu_{\pi_{w}}
. Note that
\sigma_{\pi_{w}}=\sigma_{\pi}
. We have for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position

N(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})N(w^{*}_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})=N(w,(\lambda+\nu_{w})+\nu_{\pi_{w}})N(w_{\pi_{w}}^{*},(\lambda+\nu_{w})-\nu_{\pi_{w}})N(w^{\pi_{w}},\lambda-\nu_{\pi}).

(4.24)

By Theorem
4.2
,
N(w^{\pi_{w}},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})
is regular for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Because
w\in W(P_{w})
, we conclude by the first case.
∎

From Lemma
4.8
we easily get the regularity of
N_{\pi_{w},v}
for general
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
such that
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
and assume that
\varphi_{w}
is factorizable. Then for every place
v
the local intertwining operator

N_{\pi_{w},v}(w,\lambda+\nu_{w})\varphi_{w,-\nu_{w},v}

(4.25)

is holomorphic in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;|\;\forall\alpha\in\Sigma_{P},\;\langle\Re(\lambda),\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-2/(n^{2}+1)\right\}.

(4.26)

Proof.

We know by
[
16
, Corollary 3.3]
that we can identify globally
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
as a subrepresentation of
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},\nu_{\pi}}(G)
by applying the constant term
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{P_{\pi}}
, which amounts locally to identifying
I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
with the image of the intertwining operator
N_{\sigma_{\pi},v}(\cdot,-\nu_{\pi}):I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\nu_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi},v}\to I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\nu_{\pi},\nu_{\pi},v}
. With the same notation as in Corollary
4.9
, we have

I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,-\nu_{\pi},v}\xrightarrow{N_{\sigma_{\pi},v}(w^{\pi_{w}},-\nu_{\pi})}I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,-\nu_{\pi_{w}}+\nu_{w},v}\xrightarrow{N_{\sigma_{\pi},v}(w_{\pi_{w}}^{*},-\nu_{\pi_{w}}+\nu_{w})}I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,\nu_{\pi},v},

so that
I_{P}^{G}\pi_{v}
is identified with the image of this composition in
I_{P_{\pi}}^{G}\sigma_{\nu_{\pi},\nu_{\pi},v}
. This is a subrepresentation of the image of the last arrow which is
I_{P_{w}}^{G}\pi_{w,\nu_{w},v}
. Therefore the regularity of (
4.25
) is equivalent to the regularity of
N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})N_{\sigma_{\pi,v}}(w^{*}_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})
, which is known by Lemma
4.8
.

∎

4.3.3
Regularity of global intertwining operators

We can now describe the singularities of
M(w,\lambda)
.

Proposition 4.11
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
with
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
the map

\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}

(4.27)

is regular in
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
, where
L_{\pi,w}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}]
is the product of affine root linear forms defined in Lemma
4.5
.

Remark 4.12
.

By Lemma
4.10
, all the singularities of
M(w,\lambda)
in
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
come from the scalar factor
n_{\pi_{w}}(\lambda+\nu_{w})
. But
L_{\pi,w}
can have zeros in
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
and the operator
M(w,\lambda)
can indeed have poles in this region. This can already be seen for
G=\mathrm{GL}_{4}
in the unramified case (see
[
28
]
).

Proof.

This follows from the factorization (
4.17
) and Lemmas
4.5
and
4.10
.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

The map
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}
is regular in the region
\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
, where we recall that
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
was defined in (
3.16
).

Proof.

We have

M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}=M(w,\lambda+\nu_{w})\varphi_{P_{w},-\nu_{w}},

and we know by Lemma
4.3
that
\varphi_{P_{w},-\nu_{w}}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{w},\pi_{w}}(G)
. The result now follows from decomposing the intertwining operator and applying successively Proposition
4.11
. Note that here we use the same argument as in
[
19
, Section 3.4.7]
to make
\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}}
appear and not
\Lambda_{\pi_{w}}^{M_{P_{w}}}
(which is a consequence of the fact that
\pi
and
\pi_{w}
are obtained as residues of Eisenstein series built from the same cuspidal representations).
∎

We also obtain a stronger version if
w\in W(P,Q)
. In this case, we already knew that the operator
M(w,\lambda)\varphi
was regular on
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
by
[
5
, Theorem 7.2]
. The novelty is to extend this regularity property to a neighborhood of the unitary axis.

Proposition 4.14
.

Assume that
w\in W(P,Q)
. There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
the map

\lambda\mapsto M(w,\lambda)\varphi

is regular in
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)

Proof.

This follows from the factorization (
4.17
) and Lemmas
4.7
and
4.10
.
∎

We now record a generalization of a well-known lemma on the behavior of intertwining operators along root-hyperplanes. We use the notation of §
4.1.2
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Assume that
n=2m
and let
P
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with standard Levi factor
\mathrm{GL}_{m}\times\mathrm{GL}_{m}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
be of the form
\pi=\pi_{1}\boxtimes\pi_{1}
. Let
w
be the only non-trivial element in
W(P,P)
. Then for every place
v
of
F
we have
N_{\pi,v}(w,0)=\mathrm{Id}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\pi_{1}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
with
\sigma
cuspidal. There exists
w^{\prime}\in W(P_{\pi})
such that
-w^{\prime}\nu_{\pi}
is positive. Set
P_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}.P_{\pi}
and set
\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma_{\pi}
and
\nu_{\pi}^{\prime}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}\nu_{\pi}
. Set
w_{0}=(w^{\prime})^{-1}w_{\pi}^{*}ww_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime}
. By Theorem
4.2
and Lemma
4.8
, for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{G,*}
in a neighborhood of
0
we have the commutative diagram with surjective vertical maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P_{\pi}^{\prime}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime},v}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P_{\pi}^{\prime}}^{G}\sigma_{\pi,-\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime},v}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P}^{G}\pi_{\lambda,v}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I_{P}^{G}\pi_{-\lambda,v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w_{0},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w_{\pi}^{*}w^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}-\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(w,\lambda)}

It therefore suffices to prove that
N_{\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}}(w_{0},\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})=\mathrm{Id}
. But we can decompose
\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}=(\sigma\boxtimes\sigma)^{\boxtimes d}
, and by Theorem
4.2
we see that
N_{\sigma_{\pi}^{\prime}}(w_{0},\nu_{\pi}^{\prime})
is the a product of operators
N_{\sigma\boxtimes\sigma,v}(\iota,0)
, with
\iota
the involution switching the two blocks of
\sigma\boxtimes\sigma
. By
[
34
, Proposition 6.3]
, they are equal to
\mathrm{Id}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
P
be standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
w
be the element that acts by blocks on
P
corresponding to the transposition
(i\;j)
with
i<j
. If
\pi_{i}\simeq\pi_{j}
, then for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position in the hyperplane
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
we have for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)

M(w,\lambda)\varphi=-\varphi_{\lambda}.

(4.28)

Proof.

Let us keep the notation of the proof of Lemma
4.5
. If
l\neq i
, we easily see that
n_{i,l}(\lambda)
(or
n_{l,i}(\lambda)
) is regular along the hyperplane
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
, and the same goes for
n_{l,j}(\lambda)
(or
n_{j,l}(\lambda)
). As
d_{i}=d_{j}
and
b_{a}=d_{j}
for every
1\leq a\leq d_{i}
, we compute using (
4.3.1
)

n_{i,j}(\lambda)=\frac{L(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})}{L(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+1,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})}\prod_{a=1}^{d_{i}-1}L(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a-d_{i},\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})\prod_{a=2}^{d_{i}}\frac{1}{L(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+a,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})}.

By Theorem
4.1
,
n_{i,j}(\lambda)
is regular if
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}=0
. As
N_{\pi}(w,\lambda)
is regular for
\lambda
in general position in this hyperplane by Lemma
4.10
, so is
M(w,\lambda)\varphi
.

Let
\tau\in W(P,P)
be the permutation
(i+1\;\ldots\;j-1\;j)
. Then if
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
we have
w\lambda=\lambda
and therefore by (
3.7
)

M(w,\lambda)=M(\tau^{-1},\tau\lambda)M(\tau w\tau^{-1},\tau\lambda)M(\tau,\lambda).

By the previous discussion,
M(\tau^{-1},\tau\lambda)
,
M(\tau w\tau^{-1},\tau\lambda)
and
M(\tau,\lambda)
are regular for
\lambda
in general position along the hyperplane
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
.

It follows that it is enough to prove that (
4.28
) holds in the case
m=2
, i.e.
P=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}
,
\pi=\pi_{1}\boxtimes\pi_{1}
,
P_{\pi}=\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{d}\times\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{d}
,
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma^{\boxtimes d}\boxtimes\sigma^{\boxtimes d}
and
w=(1\;2)
. By (
4.17
) and Lemma
4.15
, we have to prove that
n_{\pi}(w,\lambda)=-1
along our hyperplane. Set
s=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
. By (
4.18
) and Theorem
4.1
, it is easy to see that
n_{\pi}(w,s)
is regular at
s=0
and that
n_{\pi}(w,0)=(-1)^{d}(-1)^{d-1}=-1
. This concludes.
∎

4.4
Analytic properties of discrete Eisenstein series

The main result of this section is Theorem
4.17
below which describes the analytic behavior of discrete Eisenstein series in a neighborhood
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
of the positive Weyl chambers.

4.4.1
Bernstein–Zelevinsky segments

To write our result, we use the notion of segments from
[
9
]
. We define a segment
S
to be a
r
-tuple of complex numbers
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})
with

s_{1}-s_{2}=-1,\;s_{2}-s_{3}=-1,\ldots,s_{r-1}-s_{r}=-1.

The set
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\}
completely determines the tuple
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})
, so that we may think of
S
as a set of complex numbers. If
S
and
T
are two segments, we say that they are linked if
S
and
T
are not included in one another and if
S\cup T
is still a segment.

We use the notation of §
4.1.2
. To any
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
we associate the
m
Bernstein–Zelevinsky segments
S_{\pi}^{1},\ldots,S_{\pi}^{m}
such that each
i
,
S_{\pi}^{i}=\nu_{P_{\pi_{i}}}
. In coordinates,
S_{\pi}^{i}
is the
d_{i}
-tuple

\left(S_{\pi}^{i}\right)_{j}=\frac{2j-1-d_{i}}{2},\quad 1\leq j\leq d_{i}.

If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with coordinates
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m})
, let
S_{\pi}^{i}(\lambda)
be the segment
S_{\pi}^{i}+\lambda_{i}
. For each
i<j
, the set of
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
such that
S_{\pi}^{i}(\lambda)
and
S_{\pi}^{j}(\lambda)
are linked is a finite union of affine root hyperplanes of
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Let
L_{\pi,i,j}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}]
be the corresponding product of affine root linear forms. Define

L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\sigma_{i}\simeq\sigma_{j}\end{subarray}}L_{\pi,i,j}(\lambda),

(4.29)

where
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma_{1}^{\boxtimes d_{1}}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes\sigma_{m}^{\boxtimes d_{m}}
. Note that the set
\{L_{\pi,E}\}
is finite as
\pi
varies.

4.4.2
Analytic properties of discrete Eisenstein series

We can now state the theorem.

Theorem 4.17
.

The following properties hold.

1.

Regularity: there exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
the meromorphic function

L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)E(\varphi,\lambda)

is holomorphic in the region
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
.

2.

Zeros: let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
,
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. For every
\alpha_{i,j}\in\Sigma_{P}
which is associated to two blocks
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{j}}
of
M_{P}
with
\pi_{i}\simeq\pi_{j}
, the Eisenstein series
E(\varphi,\lambda)
has a zero along the root hyperplane
\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i,j}^{\vee}\rangle=0
.

Remark 4.18
.

Recall that we have defined a polynomial
L_{\pi,w}
in Lemma
4.5
. Then
L_{\pi,E}
divides the product
\prod_{Q}\prod_{w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}}L_{\pi,w}
, but in general is not equal to it except if
\pi
is cuspidal. Theorem
4.17
therefore shows that there are some subtle compensations of residues in the constant term
E_{P_{\pi}}(\varphi,\lambda)
. Moreover, Theorem
4.17
is sharp in the sense that all the zeros of
L_{\pi,E}
compensate poles of
E(\varphi,\lambda)
as can already be seen in the unramified case
[
28
]
.

Proof.

Let us prove 1. We first assume that
\varphi
is
K_{\infty}
-finite. By
[
46
, Lemma I.4.10]
, the singularities of
L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)E(\varphi,\lambda)
are the same as those of its cuspidal exponents. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
be such that
\varphi=E^{P,*}(\phi)
. By the computation of the constant term of Eisenstein series (
3.9
) and by Lemma
4.3
, for every standard parabolic subgroup
Q
we have

L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)E_{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)^{\mathrm{cusp}}=c_{\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}\\
P_{\pi}=P_{w},\;Q_{w}=Q\end{subarray}}L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda+\nu_{\pi})N_{\sigma}(w_{\pi}^{*},\lambda-\nu_{\pi})\phi,

(4.30)

where
c_{\pi}
is the constant from (
4.12
). By
[
45
, Lemme III.2]
, (
4.30
) is regular in a neighborhood of
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
as long as for all
\alpha\in\Sigma_{P}
and all
i
and
j
we have
L(1+\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle,\sigma_{i}\times\sigma_{j}^{\vee})\neq 0
and moreover
\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>-e(\sigma_{\pi,v})
for any place
v\in V_{F}
. More precisely, note that (
4.30
) is the first equation of
[
45
, 652]
(see the proof of Lemma
4.8
for some comments on the notation of
[
45
]
). By Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
we may choose
k
and
c_{J}
such that these conditions are satisfied in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. The case of general
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
follows from the
K_{\infty}
-finite case by the same argument as
[
7
, Remark 11.8]
.

For 2, note that by 1. the hyperplane
\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i,j}^{\vee}\rangle=0
is not singular for
E(\varphi,\lambda)
. Let
w\in W(P)\simeq\mathfrak{S}_{m}
be the transposition
(i\;j)
. By the functional equation of Eisenstein series from
[
8
, Theorem 2.3.4]
and Lemma
4.16
, for any
\lambda
such that
\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i,j}^{\vee}\rangle=0
we have

E(\varphi,\lambda)=E(M(w,\lambda)\varphi,w\lambda)=-E(\varphi,\lambda).

This proves 3.
∎

For every standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
and every
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
, set

L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)=\prod_{\pi_{i}\simeq\pi_{j}}\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i,j}^{\vee}\rangle.

(4.31)

This polynomial controls the zeros of
E(\varphi,\lambda)
. Using Theorem
4.17
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.19
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
the meromorphic function

\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(\varphi,\lambda)

is holomorphic in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
.

4.4.3
The case of generalized Eisenstein series

Let
P,Q
be standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
such that
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
. Let
\pi_{w}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P_{w}})
be the representation defined in §
4.2.3
. Write
M_{Q}=\prod_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}\mathrm{GL}_{n^{\prime}_{i}}
and set
Q_{w,i}=\mathrm{GL}_{n^{\prime}_{i}}\cap Q_{w}
. Write
w\pi_{w}=(w\pi_{w})_{1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes(w\pi_{w})_{m^{\prime}}
accordingly. We have
\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{w},\mathbb{C}}^{*}=\oplus_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{w,i},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, and for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{w},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we denote by
p_{i}(\lambda)
its projection on
\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{w,i},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Set

L_{w\pi_{w},E}^{Q}(\lambda)=\prod_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}L_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},E}\left(p_{i}(\lambda)\right),

(4.32)

where
L_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},E}
is defined in (
4.29
). Moreover, for any
c
and
k
let
\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},k,c}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{Q_{w,i},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
be the subset defined in (
3.13
) for
(w\pi_{w})_{i}
.

Proposition 4.20
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for any
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the Eisenstein series

L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)L_{w\pi_{w},E}^{Q}(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))E^{Q}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w},w\lambda)

is regular if
\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
and if
p_{i}(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},k,c_{J}}
for each
i
.

Remark 4.21
.

If
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})
(in which case
P_{w}=P_{\pi}=P
and
\nu_{w}=0
), it is enough to ask that
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. Indeed, this already implies that
\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
and that
p_{i}(w\lambda)\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},k,c_{J}}
for each
i
. Moreover for every
w
the product
L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)L_{w\pi_{w},E}^{Q}(w\lambda)
divides
L_{\pi,E}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.13
, we may choose the constants
k
and
c_{J}>0
such that the map
L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}
is regular in
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. By the Iwasawa decomposition, a generalized Eisenstein series
E^{Q}(\psi,\lambda)
is regular at some
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
if and only if its restriction to
M_{Q}(\mathbb{A})
is. By Lemma
4.3
we have

\left(\left(M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}\right)_{|M_{Q}}\right)_{-w\nu_{w}-\rho_{Q}}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q_{w},w\pi_{w},w\lambda}(M_{Q}).

The result therefore follows from Theorem
4.17
.
∎

4.5
Bounds for residual Eisenstein series

We state some results on growth of Eisenstein series along orthonormal sums. For our purposes, we need these bounds on the right of the unitary axis, or rather on a neighborhood of this region. In
[
19
, Section 3]
, such estimates are proved in a neighborhood of the unitary axis and it turns out that the method used there extends to our region. Indeed, the key input needed is the regularity of the intertwining operators
M(w,\lambda)
, which is available to us by Proposition
4.11
. Therefore, in what follows we will only sketch the main modifications to adapt
[
19
, Section 3]
to our setting. The reader can also refer to
[
15
, Section 10]
where we produced an independent proof for our bounds at a time where
[
19
]
was not yet released, but we emphasize that the arguments we used there are essentially the same.

4.5.1
Bound for the global operator

We first bound the global intertwining operators.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
D
be a holomorphic differential operator on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
and every
C>0
, there exist
c_{J}
and
C_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-triple
(P,\pi,\tau)
with
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w}
we have

\left\lVert D(L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w})\right\rVert_{Q_{w},\mathrm{Pet}}\leq C_{J}\left(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2}+(\Lambda_{\pi}^{M_{P}})^{2}\right)^{k}\left\lVert\varphi_{w}\right\rVert_{P_{w},\mathrm{Pet}},

(4.33)

for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
, where
L_{\pi,w}
is the polynomial from Lemma
4.5
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.11
, we know that
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{w}
is regular. If
\lambda
belongs to some region
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
, the bound (
4.33
) follows from the factorization of
M(w,\lambda)
in (
4.17
) and from
[
19
, Proposition 3.4.1.1]
and
[
19
, Proposition 3.6.1.1]
. In general, one readily checks that the proof goes through in the larger region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
.
∎

4.5.2
Bounds for Eisenstein series

The key estimate we need is the following.

Proposition 4.23
.

There exists
k>0
such that for all
C>0
there exists
N>0
such that for all levels
J
, all
q>0
and all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
there exist
c_{J}>0
and a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q,X}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
such that for all
J
-triples
(P,\pi,\tau)
, all
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}
we have

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\frac{\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{J,q,X}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{q}},

(4.34)

in the region
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
.

Remark 4.24
.

As the proof shows, of Proposition
4.23
can be strengthened by asking that for all
q>0
there exists
m>0
such that for all
f\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
the bound (
4.34
) remains valid, the other quantifiers remaining the same.

Proof.

This is a modified version of
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
. By replacing
f
by
L(X)f
, we can assume that
X=0
. Let
m>0
and
g\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))
. We first show that (
4.34
) holds if we replace
f
by
g*f
in the LHS. The proof of
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
relies on an estimate of the scalar product of truncated Eisenstein series (
[
19
, Proposition 3.7.1.1]
) which itself uses bounds for the intertwining operators
M(w,\lambda)
. By Proposition
4.22
, these bounds are available to us in some region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
. More precisely, by reproducing
[
19
, Proposition 3.7.1.1]
we obtain a bound for the truncated scalar product

\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)L_{\pi,E}(\lambda^{\prime})}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)L_{\pi,0}(\lambda^{\prime})}\langle\Lambda^{T,\mathrm{Art}}E(\varphi,\lambda),\overline{E(\varphi^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle_{G,\mathrm{Pet}},

which we know is regular by the properties of the truncation operator from
[
55
, Theorem 3.9]
, the continuity of Eisenstein series from
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
, and Theorem
4.17
.

The proof of Proposition
4.23
is now the same as
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
up to two minor differences. The first is that
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
is written for
x\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
. However, for any
a\in A_{G}^{\infty}
one has

\displaystyle\left|E(ax,I_{P}(\lambda,g*f)\varphi,\lambda)\right|=

\displaystyle\left|\exp(\langle\lambda,H_{G}(a)\rangle)E(x,I_{P}(\lambda,g*f)\varphi,\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert a\right\rVert^{N}\left|E(x,I_{P}(\lambda,g*f)\varphi,\lambda)\right|

for some
N
as long as
\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C
. Moreover, for
x\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}
and
a\in A_{G}^{\infty}
we have
\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{G}^{N}\ll\left\lVert a\right\rVert^{N}\left\lVert ax\right\rVert_{G}^{N}
by (
3.3
), and
\left\lVert a\right\rVert\ll\left\lVert ax\right\rVert_{G}
by
[
11
, (2.4.1.6)]
. Therefore, for our purposes we may replace
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{N}
by the sup-norm of our regularized Eisenstein series times
\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{G}^{-N}
.

The second difference is that
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
assumes that
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{G,*}
while we work with
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. This comes into play in the computation of the kernel function
k_{g}
which has to be replaced by

k_{g}(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in G(F)}\int_{A_{G}^{\infty}}g(x^{-1}a\gamma y)\exp(\langle H_{G}(a),\lambda\rangle)da.

By
[
46
, Lemma I.2.4]
, the integral over
A_{G}^{\infty}
takes place inside a compact subset of
A_{G}^{\infty}
which only depends on the support of
g
. As
\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C
, we see that
[
19
, (3.9.1.2)]
holds, namely that there exist
c_{2}
and
N>0
such that

\sup_{y\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}}\left|k_{g}(x,y)\right|\leq c_{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{G}^{N},\quad x\in G(\mathbb{A})^{1}.

With these modifications in mind,
[
19
, Lemma 3.9.1.1]
yields for every
q>0
a bound

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,g*f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N}\leq\frac{\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{J,q}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{q}}.

(4.35)

But according to
[
1
, Corollary 4.2]
, for any level
J
and any
m\geq 1
large enough there exist
Z\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
,
g_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
g_{2}\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))
such that
Z
is invariant under
K_{\infty}
-conjugation,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are invariant under
K
-conjugation and are
J
-biinvariant, and
for any
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
we have:

f=g_{1}*f+g_{2}*(Z*f).

(4.36)

Proposition
4.23
is now a direct consequence of (
4.35
).
∎

From there, one can obtain bounds for Eisenstein series in orthonormal sums.

Theorem 4.25
.

There exists
k>0
such that for all
C>0
there exists
N>0
such that for all
q>0
all levels
J
and all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
there exist
c_{J}>0
and a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q,X}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
and all
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{J,q,X}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{2})^{q}},

(4.37)

in the region
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
. Moreover, we also have for
\Re(\lambda)\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*,+}}
and
\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert\leq C
, up to changing the semi-norm,

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{J,q,X}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}},

(4.38)

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.23
and of Lemma
4.26
below.
∎

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
J
be a level. For
q>0
large enough, for any continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
the sum

\sum_{P_{0}\subset P}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})^{J}}\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}\frac{\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert\dim(\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J})}{(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{q}}

is absolutely convergent and defines a continuous semi-norm on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
.

Proof.

We first bound

\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}\frac{\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{J,q}\dim(\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J})}{(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{q}}.

According to
[
44
, Equation (6.4)]
, there exist
A>0
and
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\dim(\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J})\leq A(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{k^{\prime}},\quad\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P}).

By
[
1
, 931]
, we know that
(\sum_{\tau}\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{J,q}^{2})^{1/2}
is a continuous semi-norm on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we are reduced to bounding
\sum_{\tau}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{-q}
for
q
large enough. But by the same argument as in
[
19
, Proof of Proposition 3.8.3.1]
there exists
B>0
such that for all
P
and all
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
we have

\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{-q}\leq B(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{2})^{-q/2}\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}(1+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{-q/2},

and
\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}(1+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{-q/2}<\infty
for
q
large enough.

We now need to show that for
q>0
large enough we have

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})^{J}}\frac{1}{(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{2})^{q}}<\infty.

(4.39)

This is
[
44
, line (6.17) p. 711 and below]
.
∎

4.5.3
Application to relative characters

We use Theorem
4.25
to prove convergence properties on relative characters defined by summing along the bases
\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)
.

Proposition 4.27
.

There exists
k>0
such that for all
C>0
and all levels
J
, there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
and all
F\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
the series

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\left\langle F,\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(\varphi,\lambda)\right\rangle_{G}\varphi

is absolutely convergent in
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
(embedded with the topology of some
\mathcal{T}_{-N}([G]_{P})^{J}
) and
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{J,\infty}
, for any
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
.

More precisely, for any
N>0
large enough, any
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and any
q>0
, there exist
N^{\prime}
and a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q,X}
on
\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}}([G])^{J}
such that for any
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\left|\left\langle F,\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(\varphi,\lambda)\right\rangle_{G}\right|\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\frac{\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{J,q,X}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{2})^{q}},

(4.40)

for
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
in our region and
F\in\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}}([G])^{J}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
, for any
N
large enough there exist
c>0
and
r>0
such that

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq c\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left\lVert R(\Delta^{j})\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}.

As in the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 3.8.2.3]
, we obtain some constants
c^{\prime}>0
and
d
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{\tau,J}
we have for all
1\leq j\leq r

\left\lVert R(\Delta^{j})\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}\leq c^{\prime}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{d}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}.

As in (
4.36
), there exist for any
m\geq 1
large enough some elements
g_{1},g_{2}\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
Z\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for any
F\in\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}}([G])^{J}
we have
F=g_{1}*F+g_{2}*(Z*F)
. We now conclude by Lemma
3.1
, Proposition
4.23
(more precisely Remark
4.24
) and by repeating the proof of Theorem
4.25
. The
L^{2}([G]_{P,0})^{J,\infty}
version follows from Lemma
3.4
.

∎

4.5.4
Bounds for individual Eisenstein series

We now generalize Proposition
4.23
for non necessarily
K_{\infty}
finite Eisenstein series.

Proposition 4.28
.

There exists
k>0
such that for all
C>0
there exists
N>0
such that for all levels
J
, all
q>0
and all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
there exist
c_{J}>0
and a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q,X}
on
\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
, all
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{J,q,X}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}},

(4.41)

in the region
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
.

Moreover, for all
N^{\prime}
large enough, there exist
d>0
and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N^{\prime},Y_{i}}.

(4.42)

in the region
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
, where
k
,
C
,
N
,
X
and
c_{J}
are as before.

Proof.

Let
k
,
N
and
c_{J}
be given by Proposition
4.23
. By
[
1
, 931]
, there exists
m_{0}\geq 1
such that for all
m\geq m_{0}
and all
f\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
we have
f=\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}f_{\tau}
in
C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
. By Proposition
4.23
, or more precisely by Remark
4.24
, for any
q>0
and any
m
large enough, there exists a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q,X}
on
C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))
such that for any
f\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
, we have

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(I_{P}(\lambda,f)\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq\sum_{\tau\in\widehat{K}_{\infty}}\frac{\left\lVert f_{\tau}\right\rVert_{J,q,X}\left\lVert e_{\tau}*\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\lambda_{\pi}^{2}+\lambda_{\tau}^{2})^{q}},

for any
\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}^{C}
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and because
(\sum_{\tau}\left\lVert e_{\tau}*\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}^{2})^{1/2}=\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
, we obtain (
4.41
).

We now take
g_{1},g_{2}\in C_{c}^{m}(G(\mathbb{A}))^{J}
and
Z\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that
g_{1}+g_{2}*Z
is the Dirac distribution at identity as in (
4.36
). Because
\left\lVert I_{P}(Z,\lambda)\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d^{\prime}}\sum_{i}\left\lVert R(Z_{i})\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}
for some
d^{\prime}>0
and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
, by plugging
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
in (
4.41
) we obtain constants
c
and
d
such that

\left\lVert\frac{L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)}{L_{\pi,0}(\lambda)}E(\varphi,\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq c(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r^{\prime}}\left\lVert R(Z_{i})\varphi\right\rVert_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}.

We conclude by Lemma
3.4
.

∎

As noted in
[
19
, Section 3.9.3]
, we may apply the methods used in §
4.5.2
to bound generalized Eisenstein series. We only state the analogue of Proposition
4.28
in this setting, although it is clear that the other results can be adapted as well. We keep the notation of §
4.4.3
.

Proposition 4.29
.

Let
P
and
Q
be standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. Let
w\in{}_{Q}W_{P}
. There exists
k>0
such that for all
C>0
there exist
N>0
such that for all levels
J>0
, all
X\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and all
N^{\prime}>0
large enough there exists
c_{J}>0
,
d>0
and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left\lVert L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)L_{w\pi_{w},E}^{Q}(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))E^{Q}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N,X}\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N^{\prime},Y_{i}}.

in the region
\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}^{C}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
and
p_{i}(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{i},k,c_{J}}
for all
i
. Here
L_{w\pi_{w},E}^{Q}
is defined in (
4.32
) and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{-N,X}
is a semi-norm on
\mathcal{T}_{-N}([G]_{Q})
.

Proof.

This is an adaptation of the proof of Proposition
4.28
(see also
[
19
, Theorem 3.9.3.1]
). It follows from the bounds for intertwining operators (Corollary
4.13
) and the regularity of generalized Eisenstein series (Proposition
4.29
).
∎

4.6
An extension of Langlands spectral decomposition theorem

We now use Theorem
4.25
to extend the Langlands spectral decomposition Theorem
3.7
to functions on
[G]
of rapid enough decay.

Proposition 4.30
.

There exists
N>0
such that for all level
J
of
G
and all
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{-N}([G])^{J}
we have

\langle F_{1},F_{2}\rangle_{G}=\sum_{P_{0}\subset P}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{P})\right|^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle F_{1},E(\varphi,\lambda)\rangle_{G}\langle E(\varphi,\lambda),F_{2}\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

(4.43)

where this expression is absolutely convergent.

Proof.

For all pairs
(P,\pi)
, the polynomial
L_{\pi,E}
of Theorem
4.25
is non-zero and bounded above on
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. By Theorem
4.25
, we see that there exists
N^{\prime}>0
such that both sides of (
4.43
) define separately continuous linear forms on
\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}}([G])^{J}
. Moreover, by Theorem
3.7
, it holds if
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are pseudo Eisenstein series. By Lemma
3.2
and Lemma
3.6
, the closure in
\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}}([G])
of the vector space spanned by these functions contains
\mathcal{T}_{-N^{\prime}-1}([G])
. We conclude that
N=N^{\prime}+1
works.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

There exists
N>0
such that for all level
J
of
G
and all
F\in\mathcal{T}_{-N}([G])^{J}
we have

F=\sum_{P_{0}\subset P}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{P})\right|^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle F,E(\varphi,\lambda)\rangle_{G}E(\varphi,\lambda)d\lambda.

5
Ichino–Yamana–Zydor regularized periods

For the rest of the paper,
G
is
\mathrm{GL}_{n}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
. We embed
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
by

g\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}g&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}.

(5.1)

Let
H\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{n}
be the diagonal subgroup in
G
. The goal is this section is to introduce the regularized period
\mathcal{P}
from
[
55
]
of the period integral along
[H]
, and to study its analytic properties.

For the rest of this text, we will use the following conventions. Let
P_{0}\subset G
be the product of the subgroups of upper triangular matrices in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
, and
T_{0}\subset G
to be the product of the standard diagonal tori. If
J
is a subgroup of
G
, we write
J=J_{n}\times J_{n+1}
. Similarly we have
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\mathfrak{a}_{0,n}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{0,n+1}
.

All the constructions done relatively to
H
will be decorated by a subscript
H
. In particular, a subgroup of
H
will typically be denoted
J_{H}
, and if
J
is a subgroup of
G
we set
J_{H}=J\cap H
. The pair
(T_{0,H},P_{0,H})
is standard in
H
. Set
\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}=\mathfrak{a}_{T_{0,H}}
. It embeds into
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. We will often identify the group
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
either with
H
, either with its two copies in
G
from the left and right coordinates, using the embedding (
5.1
) for the latter.

5.1
Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroups

We start by defining a subset
\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
of the set of semi-standard parabolic subgroups of
G
which appears in the definition of the regularized period
\mathcal{P}
.

5.1.1
Definition of the set of Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroups

We define
\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
the set of
Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroups
of
G
. This is the set of semi-standard parabolic subgroups
P=P_{n}\times P_{n+1}
of
G
such that
P_{n}
is standard and, with respect to the embedding (
5.1
),
P_{n}=P_{n+1}\cap\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. In particular, if
P\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
then
P_{H}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
H
isomorphic to
P_{n}
. Conversely, if
P_{H}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
H
, set

\mathcal{P}_{\RS}(P_{H})=\{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}\;|\;Q_{H}=P_{H}\}.

Then we have

\mathcal{F}_{\RS}=\bigsqcup_{P_{H}\subset H\;\mathrm{standard}}\mathcal{P}_{\RS}(P_{H}).

(5.2)

Recall that in Section
4
we have associated to any standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
or
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
a tuple
\underline{n}(P)
. Let
P_{H}
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
H
. Write
\underline{n}(P_{H})=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{m})
with
\sum n_{i}=n
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{n+1}(P_{H})
be the set of couples
(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1},i_{0})
where
P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
and
i_{0}
