is an integer such that one of the two following alternatives is satisfied:

1.

\underline{n}(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1})=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i_{0}-1},n_{i_{0}}+1,n_{i_{0}+1},\ldots,n_{m})
and
1\leq i_{0}\leq m
;

2.

\underline{n}(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1})=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i_{0}-1},1,n_{i_{0}},\ldots,n_{m})
and
1\leq i_{0}\leq m+1
.

In the first case, set
N=\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}}n_{i}
, and in the second set
N=\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}n_{i}
. Let
w(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1},i_{0})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
be the cycle

w(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1},i_{0})=(N+2\quad\ldots\quad n\quad n+1\quad N+1).

(5.3)

We identify it with an element in
W_{n+1}
the Weyl group of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
. The following is
[
16
, Corollary 4.2]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

We have a bijection

\mathcal{F}_{\RS}\simeq\left\{(P_{n+1}^{\mathrm{std}},i_{0})\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}\;\text{standard},\\
M_{P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}},\\
1\leq i_{0}\leq m.\end{array}\right\}=\bigsqcup_{P_{H}\subset H\;\mathrm{standard}}\mathcal{P}_{n}^{n+1}(P_{H}).

We set
\mathfrak{z}_{P}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}\cap\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}
. If
P\subset Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
, let
\mathfrak{z}_{P}^{Q}
be the orthogonal of
\mathfrak{z}_{Q}
in
\mathfrak{z}_{P}
. Note that this is consistent as
\mathfrak{z}_{G}=\{0\}
. If
T\in\mathfrak{z}_{P}
, let
T_{Q}
be its projection on
\mathfrak{z}_{Q}
and
T_{P}^{Q}
be its projection on
\mathfrak{z}_{P}^{Q}
, both with respect to
\mathfrak{z}_{P}=\mathfrak{z}_{P}^{Q}\oplus\mathfrak{z}_{Q}
. This applies in particular to
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}=\mathfrak{z}_{P_{0}}
and we simply write
T^{Q}
for
T_{P_{0}}^{Q}
. We have a notion of "sufficiently positive" for elements in
\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}
(see
[
16
, Section 4.3]
).

5.1.2
Standardization

Let
P\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
. Let
(P_{n+1}^{\mathrm{std}},i_{P})
be the inverse image of
P
under the isomorphism of Proposition
5.1
. Let
w_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}=w(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1},i_{P})
be the element defined in (
5.3
). We have

P_{n+1}=w_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}.P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}.

(5.4)

Set
P^{\mathrm{std}}=P_{n}\times P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}
, which is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
. This is the
standardization
of
P
. Write
(n_{i})=\underline{n}(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1})
. We may decompose the standard Levi factor
M_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}
of
P^{\mathrm{std}}
as

M_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}=M_{P,n}^{\mathrm{std}}\times M_{P,n+1}^{\mathrm{std}}=\left(\mathbf{M}_{P,+}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,n}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-}\right)\times\left(\mathbf{M}_{P,+}\times\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,n+1}\times\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,-}\right),

(5.5)

where

\mathbf{M}_{P,+}=\prod_{i<i_{P}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}},\quad\mathbf{M}_{P,-}\simeq\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,-}=\prod_{i>i_{P}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}},\quad\mathcal{M}_{P,n}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}-1},\quad\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,n+1}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}}.

(5.6)

We add a
std
on
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,-}
to emphasize that, although they are isomorphic, the groups
\mathbf{M}_{P,-}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,-}
are not identified by the embedding (
5.1
). We also set

\mathbf{M}_{P,+}^{\mathrm{std},2}=\mathbf{M}_{P,+}^{2},\quad\mathbf{M}_{P,-}^{\mathrm{std},2}=\mathbf{M}_{P,-}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-}^{\mathrm{std}},\quad\mathbf{M}_{P}^{\mathrm{std},2}=\mathbf{M}_{P,+}^{\mathrm{std},2}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-}^{\mathrm{std},2},\quad\mathcal{M}_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}=\mathcal{M}_{P,n}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,n+1}^{\mathrm{std}},

which all naturally embed into
G
. By composing with
w_{P}^{\mathrm{std}}
, we get a decomposition

M_{P}\simeq\left(\mathbf{M}_{P,+}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,n}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-}\right)\times\left(\mathbf{M}_{P,+}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,n+1}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-}\right).

(5.7)

The two copies of
\mathbf{M}_{P,-}
in (
5.7
) are now identified by the embedding (
5.1
). The groups
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{std}}_{P,n+1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{P,n+1}
are isomorphic but in general embedded in two different ways in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
. Set

\mathbf{M}_{P}=\mathbf{M}_{P,+}\times\mathbf{M}_{P,-},\quad\mathbf{M}_{P}^{2}=\mathbf{M}_{P}\times\mathbf{M}_{P},\quad\mathcal{M}_{P}=\mathcal{M}_{P,n}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,n+1}.

The restriction of the diagonal embedding
H\subset G
gives
\mathcal{M}_{P,H}\subset\mathcal{M}_{P}
and we have
\mathcal{M}_{P,H}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{P,n}
. Note that
M_{P_{H}}=M_{P,H}=\mathbf{M}_{P}\times\mathcal{M}_{P,H}
. The group
\mathcal{M}_{P}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}-1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}}
, and we can define embedding of
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{P}
as in (
5.1
). This is compatible with the inclusion
H\subset G
in the sense that
\mathcal{M}_{P,H}=\mathcal{M}_{P}\cap\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{P}}-1}
.

Set

\underline{\rho}_{P}=(\rho_{P}-2\rho_{P_{H}})_{|\mathfrak{z}_{P}}\in\mathfrak{z}_{P}^{*}.

In coordinates, we have
(\underline{\rho}_{P})_{i}=\frac{1}{2}
if
i<i_{P}
, and
(\underline{\rho}_{P})_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}
if
i>i_{P}
.

5.2
Regularized Rankin–Selberg periods à la Zydor

In this section, we fix
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
. We write
M^{\mathrm{std}}_{Q,n+1}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
. We recall the definition of the regularized period
\mathcal{P}^{Q}
from
[
55
]
.

5.2.1
Iwasawa decomposition and measures

Set

M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}=\left\{m\in M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})\;\middle|\;\left(H_{Q_{H}}(m)\right)_{Q}=0\right\},\quad Z_{Q}^{\infty}=A_{Q}^{\infty}\cap A_{Q_{H}}^{\infty}.

The restriction of
H_{Q_{H}}
to
Z_{Q}^{\infty}
is an isomorphism with image
\mathfrak{z}_{Q}
. This gives
Z_{Q}^{\infty}
a Haar measure. We have a direct product decomposition of commuting groups

M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})=Z_{Q}^{\infty}M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}.

(5.8)

By the Iwasawa decomposition, there is a unique Haar measure on
M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}
such that for all
f\in C_{c}(H(\mathbb{A}))
we have

\int_{H(\mathbb{A})}f(h)dh=\int_{K_{H}}\int_{N_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}}\int_{Z_{Q}^{\infty}}\exp(\langle-2\rho_{Q_{H}},H_{Q_{H}}(am)\rangle)f(nmak)dadmdndk.

(5.9)

We set

[M_{Q_{H}}]^{Q,1}=M_{Q_{H}}(F)\backslash M_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}

which is given the quotient by the counting measure. Note that we have

[M_{Q,H}]^{Q,1}\simeq\left(\prod_{i\neq i_{Q}}[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}]^{1}\right)\times[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{Q}}-1}]=[\mathbf{M}_{Q}]^{1}\times[\mathcal{M}_{Q,H}].

Then the measure
dh
coincides the product of the ones on each
[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}]^{1}
and on
[\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{Q}}-1}]
described in §
3.2.3
. Moreover, if
Q=G
we have
M_{G,H}(\mathbb{A})^{G,1}=H(\mathbb{A})
and
Z_{G}^{\infty}=\{1\}
.

5.2.2
Truncated periods

Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}
. In
[
55
]
, Zydor introduces a truncated operator
\Lambda^{T,Q}
defined on the space of locally integrable function on
Q(F)\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
. If
Q=G
we simply write
\Lambda^{T}
. Its main property is the following.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
T\in\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}
be sufficiently positive. Let
J
be a level of
G
. For any
N,N^{\prime}>0
there exists a finite family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements in
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for any smooth and right
J
-invariant function
\phi
on
[G]_{Q}
, the function
\Lambda^{T,Q}\phi
is a function on
[H]_{Q_{H}}
and we have

\sup_{m\in M_{Q,H}(\mathbb{A})^{Q,1}}\left\lVert m\right\rVert^{N}_{M_{Q,H}}\left|\Lambda^{T,Q}\phi(mk)\right|\leq\sum_{i\in I}\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert_{-N^{\prime},X_{i}}.

Proof.

This is
[
55
, Theorem 3.9]
. Note that the statement in ibid. is weaker, but our version can easily be extracted from the proof.
∎

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{T}([G])
. We have
\phi_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}([G]_{Q})
. For
T
sufficiently positive, we define the truncated period of
\phi
relative to
Q
to be

\mathcal{P}^{T,Q}(\phi)=\int_{K_{H}}\int_{[M_{Q,H}]^{Q,1}}\exp(-\langle 2\rho_{P_{H}},H_{P_{H}}(m)\rangle)\Lambda^{T,Q}\phi(mk)dmdk.

This is absolutely convergent by Lemma
3.1
and Theorem
5.2
. Note that if
Q=G
it reduces to

\mathcal{P}^{T}(\phi)=\int_{[H]}\Lambda^{T}\phi(h)dh.

5.2.3
Regularized periods

In
[
55
, Section 4.5]
, a subspace of
regular
automorphic forms
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)
is defined. More precisely, a
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}
if for every Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup
P\subset Q
and
h\in H(\mathbb{A})
, the exponents of the
Z_{P}^{\infty}
finite function
z\mapsto\varphi(zh)
belong to a dense open subset of
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. The space
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}
is stable under right-translations by
G(\mathbb{A})
. We then have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.3
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}
there exists a unique exponential polynomial on
\mathfrak{a}_{0,H}
that coincides with
T\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{T,Q}(\varphi)
for
T
sufficiently positive and whose purely polynomial part is constant. We denote it by
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi)
.

The number
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi)
is the
regularized period à la Zydor
along
Z_{Q}^{\infty}M_{Q_{H}}(F)N_{Q_{H}}(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
.

5.2.4
Parabolic descent

The period
\mathcal{P}^{Q}
can be described by parabolic descent. Note that
M_{Q,H}
identifies as
\mathbf{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q,H}\subset\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}
, where the map
\mathbf{M}_{Q}\subset\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}
is diagonal and
\mathcal{M}_{Q,H}\subset\mathcal{M}_{Q}
is the Rankin–Selberg embedding. Using
[
55
]
, one can associate to these two pairs of groups and subgroups two regularized periods
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
on some spaces of automorphic forms
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})^{\mathrm{reg}}
respectively. The following lemma is then proved in
[
16
, Lemma 4.12]
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}
. Set

\varphi_{M_{Q}}=\left(R(e_{K_{H}})\varphi\right)_{|M_{Q}(\mathbb{A}),-\rho_{Q}}.

Then we have

\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi)=\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}\left(m\in[\mathbf{M}^{2}_{Q}]\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}(R(m)\varphi_{M_{Q}})\right)=\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}\left(m\in[\mathcal{M}_{Q}]\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}(R(m)\varphi_{M_{Q}})\right),

(5.10)

where all the automorphic forms belong to the relevant
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{reg}}
spaces.

With the notation of Lemma
5.4
, we write

\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi)=(\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}})(\varphi_{M_{Q}}),

(5.11)

where the tensor product notation means that the regularized periods can be taken in any order as in (
5.10
). If
\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}^{2}
and if
\phi\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
, we may further decompose

\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}(\phi)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}^{2}}(\phi).

(5.12)

We call
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}^{2}}
the
regularized diagonal Arthur period on
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}
.

5.2.5
Regularized periods of Eisenstein series

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
, let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Let
w\in{}_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{P}
. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{w},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position, set

\mathcal{P}^{T,Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)=\mathcal{P}^{T,Q}(E^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1}\cdot,M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w\lambda)),

(5.13)

Remark 5.5
.

The element
w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}
was defined in (
5.4
). We prefer to make it appear in (
5.13
) to deal with the standard parabolic subgroup
Q^{\mathrm{std}}
rather than
Q
.

The truncated period is well defined for
\lambda
in general position by
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
(which ensures that generalized Eisenstein series are of uniform moderate growth), and if
P_{\pi}\not\subset P_{w}
it is zero. In
[
16
, Lemma 4.15]
, we show that the generalized Eisenstein series
E^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1}\cdot,M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w\lambda)
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{Q}(G)^{\mathrm{reg}}
for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position. We can therefore define

\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)=\mathcal{P}^{Q}(E^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1}\cdot,M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w\lambda)).

(5.14)

The following property follows directly from the definition of regularized periods in
[
55
]
.

Proposition 5.6
.

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position and
T
sufficiently positive we have

\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}\\
R\subset Q\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{R}^{Q}\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}w}\mathcal{P}^{T,R}(\varphi,\lambda,w^{\prime})\cdot\frac{\exp(\langle w_{R}^{\mathrm{std}}w^{\prime}(\lambda+\nu_{w^{\prime}})+\underline{\rho}_{R},T_{R}^{Q}\rangle)}{\hat{\theta}_{R}^{Q}(w_{R}^{\mathrm{std}}w^{\prime}(\lambda+\nu_{w^{\prime}})+\underline{\rho}_{R})},

(5.15)

where
\varepsilon_{R}^{Q}
is a sign,
\hat{\theta}_{R}^{Q}
is the product of affine linear forms defined in
[
16
, Section 4.2]
, and we understand that the summands are zero unless
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w^{\prime}}
(whether or not the denominator is identically zero). A similar expression holds for
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
.

5.3
Regularized periods as Zeta integrals and inner-products

In this section, we recall the results of
[
16
]
which compute the regularized periods
\mathcal{P}^{Q}
in terms of global Zeta integrals and Petersson inner products. They deeply rely on
[
29
]
, and will have important analytic consequences in §
5.4
.

5.3.1
Rankin–Selberg periods and Zeta functions

Let
\psi
be a generic character of
[N_{0}]
which is trivial on
N_{0,H}(\mathbb{A})
. For any automorphic form
\Phi\in\mathcal{A}(G)
, we may consider the global Whittaker function

W^{\psi}(g,\Phi)=\int_{[N_{0}]}\Phi(ng)\overline{\psi}(n)dn,\quad g\in[G].

(5.16)

If
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
, for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position we can consider the global Zeta integral

Z_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda):=\int_{N_{0,H}(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}W^{\psi}(h,E(\varphi,\lambda))dh,\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G).

(5.17)

By
[
11
, Lemma 7.1.1.1]
, this integral is absolutely convergent for
\Re(\lambda)
in some open subset of
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, and extends to a meromorphic function in
\lambda
by
[
29
, Corollary 5.4]
and
[
29
, Equation (4.2)]
. Note that because residual representations are not generic, it is zero as soon as
\pi
is not cuspidal.

We now assume that
\pi
is cuspidal. Write
M_{P}=\prod_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{n,i}}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{n+1}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{n+1,i}}
and
\pi=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\pi_{n,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{n+1}}\pi_{n+1,i}
accordingly. Consider the products of completed Rankin–Selberg
L
functions

b(\lambda,\pi)=\prod_{i<j}L(1+\lambda_{n,i}-\lambda_{n,j},\pi_{n,i}\times\pi_{n,j}^{\vee})\prod_{i<j}L(1+\lambda_{n+1,i}-\lambda_{n+1,j},\pi_{n+1,i}\times\pi_{n+1,j}^{\vee}).

(5.18)

and

L\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2},\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n+1}\right):=\prod_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{n+1}}L(1/2+\lambda_{n,i}+\lambda_{n+1,j},\pi_{n,i}\times\pi_{n+1,j}).

(5.19)

Then it is shown in
[
29
, Corollary 5.7]
that if
\varphi=\otimes_{v}\varphi_{v}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
is factorizable, there exists a finite set
\mathtt{S}
of places of
F
such that

Z_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\frac{L(\lambda+\frac{1}{2},\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n+1})}{b(\lambda,\pi)}\times\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}Z_{\pi_{v}}^{\natural}(\varphi_{v},\lambda),

(5.20)

where the
Z_{\pi_{v}}^{\natural}
are local Zeta integrals normalized by the local version of the quotient of
L
-functions. Moreover, we know by
[
29
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
16
, Proposition 4.20]
that
\mathcal{P}
computes the global Zeta integral.

Proposition 5.7
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position, we have
\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)=Z_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
.

Set

L_{\pi,Z}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\\
\pi_{n,i}\simeq\pi_{n+1,j}^{\vee}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda_{n,i}+\lambda_{n+1,j}\pm\frac{1}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{n,i}\simeq\pi_{n,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda_{n,i}-\lambda_{n,j})^{-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{n+1,i}\simeq\pi_{n+1,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda_{n+1,i}-\lambda_{n+1,j})^{-1}.

(5.21)

Corollary 5.8
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
of
G
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
with
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})
and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the map

\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\mapsto L_{\pi,Z}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)

is regular on
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
.

Proof.

By
[
16
, Lemma 4.18]
, for every place
v
the local factor
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\mapsto Z_{\pi_{v}}^{\natural}(\varphi_{v},\lambda)
is a meromorphic function which is regular for
\Re(\lambda)\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*,+}}
.
By Theorem
4.1
the factorization of the Zeta function from (
5.20
), we know that
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)
has simple zeros along
\lambda_{n,i}-\lambda_{n,j}=0
and
\lambda_{n+1,i}-\lambda_{n+1,j}=0
if
\pi_{n,i}\simeq\pi_{n,j}
and
\pi_{n+1,i}\simeq\pi_{n+1,j}
respectively. Indeed, note that these zeros can neither be compensated by poles of
L(\lambda+\frac{1}{2},\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n+1})
nor of
\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}Z_{\pi_{v}}^{\natural}
. Set

L_{\pi,Z}^{\mathrm{num}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\\
\pi_{n,i}\simeq\pi_{n+1,j}^{\vee}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda_{n,i}+\lambda_{n+1,j}\pm\frac{1}{2}\right).

Then it is enough to show that
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,Z}^{\mathrm{num}}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)
is regular on
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. We know that it is regular at least on
\Re(\lambda)\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*,+}}
.

To obtain regularity on the bigger region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
, we use the localization of the poles of the cuspidal Eisenstein series in Theorem
4.17
and of the generalized cuspidal Eisenstein series in Proposition
4.20
(see also Remark
4.21
). By Proposition
5.6
, we conclude that there exist
k
and
c_{J}
as in the statement of Corollary
5.8
such that

\lambda\mapsto\left(\prod_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}}\prod_{w\in W(P;Q^{\mathrm{std}})}\hat{\theta}_{Q}(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}w\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{Q})\right)L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)

is regular on
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
, where
L_{\pi,E}
is defined in (
4.29
). Because the intersection of any hyperplane cut out by
(\prod\hat{\theta}_{Q}(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}w\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{Q}))L_{\pi,E}(\lambda)/L_{\pi,Z}^{\mathrm{num}}(\lambda)
with the region
\Re(\lambda)\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*,+}}
is of codimension
1
, we conclude that
L_{\pi,Z}^{\mathrm{num}}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)
must be regular on
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
.
∎

5.3.2
The diagonal Arthur period

We now investigate the diagonal Arthur period
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}
for
k\geq 1
of (
5.12
). This is the regularized period of
[
55
]
associated to the diagonal subgroup
\mathrm{GL}_{k}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}
. It is well defined for all automorphic forms in some subspace
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P}^{2})
. Let
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2})
. We have the following alternative.

1.

If
P=\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}
, then
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
and

\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}(\varphi)=\langle\varphi_{1},\overline{\varphi_{2}}\rangle_{\mathrm{GL}_{k},\mathrm{Pet}}.

2.

If
P\neq\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}
, then for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position we have
E(\varphi,\lambda)\in\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
and

\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}(E(\varphi,\lambda))=0.

Proof.

The first point follows by
[
55
, Theorem 4.6]
. For the second, let
P\neq\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}
. It is shown in
[
19
, Lemma 3.1.5.1]
that for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position we have
E(\varphi,\lambda)\in\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2})^{\mathrm{reg}}
and the map
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}(E(\varphi,\lambda))
is meromorphic. Write
P=P_{1}\times P_{2}
,
\pi=\pi_{1}\boxtimes\pi_{2}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
. By
[
55
, Theorem 4.1]
, the regularized period yields for
\lambda
in general position a
\mathrm{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{A})^{1}
-invariant pairing

(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{1},\pi_{1},\lambda_{1}}(\mathrm{GL}_{k})\times\mathcal{A}_{P_{2},\pi_{2},\lambda_{2}}(\mathrm{GL}_{k})\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}(E(\varphi,\lambda))\in\mathbb{C}.

Such a pairing must be zero for
\lambda
in general position (globally and locally) by Bernstein’s principle (
[
30
, 208]
). It follows that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{GL}_{k}^{2}}(E(\varphi,\lambda))
is identically zero.
∎

5.4
Analytic properties of regularized periods

In this section, we fix
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{RS}
. We take
P
a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. By combining Lemma
5.4
, Proposition
5.7
and Proposition
5.9
, we obtain a complete description of the periods
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
and of their analytic properties on some open set of
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.

5.4.1
Poles of regularized periods

We first assume that
P\subset Q^{\mathrm{std}}
. We have a decomposition
\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
corresponding to the decomposition of the Levi factor
M_{P}
. We further decompose
\pi_{\mathbf{P}}=\pi_{\mathbf{P},n}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P},n+1}
. For every
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, denote by
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
the restriction of
\lambda
to
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover, recall that
\mathbf{M}_{Q,+}^{\mathrm{std},2}
is a standard Levi subgroup of some
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}^{2}
(embedded in the "upper-left corner" of
G
) and that
\mathbf{M}_{Q,-}^{\mathrm{std},2}
is a standard Levi subgroup of some
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}^{2}
(embedded in the "lower-right" corner of
G
). Set
\mathbf{P}=(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})^{2}\cap P
. This is a standard parabolic subgroup of
(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})^{2}
with Levi factor
\mathbf{M}_{P}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}
is a Levi subgroup of
(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})^{2}
that contains
\mathbf{M}_{P}
. Let
R
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi factor
(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q,n}^{\mathrm{std}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q,n+1}^{\mathrm{std}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Set

\varphi_{M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}=\left(R(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1})R(e_{K_{H}})\varphi\right)_{|M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}(\mathbb{A}),-\rho_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}},\quad\varphi_{M_{R}}=\left(R(w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1})R(e_{K_{H}})\varphi\right)_{|M_{R}(\mathbb{A}),-\rho_{R}}.

Then
\varphi_{M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{P},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}}(\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}})
and
\varphi_{M_{R}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{P},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}}((\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}})^{2})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}})
. Because
\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{r+1}
for some
r
, we can consider the Zeta function
Z_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}(\cdot,\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})
which is defined on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}})
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. We have the following alternative:

•

If
\mathbf{M}_{P}=\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{{}^{\mathrm{std}},2}
,
\pi_{\mathbf{P},n}\simeq\pi_{\mathbf{P},n+1}^{\vee}
and if
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
is cuspidal, then for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
we have

\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)=\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{P,H},\mathrm{Pet}}\otimes Z_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}(\cdot,\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\right)(\varphi_{M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}})=\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P}_{H},\mathrm{Pet}}\otimes Z_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}(\cdot,\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\right)(\varphi_{M_{R}}),

(5.22)

where
\mathbf{P}\simeq\mathbf{P}_{H}^{2}
and the tensor product notation is used as in (
5.11
).

•

Otherwise, for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the map
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)
is zero.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of parabolic descent (Lemma
5.4
) and the description of
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
in Proposition
5.7
and of
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}
in Proposition
5.9
and (
5.12
). The only thing that we have to prove is the final equality in (
5.22
). Note that
\mathbf{K}=w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},-1}K_{H}w_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}\cap(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}(\mathbb{A}))
is a good maximal compact subgroup of this group. We equip it with the probability Haar measure. Write
\varphi=\varphi_{n}\otimes\varphi_{n+1}
. Then by the Iwasawa decomposition we have

\langle\varphi_{M_{R},n},\overline{\varphi}_{M_{R},n+1}\rangle_{\mathbf{P}_{H},\mathrm{Pet}}=\int_{\mathbf{K}}\langle R(k)\varphi_{M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}},n},\overline{R(k)\varphi_{M_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}},n+1}}\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{P,H},\mathrm{Pet}}dk.

The second equality follows.
∎

We now lift the hypothesis that
P\subset Q^{\mathrm{std}}
and take
w\in{}_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{P}
. We study the regularity of the period
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
for
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. By Lemma
4.3
, there exist a discrete automorphic representation
\pi_{w}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P_{w}})
and an unramified character
\nu_{w}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{w}}^{*}
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
we have
\varphi_{P_{w}}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{w},\pi_{w},\nu_{w}}(G)
. We can apply the above notation to this representation, for example by writing
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}
for the restriction of any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{Q}_{w},\mathbb{C}}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{w}=Q_{w}\cap\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}
.

Recall that we have defined in §
4.3.1
a polynomial
L_{\pi,w}
which controls the poles of
M(w,\lambda)
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w)
(see (
3.16
)). We have the following description of the poles of
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
.

Proposition 5.11
.

There exists
k
such that for every level
J
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the map

\lambda\mapsto L_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},Z}\left((w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\right)L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)

(5.23)

is regular in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w),\;(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}\right\}.

(5.24)

Proof.

By Corollary
4.13
, we know that
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)M(w,\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}}
is regular in the region (
5.24
). By Theorem
5.2
and
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
, we are reduced to the case
w=1
and
P=P_{w}
.

We now want to prove that
\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},Z}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda)
is regular in the region
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
. We can assume that we are in the first case of Proposition
5.10
. By parabolic descent (Lemma
5.4
), we need to study the regularity of

(\lambda_{\mathbf{P}},\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\times\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}^{2}_{Q}}\left(m\in\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}(\mathbb{A})\mapsto L_{\pi,Z}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}(R(m)\varphi_{M_{Q}},\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}+\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\right).

(5.25)

By Proposition
5.9
, because
\mathbf{M}_{P}=\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}
the period
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}
is just a Petersson inner-product so that (
5.25
) is constant in
\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}
. It is therefore enough to show that it is holomorphic in the variable
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
for
\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}
fixed by Hartogs’ theorem. By Corollary
5.8
, for every
m
the map
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto L_{\pi,Z}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}(R(m)\varphi_{M_{Q}},\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}+\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})
is regular in the desired region. Moreover, by Corollary
5.8
below (which for this purpose is independent from this argument), one can easily upgrade this to a regular map
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\mapsto L_{\pi,Z}(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}}(R(\cdot)\varphi_{M_{Q}},\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}+\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})
valued in
\mathcal{T}([\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}])
(see
[
11
, Section A.0.3]
for this notion). Because
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{2}}
is a sum of truncated periods by Proposition
5.6
, we infer that (
5.25
) is also regular in the region
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
by Theorem
5.2
. This concludes.
∎

5.4.2
Bounds for regularized periods

We now bound
\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
.

Proposition 5.12
.

There exists
k>0
such that for any level
J
and
C>0
there exist
c_{J}>0
,
d>0
,
N>0
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for all
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left|L_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},Z}\left((w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\right)L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)\right|\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X_{i}},

(5.26)

in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;\middle|\;\lambda+\nu_{w}\in\mathcal{R}^{C}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w),\;(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}\right\}.

(5.27)

Proof.

Let
(P,\pi)
be a
J
-pair. We can assume that the discrete automorphic representation
w\pi_{w}
of
M_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}
satisfies the first condition of Proposition
5.10
, as otherwise the period is zero. This implies that
Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}\cap\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}=\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}
.

Let
R\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
such that
R\subset Q
, and let
w^{\prime}\in{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}w
with
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w^{\prime}}
. The set
{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}
decomposes as
{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{\mathbf{M}_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}^{\mathrm{std},2}}\times{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std}}}
, so that we can write
w^{\prime}=(w^{\prime}_{\mathbf{M}},w^{\prime}_{\mathcal{M}})w
under this decomposition. We have
w^{\prime}_{\mathbf{M}}=1
and moreover
\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}\cap R^{\mathrm{std}}=\mathbf{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{std},2}\cap R^{\mathrm{std}}_{w^{\prime}}
,
P_{w}=P_{w^{\prime}}
,
\pi_{w}=\pi_{w^{\prime}}
and
\nu_{w}=\nu_{w^{\prime}}
. We now want to use Proposition
4.29
to bound the generalized Eisenstein series
E^{R^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w^{\prime},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w^{\prime}\lambda)
. Recall that in §
4.4.3
we have defined some projectors
p_{i}
that appear in the statement of Proposition
4.29
. Then it is easily checked by the previous discussion and because
(w^{\prime}\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}}
is cuspidal that the condition
(w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
implies
p_{i}(w^{\prime}(\lambda+\nu_{w^{\prime}}))\in\mathcal{R}_{(w^{\prime}\pi_{w})_{i},k,c_{J}}
for all
i
(see Remark
4.21
). By Proposition
4.29
, there exists a finite product of linear forms
L_{w^{\prime}}(\lambda)
(which can be chosen independently of
\pi
) and
N^{\prime}>0
such that for all
Y\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and all
N>0
large enough we have
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
and
d>0
such that

\left\lVert L_{w^{\prime}}(\lambda)E^{R^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w^{\prime},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w^{\prime}\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N^{\prime},Y}\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X_{i}},

(5.28)

for
\lambda
in the region (
5.27
). This estimate is uniform in
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
and
(P,\pi)
.

Let us denote by
L(\lambda)
the product of all the
L_{w^{\prime}}
with all the
\hat{\theta}_{R}^{Q}(w_{R}^{\mathrm{std}}w^{\prime}(\lambda+\nu_{w^{\prime}})+\underline{\rho}_{R})
. By Theorem
5.2
and Proposition
5.6
, we see that for any
N^{\prime}>0
large enough we have
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for all
J
-pairs
(P,\pi)
, all
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
and all
\lambda
in (
5.27
) we have

\left|L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)\right|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}\\
R\subset Q\end{subarray}}\sum_{w^{\prime}\in{}_{R^{\mathrm{std}}}W_{Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{w}}^{Q^{\mathrm{std}}}w}\sum_{i=1}^{r^{\prime}}\left\lVert L(\lambda)E^{R^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w^{\prime},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{w}},w^{\prime}\lambda)\right\rVert_{-N^{\prime},Y_{i}}.

By (
5.28
), we conclude that (
5.26
) holds for
L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
. But by Proposition
5.11
, we know that
\lambda\mapsto L_{(w\pi_{w})_{\mathcal{P}},Z}\left((w(\lambda+\nu_{w}))_{\mathcal{P}}\right)L_{\pi,w}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w)
is holomorphic in our region. It remains to use
[
19
, Lemma 2.4.2.1]
to eliminate the superfluous linear forms.
∎

5.5
Residues of Rankin–Selberg periods

In this section, we recall the results of
[
16
]
on residues of the regularized period
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)
.

5.5.1
A naive notion of residues

Let
m\geq 1
, let
f
be a meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Let
\Lambda
be a non-zero affine linear form on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Write
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
for the affine hyperplane
\{v\in\mathbb{C}^{m}\;|\;\Lambda(v)=0\}
. The map
v\mapsto\Lambda(v)f(v)
is a meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Assume that
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
is not contained in its polar divisor (i.e.
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
is at most a simple polar divisor of
f
). Then its restriction to
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
is a meromorphic function on
\mathcal{H}
, and we set

\underset{\Lambda}{\mathrm{Res}}f:=\left(\Lambda f\right)_{|\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}}.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{r}
be a family of affine linear forms such that the underlying family of linear forms is linearly disjoint. We consider the iterated residue

\underset{\Lambda_{r}\leftarrow\Lambda_{1}}{\mathrm{Res}}f:=\underset{\Lambda_{r}}{\mathrm{Res}}\ldots\underset{\Lambda_{1}}{\mathrm{Res}}f,

provided each residue is defined in the above sense. This is a meromorphic function on
\mathcal{H}:=\bigcap\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda_{i}}
. Note that the iterated residue a priori depends on the order of the affine linear forms.

5.5.2
Residues as regularized periods

Let
P
be a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{P})
. Write the Levi factor
M_{P}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{n,i}}\right)\times\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m_{n+1}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{n+1,j}}\right)
and
\pi=\boxtimes\pi_{n,i}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1,j}
accordingly. The next proposition summarizes the results of
[
16
, Proposition 4.23]
and
[
16
, Lemma 5.1]
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
1\leq i_{+,1},\ldots,i_{+,m_{+}}\leq m_{n}
,
1\leq i_{-,1},\ldots,i_{-,m_{-}}\leq m_{n}
,
1\leq j_{+,1},\ldots,j_{+,m_{+}}\leq m_{n+1}
and
1\leq j_{-,1},\ldots,j_{-,m_{-}}\leq m_{n+1}
. Assume that

•

The indices
i_{+,1},\ldots,i_{+,m_{+}},i_{-,1},\ldots,i_{-,m_{-}}
are distinct;

•

The indices
j_{+,1},\ldots,j_{+,m_{+}},j_{-,1},\ldots,j_{-,m_{-}}
are distinct;

•

For every
l
we have
\pi_{n,i_{+,l}}=\pi_{n+1,j_{+,l}}^{\vee}
and
\pi_{n,i_{-,l}}=\pi_{n+1,j_{-,l}}^{\vee}
.

For
l
in the suitable range, consider the affine linear forms on
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
defined by
\Lambda_{+,l}(\lambda)=\lambda_{n,i_{+,l}}+\lambda_{n+1,j_{+,l}}+\frac{1}{2}
, and
\Lambda_{-,l}(\lambda)=\lambda_{n,i_{-,l}}+\lambda_{n+1,j_{-,l}}-\frac{1}{2}
. Let
Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
with standard Levi factor

\prod_{l=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{+,l}}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{k+1}\times\prod_{l=m_{-}}^{1}\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{-,l}}},

(5.29)

and let
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{\RS}
be the element corresponding to
(Q^{\mathrm{std}}_{n+1},m_{+}+1)
under the bijection of Proposition
5.1
. Let
w\in W(P;Q^{\mathrm{std}})
be the only element such that
w_{n}(i_{+,l})=l
,
w_{n}(i_{-,l})=m_{n}-l+1
,
w_{n+1}(j_{+,l})=l
and
w_{n+1}(j_{-,l})=m_{n+1}-l+1
. Then for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
we have

\underset{\Lambda_{m_{-},-}\leftarrow\Lambda_{-,1}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{+,m_{+}}\leftarrow\Lambda_{+,1}}{\mathrm{Res}}\mathcal{P}(\varphi,\lambda)=(-1)^{m_{+}}\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\varphi,\lambda,w).

(5.30)

Moreover, the iterated residue may be taken in any order in the set
\{\Lambda_{1,\pm},\ldots,\Lambda_{m_{\pm},\pm}\}
.

6
Extension of the Rankin–Selberg period

6.1
An extension of
[
16
, Section 5]

In this section, we define a set of
relevant inducing data

\Pi_{H}
. They parametrize inductions
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
on which we can build the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
from
[
16
]
. In fact, for the purpose of the fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period, we need to work with slightly more general inductions than in
[
16
]
.

6.1.1
Relevant inducing data

In the rest of the text, we will use the following convention. If
\underline{n}(1),\ldots,\underline{n}(k)
are tuples of integers in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{1}},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{k}}
respectively, we write
\underline{n}(i,j)
for their elements with
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
. We also write
(\underline{n}(1),\ldots,\underline{n}(k))
for the tuple in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{k}}
obtained by concatenating them. Recall that any standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
is determined by the tuple of integers
\underline{n}(P)
defined in Section
4
. We naturally extend this notion to standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. We also allow entries of
\underline{n}(P)
to be zero.

Let
k\geq 1
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{k})
. Write
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma^{\boxtimes d}
with
\sigma\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r})
so that
\pi=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d)
. By analogy with the local notion of derivatives introduced in
[
52
]
, we define the automorphic derivative of
\pi
to be

\pi^{-}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d-1)\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(\mathrm{GL}_{r(d-1)}).

Note that if
\pi
is cuspidal, i.e. if
d=1
, the representation
\pi^{-}
is the trivial representation of the trivial group.

We now define the set
\Pi_{H}
of relevant inducing data. It is the set of triples
(I,P,\pi)
such that the following conditions hold.

•

I\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{4}
with
I=(n_{+},n_{1},n_{2},n_{-})
.

•

We have tuples of integers
\underline{n}(+)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{+}}
,
\underline{n}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{1}}
,
\underline{n}^{\prime}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{1}}
,
\underline{n}(2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{2}}
,
\underline{n}^{\prime}(2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{2}}
and
\underline{n}(-)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{-}}
such that
\sum\underline{n}(+,i)=n_{+}
,
\sum\underline{n}(1,i)=n_{1}
,
\sum\underline{n}(2,i)=n_{2}
and
\sum\underline{n}(-,i)=n_{-}
and
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

\underline{n}(P)=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

(6.1)

•

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
is a discrete automorphic representation (with trivial central character on
A_{P}^{\infty}
) such that, with respect to (
6.1
),
\pi=\pi_{n}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1}
decomposes as

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i},

(6.2)

\displaystyle\pi_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i}^{\vee}.

(6.3)

In this situation, for
1\leq i\leq m_{1}
we can write for
\pi_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i},\underline{d}(1,i))
, for some representation
\sigma_{1,i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)})
and
\underline{d}(1,i)
and
\underline{r}(1,i)
some positive integers. Similarly, for
1\leq i\leq m_{2}
we have
\pi_{2,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{2,i},\underline{d}(2,i))
. In particular, we have for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
the formulae
\underline{n}(i,j)=\underline{d}(i,j)\underline{r}(i,j)
and
\underline{n}^{\prime}(i,j)=(\underline{d}(i,j)-1)\underline{r}(i,j)
.

Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. With the choices of coordinates made in Section
4
,

\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
is realized as a subspace

\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\subset\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{-}}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{m_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{-}}\right).

(6.4)

A similar decomposition holds for
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. If
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, we write

\lambda=\left((\lambda(+)_{n},\lambda(1)_{n},\lambda(2)_{n},\lambda(-)_{n}),(\lambda(+)_{n+1},\lambda(1)_{n+1},\lambda(2)_{n+1},\lambda(-)_{n+1})\right)

(6.5)

according to this decomposition. Note that we have
\lambda(2)_{n,i}=0
if
\underline{d}(2,i)=1
, and
\lambda(1)_{n+1,i}=0
if
\underline{d}(1,i)=1
. Let
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
be the element of
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
defined as

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}:=\left(\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{4},\ldots,\frac{1}{4}}_{m_{+}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{1}+m_{2}},\underbrace{-\frac{1}{4},\ldots,-\frac{1}{4}}_{m_{-}}\right),\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{4},\ldots,\frac{1}{4}}_{m_{+}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{1}+m_{2}},\underbrace{-\frac{1}{4},\ldots,-\frac{1}{4}}_{m_{-}}\right)\right)\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}.

(6.6)

We define the anti-diagonal subspace
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
to be

\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda(+)_{n}=-\lambda(+)_{n+1},&\\
\lambda(1)_{n,i}=-\lambda(1)_{n+1,i},&1\leq i\leq m_{1},\;\text{if }\underline{d}(1,i)\neq 1,\\
\lambda(2)_{n,i}=-\lambda(2)_{n+1,i},&1\leq i\leq m_{2},\;\text{if }\underline{d}(2,i)\neq 1,\\
\lambda(-)_{n}=-\lambda(-)_{n+1},&\end{array}\right\}.

(6.7)

We have an isomorphism

\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}\mapsto\left(\lambda(+),\lambda(1),\lambda(2),\lambda(-)\right):=\left(\lambda(+)_{n},\lambda(1)_{n},\lambda(2)_{n+1},\lambda(-)_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{-}}

(6.8)

We also have an anti-diagonal subspace
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
defined by the same equations. Note that
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
is exactly the subspace of
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
such that
(P,\pi_{\lambda})\in\Pi_{H}
if we lift the requirement that the central character is trivial on
A_{P}^{\infty}
, and ask that it is unitary instead.

Remark 6.1
.

The set of relevant inducing pairs
\Pi_{H}
introduced in
[
16
, Section 5.1]
corresponds to the special case where
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
. We limited ourselves to this setting in
[
16
]
as our goal there was to prove the non-tempered Gan–Gross–Prasad conjecture from
[
24
]
.

6.1.2
The regularized Rankin–Selberg period

Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. Let
\sigma_{\pi}
and
\nu_{\pi}
be respectively the cuspidal automorphic representation of
M_{P_{\pi}}
and the element of
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}}^{*}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
is obtained by taking residues of Eisenstein series on the induction
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},-\nu_{\pi}}(G)
(see §
4.1.2
). For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position, we have the global Zeta function
Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\cdot,\lambda)
from §
5.3.1
. By Proposition
5.7
, we know that it is equal to the regularized period
\mathcal{P}(\cdot,\lambda)
. Moreover, it has the Euler product expansion from (
5.20
) which involves a global
L
-factor
L\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2},\sigma_{\pi,n}\times\sigma_{\pi,n+1}\right)
. We can identify
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\nu_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
as an affine subspace of
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. By Theorem
4.1
, we know that all the singularities of
Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}
that contain this subspace are affine hyperplanes coming from singularities of
L\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2},\sigma_{\pi,n}\times\sigma_{\pi,n+1}\right)
. We can consider the residue
\mathrm{Res}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\cdot,\lambda)
defined by multiplying by all the corresponding affine linear forms (normalized as in Proposition
5.13
) and evaluating on
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\nu_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. This a priori depends on the chosen order. We now state a slight generalization of
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
.

Theorem 6.2
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
, the residue
\mathrm{Res}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi,\lambda)
is well-defined and independent on the order. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position, the linear form
\phi\mapsto\mathrm{Res}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi,\mu)
factors through the quotient
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},\mu}(G)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
, where
\mu=\lambda-\nu_{\pi}
.

The linear form thus obtained on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
will be denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\cdot,\lambda)
. It is
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant.

Proof.

That the residue is independent of the order of affine linear forms follows from the same argument as
[
16
, Lemma 5.1]
. If
n_{+}=n_{-}=0
, the rest of Theorem
6.2
is
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
. For the general case, we proceed as follows. We have a natural identification of
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(+,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(1,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(2,i)-1}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(-,i)}\right)\times\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(+,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(1,i)-1}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(2,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(-,i)}\right).

We write the coordinates of any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with respect to these identifications. More precisely, if
\lambda=(\lambda_{n},\lambda_{n+1})\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we write
\lambda_{n}
as

(\lambda(+,1)_{n},\ldots,\lambda(+,m_{+})_{n},\lambda(1,1)_{n},\ldots,\lambda(1,m_{1})_{n},\lambda(2,1)_{n},\ldots,\lambda(2,m_{2})_{n},\lambda(-,1)_{n},\ldots,\lambda(-,m_{-})_{n})

where for example,
\lambda(+,1)_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(+,1)}
with coordinates
\lambda(+,1)_{n,1},\ldots,\lambda(+,1)_{n,\underline{d}(+,1)}
. We now consider the set
\mathcal{L}
of affine linear forms on
\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
defined as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda(+,i,j)(\lambda)=-(\lambda(+,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(+,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(+,i)-j+1}+1/2),&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\leq i\leq m_{+},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(+,i),\end{array}\right.\\
\Lambda(-,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(-,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(-,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(-,i)-j+1}-1/2,&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\leq i\leq m_{-},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(-,i).\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

We also have the set
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of linear forms defined by the following equations.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda^{\prime}(+,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(+,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(+,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(+,i)-j}-1/2,&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\leq i\leq m_{+},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(+,i)-1,\end{array}\right.\\
\Lambda^{\prime}(-,i,j)(\lambda)=-(\lambda(-,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(-,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(-,i)-j+2}+1/2),&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1\leq i\leq m_{-},\\
2\leq j\leq\underline{d}(-,i).\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

All these linear forms direct singular affine hyperplanes of
Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}
. If we write
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for their respective intersections, then
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\nu_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\subset\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. We now order the set
\mathcal{L}
by

\left(\xleftarrow[i=m_{-}]{1}\xleftarrow[j=1]{\underline{d}(-,i)}\Lambda(-,i,j)\right)\leftarrow\left(\xleftarrow[i=1]{m_{+}}\xleftarrow[j=\underline{d}(+,i)]{1}\Lambda(+,i,j)\right),

(6.9)

where for example the notation
\xleftarrow[i=1]{m_{+}}\xleftarrow[j=\underline{d}(+,i)]{1}\Lambda(+,i,j)
means

\Lambda(+,m_{+},1)\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\Lambda(+,2,\underline{d}(+,2))\leftarrow\Lambda(+,1,1)\leftarrow\ldots\leftarrow\Lambda(+,1,\underline{d}(+,1)).

Let
Q^{\mathrm{std}}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
with standard Levi of the form

\prod_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(+,i)}^{\underline{d}(+,i)}\times\mathrm{GL}_{r+1}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(-,i)}^{\underline{d}(-,i)}.

Let
Q
be the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup of
G
associated to the pair
(Q^{\mathrm{std}},\sum_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\underline{d}(+,i))
under the bijection of Proposition
5.1
. Moreover, set
\pi_{+}=\boxtimes\pi_{+,i}
and
\pi_{-}=\boxtimes\pi_{-,i}
. They are representations of standard Levi subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}
. For
m\in\{n,n+1\}
, we have the elements
w_{\pi_{+},m}^{*}
and
w_{\pi_{-},m}^{*}
, which we identify as elements of
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{+}}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{-}}
embedded in
M_{P,m}
. Then by applying Proposition
5.13
, we see that if we take the residues in the order prescribed above we have for
\lambda\in\mathcal{H}
in general position

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathcal{L}}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{Q}(\phi,\lambda,w_{\pi_{+},n}^{*}w_{\pi_{-},n}^{*}).

Note that, when restricted to
\mathcal{H}
, the linear forms in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
can be written, for
i
and
j
in the suitable range, as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Lambda^{\prime}(+,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(+,i)_{n,j}-\lambda(+,i)_{n,j+1}-1=\lambda(+,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(+,i)-j}-\lambda(+,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(+,i)-j+1}-1\\
\Lambda^{\prime}(-,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(-,i)_{n,j-1}-\lambda(-,i)_{n,j}+1=\lambda(-,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(-,i)-j+1}-\lambda(-,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(-,i)-j+2}+1.\end{array}\right.

(6.10)

It follows as in
[
16
, Lemma 5.5]
that we have for
\lambda\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
in general position

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}\mathrm{Res}_{\mathcal{L}}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{Q}(M^{*}(w_{\pi_{+},n}^{*}w_{\pi_{-},n}^{*},\lambda)\phi,w_{\pi_{+},n}^{*}w_{\pi_{-},n}^{*}\lambda).

By reversing the order in (
6.9
), we see that the same relation holds with
M^{*}(w_{\pi_{+},n+1}^{*}w_{\pi_{-},n+1}^{*},\lambda)
instead. By parabolic descent (Proposition
5.10
), we can now input the result of
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
. This shows that the residue factors through
M^{*}(w_{\pi,n}^{*},\lambda)
and
M^{*}(w_{\pi,n+1}^{*},\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\nu_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. But we know by
[
16
, Lemma 3.2]
that these operators realize the global quotient maps, which concludes the proof.
∎

6.1.3
The residue-free construction

We now explain an alternative construction of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
without residues. The idea is to realize
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
as a subrepresentation of some parabolic induction from the cuspidal spectrum rather than as a quotient. It is more suited to study the analytic properties of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
which we will prove in §
6.1.5
below.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\underline{n}^{-}(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2m_{i}}
be the tuple such that
\underline{n}^{-}(i,2j-1)=(\underline{d}(i,j)-1)\underline{r}(i,j)
and
\underline{n}^{-}(i,2j)=\underline{r}(i,j)
for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
. Let
P_{\pi,+}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

\underline{n}(P_{\pi,+})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{-}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

Then
P_{\pi}\subset P_{\pi,+}\subset P
. Let
w_{+}\in W(P_{\pi,+})
be the shortest element such that, if we set
Q_{\pi,+}=w_{+}P_{\pi,+}
, then

\underline{n}(Q_{\pi,+})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{r}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{r}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

In words, on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
component
w_{+}
sends the last
\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)}
block in each product
\mathrm{GL}_{(\underline{d}(1,i)-1)\underline{r}(1,i)}\times\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)}
after the product
\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathrm{GL}_{(\underline{d}(2,i)-1)\underline{r}(2,i)}
, while preserving the order. The description on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
component is the same.

Let
P_{+,n+1}^{\mathrm{std}}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
such that

\underline{n}(P_{+,n+1}^{\mathrm{std}})=\left(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),k+1,\underline{n}(-)\right).

(6.11)

where we set
k+1=\sum_{i}\underline{r}(2,i)
. Let
P_{+}
be the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup of
G
corresponding to the pair
(P^{\mathrm{std}}_{+,n+1},m_{+}+m_{1}+m_{2}+1)
under the bijection of Proposition
5.1
. Here we allow for blocks of size zero, so that the
(m_{+}+m_{1}+m_{2}+1)^{\text{th}}
block is
\mathrm{GL}_{k+1}
. Note that
w_{+}\in{}_{P^{\mathrm{std}}_{+}}W_{P}
and that
P_{\pi}\subset P_{w_{+}}=P_{\pi,+}
.

We now consider the regularized period
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{P_{+}}(\varphi,\lambda,w_{+})
which is well-defined for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position. In fact, it is also meromorphic for
\lambda
in the smaller subspace
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
as shown in the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. The map

\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{P_{+}}\left(\varphi,\lambda,w_{+}\right)

(6.12)

is a well-defined meromorphic function on
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
. Moreover, for
\lambda
in general position we have

\mathcal{P}^{P_{+}}\left(\varphi,\lambda,w_{+}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda).

(6.13)

Proof.

This is the same proof as
[
16
, Proposition 5.9]
, taking into account the additional affine linear forms appearing in the proof of Theorem
6.2
.
∎

6.1.4
Functional equations of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}

Note that any
w\in W(P)
can be identified with a couple
(w_{n},w_{n+1})\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{+}+m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{-})^{2}
, where we recall that we allow blocks of size zero in the case
\underline{d}(1,i)=1
or
\underline{d}(2,j)=1
. We define
W(\pi)
to be the set of
w\in W(P)
which belong to the subgroup
(\mathfrak{S}(m_{+})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{1}+m_{2})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{-}))^{2}
and which satisfy

w_{n}=w_{n+1}=(\sigma_{+},\sigma,\sigma_{-}),\;\sigma_{+}\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{+}),\;\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{1})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{2})\subset\mathfrak{S}(m_{1}+m_{2}),\;\sigma_{-}\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{-}).

(6.14)

Therefore, with the definitions in §
6.1.1
,
W(\pi)
is exactly the subset of
w\in W(P)
such that
(I,w.P,w\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. Moreover, we have
w(\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})=\mathfrak{a}_{w\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{w\pi}
.

To any element
((\sigma_{+},\sigma,\sigma_{-}),(\sigma_{+},\sigma,\sigma_{-}))\in(\mathfrak{S}(m_{+})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{1}+m_{2})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{-}))^{2}
we may associate
w_{\mathbf{M}}\in W(M_{P^{\mathrm{std}}_{+}})
which acts by blocks on
M_{P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}}
in a natural way on (
6.11
) by stabilizing the
\mathrm{GL}_{k}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{k+1}
blocks on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
components respectively. We denote by
W_{\mathbf{M}}(\pi)
the subset of
w
that arise this way, identified with a subset of
W(P_{+}^{\mathrm{std}})\subset W
. By conjugating by
w_{P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}}
, we can identify
w_{\mathbf{M}}
with an element of
\mathbf{M}_{P_{+}}^{2}
, which we still denote by
w_{\mathbf{M}}
. We then have the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup
w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{+}
. Moreover, the standard parabolic subgroup
(w_{+}.P_{\pi,+})\cap\mathcal{M}_{P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}}
has standard Levi
\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(2,i)}
. Write
W_{\mathcal{M}}(\pi)
for
W((w_{+}.P_{\pi,+})\cap\mathcal{M}_{P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}})
of elements that act by blocks on it. It is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}(m_{1})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{2})
. If we identify it with a subgroup of
W
, its elements commute with
W_{\mathbf{M}}(\pi)
.

The functional equations satisfied by
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
are summarized in the following proposition.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Let
w_{\mathbf{M}}\in W_{\mathbf{M}}(\pi)
, let
w_{\mathcal{M}}\in W_{\mathcal{M}}(\pi)
. Then we have for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{+}}\left(M(w_{\mathbf{M}}w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}},w_{\mathbf{M}}w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+}\lambda\right).

(6.15)

Proof.

It is easy to show using the same arguments as in the proof of
[
16
, Lemma 5.5]
that the intertwining operator and the regularized period in the RHS of (
6.15
) are well-defined for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
, so that it yields a meromorphic function in
\lambda
. By the functional equation of Eisenstein series from
[
8
, Theorem 2.3.4]
, we have for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position the equality

E^{P_{+}^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w_{+},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}},w_{+}\lambda)=E^{P_{+}^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}},w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+}\lambda).

Let us now assume that
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
. By Proposition
5.10
, we know that
\mathcal{P}^{P_{+}}
decomposes as a product of a (bilinear) inner-product on
\mathbf{M}_{P_{+}}^{2}
and a Zeta function on
\mathcal{M}_{P_{+}}
. For the former, we have the have the unitarity of global intertwining operators on the unitary axis from
[
5
, Theorem 7.2]
, and for the latter we have the functional equations of Eisenstein series. Using the expression of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)
from Proposition
6.3
, we see that

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{+}}\left(\left(M(w_{\mathbf{M}},w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+}\lambda)E^{P_{+}^{\mathrm{std}}}(M(w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}},w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{+}\lambda)\right)(w_{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std},-1}\cdot)\right).

Note that here we use
w_{P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}}=w_{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{+}}^{\mathrm{std}}
. We now conclude by Lemma
3.5
that we can switch the intertwining operator with the partial Eisenstein series. This shows that (
6.15
) holds for
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
, and we conclude for
\lambda
in general position by analytic continuation.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
w\in W(\pi)
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position we have

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}_{w\pi}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi,w\lambda).

(6.16)

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
6.3
and Lemma
6.4
.
∎

6.1.5
Analytic properties of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}

We now investigate the singularities of the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\cdot,\lambda)
. Let
(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. Set

\pi_{1,2}=\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\right)\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}\right).

This is a discrete representation of a subgroup
M_{1,2}\subset M_{P}
. Moreover, if
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, let
\lambda_{1,2}
be its restriction to
\mathfrak{a}_{M_{1,2},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. In the coordinates of (
6.8
), we have the expression
\lambda_{1,2}=((\lambda(1),-\lambda(2)),(-\lambda(1),\lambda(2)))
. Let
L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
be the product of linear forms on
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\nu_{\pi}
defined by

L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\\
\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\\
\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}\right).

(6.17)

It only depends on
\lambda_{1,2}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
of
G
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the meromorphic function

\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\mapsto L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)

is regular in the region
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
(see (
3.15
)).

Proof.

Let
w\in W(\pi)
. By Proposition
4.14
, there exists
k
and
c_{J}
as in the proposition such that the operator
M(w,\lambda)
is regular in the region
\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
. Moreover, this region is stable by
w
. It follows from Corollary
6.5
that we can replace
\pi
by
w\pi
. In particular, we can and will assume that
\underline{d}(1,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(1,m_{1})
and
\underline{d}(2,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(2,m_{2})
.

By Proposition
6.3
, we have to study the poles of
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{P_{+}}(\varphi,\lambda,w_{+})
. To do this, we want to use Proposition
5.11
. By Lemma
4.3
, we have
\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\pi_{+},\nu_{+}}(G)
where
\pi_{+}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P_{\pi,+}})
and
\nu_{+}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi,+}}^{*}
. In coordinates, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{+,n}

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{+}},\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\underline{d}(1,1)-1}{2},\ldots,-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\underline{d}(1,m_{1})-1}{2}}_{m_{1}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{2}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{-}}\right),

(6.18)

\displaystyle\nu_{+,n+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{+}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{1}},\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\underline{d}(2,1)-1}{2},\ldots,-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\underline{d}(2,m_{2})-1}{2}}_{m_{2}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{-}}\right),

(6.19)

Going back to the description of §
6.1.3
, we see that

\left(\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi,+}},\quad w_{+}\alpha<0\right)\implies\langle\nu_{+},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\geq 0.

(6.20)

Moreover,
w_{+}
only acts on
M_{1,2}
. It follows that the condition
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
implies that
\lambda+\nu_{+}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w_{+})
. We now compute
n_{\pi_{+}}(w_{+},\lambda+\nu_{+})=n_{\pi_{+},n}(w_{+},\lambda+\nu_{+})n_{\pi_{+},n+1}(w_{+},\lambda+\nu_{+})
. On the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
side, using the fact that
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
, it follows from (
6.18
) and the computations of Lemma
4.5
that

\displaystyle n_{\pi_{+},n}(w_{+},\lambda+\nu_{+})=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m_{1}}\frac{L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)-\underline{d}(1,j)}{2}+1,\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{1,j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)+\underline{d}(1,j)}{2},\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{1,j}^{\vee}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m_{1}\\
1\leq j\leq m_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)-\underline{d}(2,j)+1}{2},\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}\right)}{L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)+\underline{d}(2,j)-1}{2},\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}\right)},

and on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
we have

n_{\pi_{+},n}(w_{+},\lambda+\nu_{+})=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq m_{2}}\frac{L\left(\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,i)-\underline{d}(2,j)}{2}+1,\sigma_{2,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}^{\vee}\right)}{L\left(\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,i)+\underline{d}(2,j)}{2},\sigma_{2,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}^{\vee}\right)}.

By Theorem
4.1
, we can localize the poles of these expressions. In some region
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
, all the denominators are non-zero except
L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)+\underline{d}(2,j)-1}{2},\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}\right)
with
\underline{d}(1,i)=\underline{d}(2,j)=1
. However, this term is compensated by the corresponding numerator so that all the poles come from the numerators. Moreover, the possible pole of
L\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)-\underline{d}(2,j)+1}{2},\sigma_{1,i}\times\sigma_{2,j}\right)
for
\underline{d}(1,i)=2
and
\underline{d}(2,j)=1
, or for
\underline{d}(1,i)=1
and
\underline{d}(2,j)=2
, is always compensated by a pole of the corresponding denominator. Finally we see that we can take

\displaystyle L_{\pi,w_{+}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{1,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{j})

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{2,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{j})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\\
\underline{d}(2,j)\neq 2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\\
\underline{d}(1,i)\neq 2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}).

(6.21)

This product of affine linear forms controls the poles of
M(w_{+},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi,+}}
in our region.

On the other hand, with the notation of Proposition
5.10
, we have

(w_{+}\nu_{+})_{\mathcal{P}}=\left(\left(\frac{\underline{d}(1,1)-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{\underline{d}(1,m_{1})-1}{2}\right),\left(\frac{\underline{d}(2,1)-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{\underline{d}(2,m_{2})-1}{2}\right)\right).

Because of our hypothesis on the
\underline{d}(1,i)
and
\underline{d}(2,j)
, this elements belongs to
\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*,+}}
. In particular,
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
implies that
(w_{+}(\lambda+\nu_{+}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{(w_{+}\pi_{+})_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
. With the notation of (
5.21
), we see that
L_{(w_{+}\pi_{+})_{\mathcal{P}},Z}(w_{+}(\lambda+\nu_{+})_{\mathcal{P}_{+}})
is

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma_{2,j}\simeq\sigma_{1,i}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)+\underline{d}(2,j)-1}{2}\right)\prod_{\sigma_{2,j}\simeq\sigma_{1,i}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)+\underline{d}(2,j)-3}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m_{1}\\
\sigma_{1,i}\simeq\sigma_{1,j}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)-\underline{d}(1,j)}{2}\right)^{-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq m_{2}\\
\sigma_{2,i}\simeq\sigma_{2,j}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,i)-\underline{d}(2,j)}{2}\right)^{-1}.

(6.22)

The first term is always non-zero for
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
. The second has the same zeros as

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\\
\underline{d}(2,j)=2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\\
\underline{d}(1,i)=2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}).

Finally, the last two products compensate the first two terms in (
6.1.5
). Putting everything together, we conclude that the result follows from Proposition
5.11
.
∎

We finally bound the regularized period.

Proposition 6.7
.

There exists
k>0
such that for any level
J
and
C>0
there exist
c_{J}>0
,
d>0
,
N>0
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for any
J
-pair
(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left|L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)\right|\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X_{i}},

(6.23)

in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\;\middle|\;\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}},\;\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C\right\}.

(6.24)

Proof.

Given all the explicit computations of Proposition
6.6
, this is a direct consequence of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

6.1.6
Proof of Theorem
1.4

We now end the proof of Theorem
1.4
which described the main properties of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
. Its first point was the fact that the residue of the Rankin–Selberg Zeta integrals
\mathrm{Res}\;Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi,\lambda)
factored through
\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi},\lambda-\nu_{p}i}(G)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi,\lambda}(G)
for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position. This was the content of Theorem
6.2
. The second point was that it defined a continuous linear form in
\varphi
, which was shown in Proposition
6.7
. Finally, we have to prove that
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
admits an Euler product expansion. If we set

\mathcal{L}(\lambda,\pi)=\underset{\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}}{\mathrm{Res}}\frac{L(\lambda-\nu_{\pi}+\frac{1}{2},\sigma_{\pi,n}\times\sigma_{pi,n+1})}{b(\lambda-\nu_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi})},

then by computing the residues on the Euler product expansion of (
5.20
), we see as in
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
that for
\varphi=E^{P,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi})
and
\phi=\otimes_{v}\phi_{v}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
we have for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
in general position

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{L}(\lambda,\pi)\prod_{v\in\mathtt{S}}Z^{\natural}_{\sigma_{\pi},v}(\phi_{v},\lambda-\nu_{\pi}),

for some finite set of places
\mathtt{S}
. This was exactly the content of Theorem
1.4
, which therefore concludes the proof.

6.2
Increasing inducing data

For the purpose of our proof of the fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period, we define a set
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
of
increasing inducing data
. More precisely, if
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
, we want to choose an induction
\mathcal{A}_{Q,\pi^{\prime}}(G)
isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
such that the singularities of the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot,\lambda)
are controlled in a larger region than
\lambda_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{\prime}_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
. This will prove to be crucial in our shift of contours arguments. The definition of
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
is rather involved, and we invite the reader to come back to it when necessary in the course of Section
7
.

6.2.1
A set of combinatorial gadgets

We define
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
to be the set of tuples
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})
satisfying the following conditions.

•

I\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{6}
is a tuple of the form
I=\left(n_{+},n_{1},n_{\mathrm{c},1},n_{2},n_{\mathrm{c},2},n_{-}\right)
.

•

I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are subset of
\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}
and
\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}
for some integers
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
respectively, with
\left|I_{1}\right|=\left|I_{2}\right|
. We set
m=\left|I_{1}\right|
and write
I_{1}=\{i_{1}(1)<\ldots<i_{1}(m)\}
and
I_{2}=\{i_{2}(1)<\ldots<i_{2}(m)\}
.

•

We have tuples of integers
\underline{n}(+)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{+}}
,
\underline{n}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{1}}
,
\underline{n}^{\prime}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{1}-m}
,
\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}
,
\underline{n}(2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{2}}
,
\underline{n}^{\prime}(2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m_{2}-m}
,
\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}
and
\underline{n}(-)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{m_{-}}
such that
\sum\underline{n}(+,i)=n_{+}
,
\sum\underline{n}(1,i)=n_{1}
,
\sum\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1,i)=n_{\mathrm{c},1}
,
\sum\underline{n}(2,i)=n_{2}
,
\sum\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2,i)=n_{\mathrm{c},2}
and
\sum\underline{n}(-,i)=n_{-}
and
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

\underline{n}(P)=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}(1),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}(2),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

(6.25)

•

\pi=\pi_{n}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
is of the form

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i},

(6.26)

\displaystyle\pi_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\pi_{-,i}^{\vee},

(6.27)

where

\bullet

all the
\pi_{\ldots}
are discrete automorphic representations of the corresponding block of
M_{P}
,

\bullet

for the relevant
i
, we have
\pi_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i},\underline{d}(1,i))
,
\pi_{2,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{2,i},\underline{d}(2,i))
for some representations
\sigma_{1,i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)})
and
\sigma_{2,i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(2,i)})
,

\bullet

\underline{d}(1,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(1,m_{1})\geq 2
and
\underline{d}(2,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(2,m_{2})\geq 2
.

\bullet

for the relevant
i
, we have
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1,i)})
,
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2,i)})
,

\bullet

for
1\leq j\leq m
, we have
\pi_{1,i_{1}(j)}\simeq\pi_{2,i_{2}(j)}^{\vee}
(and therefore
\underline{d}(1,i_{1}(j))=\underline{d}(2,i_{2}(j))
and
\underline{r}(1,i_{1}(j))=\underline{r}(2,i_{2}(j))
).

We identify
\underline{n}^{\prime}(1)
and
\underline{n}^{\prime}(2)
with tuples indexed by
\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}\setminus I_{1}
and
\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}\setminus I_{2}
respectively. In particular, we have for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
the formulae
\underline{n}(i,j)=\underline{d}(i,j)\underline{r}(i,j)
, and for
j\notin I_{i}
,
\underline{n}^{\prime}(i,j)=(\underline{d}(i,j)-1)\underline{r}(i,j)
. Note that all the
\pi_{1,j}
and
\pi_{2,j}
are residual. If
I_{1}=I_{2}=\emptyset
, we simply write
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
.

The elements in
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
decompose as

\lambda=\left((\lambda(+)_{n},\lambda(2)_{n},\lambda(1)_{n},\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(-)_{n}),(\lambda(+)_{n+1},\lambda(1)_{n+1},\lambda(2)_{n+1},\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(-)_{n+1})\right).

(6.28)

We define

\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}=\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda(+)_{n}=-\lambda(+)_{n+1},&\\
\lambda(1)_{n,i}=-\lambda(1)_{n+1,i},&1\leq i\leq m_{1},\;\text{if }\underline{d}(1,i)\neq 1\text{ and }i\notin I_{1},\\
\lambda(2)_{n,i}=-\lambda(2)_{n+1,i},&1\leq i\leq m_{2},\;\text{if }\underline{d}(2,i)\neq 1\text{ and }i\notin I_{2},\\
\lambda(1)_{n,i_{1}(j)}=-\lambda(2)_{n,i_{2}(j)},&1\leq j\leq m,\\
\lambda(-)_{n}=-\lambda(-)_{n+1},&\end{array}\right\}.

(6.29)

This notation is somewhat abusive as this space really depends on the data of
I
,
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
. The dependence should be clear in context and we use similar simplifications throughout this section.

We have an element
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
defined in the coordinates of (
6.25
) by

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=\left(\left(\underbrace{1/4,\ldots,1/4}_{m_{+}},0,0,0,\underbrace{-1/4,\ldots,-1/4}_{m_{-}}\right),\left(\underbrace{1/4,\ldots,1/4}_{m_{+}},0,0,0,\underbrace{-1/4,\ldots,-1/4}_{m_{-}}\right)\right).

(6.30)

We will also need the variation

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}=\left(\left(0,\underbrace{1/4,\ldots,1/4}_{m_{2}-m},\underbrace{-1/4,\ldots,-1/4}_{m_{1}},0,0\right),\left(0,\underbrace{1/4,\ldots,1/4}_{m_{1}-m},\underbrace{-1/4,\ldots,-1/4}_{m_{2}},0,0\right)\right)\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}.

(6.31)

We emphasize that it does not belong to
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
except if
I_{1}=I_{2}=\emptyset
.

6.2.2
Construction of the period

Let
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. We now build a regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
on
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\underline{n}^{-}(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2m_{i}}
be the tuple such that
\underline{n}^{-}(i,2j-1)=(\underline{d}(i,j)-1)\underline{r}(i,j)
and
\underline{n}^{-}(i,2j)=\underline{r}(i,j)
for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
. Let
P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
such that

\underline{n}(P_{\pi}^{\uparrow})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}^{-}(1),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}(2),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

For
j\in\{1,2\}
, we also define the tuple
\underline{n}_{I_{i}}^{-}(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I_{i}}
by
\underline{n}_{I_{i}}^{-}(i,j)=(\underline{d}(i,j)-1)\underline{r}(i,j)
for
j\in I_{i}
. Note that if we identify
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
with
\{1,\ldots,m\}
via the maps
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, then
\underline{n}_{I_{1}}^{-}(1)=\underline{n}^{-}_{I_{2}}(2)
. We then let
w^{\uparrow}
be the shortest element in
W(P_{\pi}^{\uparrow})
such that, if we set
Q_{\pi}^{\uparrow}=w^{\uparrow}.P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
, we have

\underline{n}(Q_{\pi}^{\uparrow})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}_{I_{1}}(1),\underline{r}(1),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}_{I_{2}}(2),\underline{r}(2),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

In words,
w^{\uparrow}
only acts on the
\underline{n}^{-}(1)
component on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side, and on the
(\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}(2))
component on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side. It sends all the
\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(1,i)}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{r}(2,i)}
blocks at the bottom, and then sorts the
\mathrm{GL}_{(\underline{d}(1,i)-1)\underline{r}(1,i)}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
side, and
\mathrm{GL}_{(\underline{d}(2,i)-1)\underline{r}(2,i)}
on
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
by bringing those coming from
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
at the bottom. Finally, it exchanges the resulting
\underline{n}^{\prime}(1)
and
\underline{n}^{\prime}(2)
blocks on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side.

Let
P^{\uparrow,\mathrm{std}}_{n+1}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
such that

\underline{n}(P_{n+1}^{\uparrow,\mathrm{std}})=\left(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}^{-}_{I_{2}}(2),k+1,\underline{n}(-)\right).

where

k+1=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\underline{r}(2,i)+\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2,i).

Let
P^{\uparrow}
be the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup of
G
associated the pair
(P_{n+1}^{\uparrow,\mathrm{std}},m_{+}+m_{1}+m_{2}-m+1)
