under the bijection of Proposition
5.1
, where once again the
(m_{+}+m_{1}+m_{2}-m+1)^{\text{th}}
block is
\mathrm{GL}_{k+1}
. We have
w^{\uparrow}\in{}_{P^{\uparrow,\mathrm{std}}}W_{P}
and
P_{w^{\uparrow}}=P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
.

We set for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda):=\mathcal{P}^{P^{\uparrow}}(\varphi,\lambda,w^{\uparrow}).

(6.32)

Note that contrary to the case of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
, we prefer to view
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
as a meromorphic function on the whole space
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
rather than solely on a translate of
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. This is possible by
[
16
, Lemma 4.15]
. The trade-off is that the linear form
\varphi\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
is not
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant in general. By reproducing the proof of Proposition
6.3
, the restriction of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
to
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
is well-defined (see also Proposition
6.8
below).

6.2.3
Relation with the regularized period

We connect the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
from (
6.32
) to the linear forms
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
built in Theorem
6.2
. First, for
i\in\{1,2\}
we write
\underline{n}_{I_{i}}(i)
and
\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{i}}(i)
for the tuples in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{I_{i}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{\{1,\ldots,m_{i}\}\setminus I_{i}}
such that for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, we have
\underline{n}_{I_{i}}(i,j)=\underline{n}(i,j)
if
j\in I_{i}
, and
\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{i}}(i,j)=\underline{n}(i,j)
otherwise. We now let
w^{\downarrow}\in W(P)
be the shortest element such that, if we set
P^{\downarrow}=w^{\downarrow}.P
, then

\underline{n}(P^{\downarrow})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{1}}(1),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}_{I_{1}}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{2}}(2),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2),\underline{n}_{I_{2}}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

Set
\pi^{\downarrow}:=w^{\downarrow}\pi
. Define

N_{1}=\sum_{j\notin I_{1}}\underline{n}(1,j)+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{{\mathrm{c},1}}}\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1,j),\quad N_{2}=\sum_{j\notin I_{2}}\underline{n}(2,j)+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{{\mathrm{c},2}}}\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2,j),\quad N_{-}=\sum_{j\in I_{1}}\underline{n}(1,j)+n_{-}.

Then we have
((n_{+},N_{1},N_{2},N_{-}),P^{\downarrow},\pi^{\downarrow})\in\Pi_{H}
and we can therefore consider the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}
built in Theorem
6.2
. Note that we have

w^{\downarrow}(\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow})=\mathfrak{a}_{\pi^{\downarrow},\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}

(6.33)

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in general position we have

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}(M(w^{\downarrow},\lambda)\varphi,w^{\downarrow}\lambda).

(6.34)

Proof.

We begin by expressing each side of (
6.34
) as a residue of a regularized period induced from
\sigma_{\pi}
. By parabolic descent (Lemma
5.4
), it is enough to deal with the case
m_{+}=m_{-}=0
. We decompose the standard Levi
M_{P_{\pi}}
of
P_{\pi}
with respect to
M_{P_{\pi}}\subset M_{P}
. This yields an identification

\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},n,\mathbb{C}}^{*}=\prod_{i\notin I_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(2,i)-1}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(1,i)}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}\mathbb{C},\quad\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi},n+1,\mathbb{C}}^{*}=\prod_{i\notin I_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(1,i)-1}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{\underline{d}(2,i)}\times\prod_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\mathbb{C}.

We now define affine linear forms as in the proof of Theorem
6.2
. We use the same convention for coordinates. We begin with the set
\Lambda^{\uparrow}_{+}
of affine linear forms described by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda_{+}(1,i,j)(\lambda)=-(\lambda(1,i)_{n,\underline{d}(1,i)-j+1}+\lambda(1,i)_{n+1,j}+1/2),&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m_{1},\;i\notin I_{1},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(1,i)-1,\end{array}\right.\\
\Lambda_{+}(2,i,j)(\lambda)=-(\lambda(2,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(2,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(2,i)-j+1}+1/2),&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m_{2},\;i\notin I_{2},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(2,i)-1,\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

as well as the set
\Lambda^{\uparrow}_{-}
of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda_{-}(1,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(1,i)_{n,\underline{d}(1,i)-j}+\lambda(1,i)_{n+1,j}-1/2,&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m_{1},\;i\notin I_{1},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(1,i)-1,\end{array}\right.\\
\Lambda_{-}(2,i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(2,i)_{n,j}+\lambda(2,i)_{n+1,\underline{d}(2,i)-j}-1/2,&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m_{2},\;i\notin I_{2},\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(2,i)-1.\end{array}\right.\\
\end{array}\right.

We then add the set
\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}(i,j)(\lambda)=-(\lambda(1,i_{1}(i))_{n,j}+\lambda(2,i_{2}(i))_{n+1,\underline{d}(2,i)-j+2}+1/2),&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m,\\
2\leq j\leq\underline{d}(1,i_{1}(i)),\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

as well as
\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}(i,j)(\lambda)=\lambda(1,i_{1}(i))_{n,j}+\lambda(2,i_{2}(i))_{n+1,\underline{d}(2,i)-j+1}-1/2,&\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq m,\\
1\leq j\leq\underline{d}(1,i_{1}(i)),\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the intersection of all the zero sets of the affine linear forms in
\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow}
,
\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow}
,
\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
. Then we have

\mathcal{H}=\mathfrak{a}^{*}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}-\nu_{\pi}=(w^{\downarrow})^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{a}_{\pi^{\downarrow},\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}-\nu_{\pi^{\downarrow}}\right).

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Assume that
\varphi=E^{*}(\phi,-\nu_{\pi})
with
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi}}(G)
. By reproducing the proof of Theorem
6.2
(see also
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
), we see that for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in general position we have

\left(\underset{\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda^{\uparrow}_{-}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow}}{\mathrm{Res}}\right)Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi)(\lambda-\nu_{\pi})=\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda).

(6.35)

Moreover,
\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow}\cup\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow}\cup\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}
are the sets used in Theorem
6.2
and
[
16
, Theorem 5.2]
to define the period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}
for
(P^{\downarrow},\pi^{\downarrow})
. By reproducing once again the argument, we end up at

\left(\underset{\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{-}^{\uparrow,^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{Res}}\;\underset{\Lambda_{+}^{\uparrow}}{\mathrm{Res}}\right)Z_{\sigma_{\pi}}(\phi)(\lambda-\nu_{\pi})=\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}(M(w^{\downarrow},\lambda)\varphi,w^{\downarrow}\lambda).

(6.36)

By the same argument as in
[
16
, Lemma 5.1]
, we can compute these residues in any order. Therefore, (
6.35
) and (
6.36
) are equal, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

We spell out the content of Proposition
6.8
in a simple example. We take
n=2
, so that
G=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{3}
. We consider the tuple
((0,2,0,2,1,0),P,\pi,\{1\},\{1\})
where
P
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with standard Levi
(\mathrm{GL}_{2})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1})
and
\pi
is the trivial representation of
M_{P}
. We have
\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\simeq\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and with respect to these coordinates,
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}=\{(a,(-a,b))\;|\;a,b\in\mathbb{C}\}
and
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}=(-1/4,(-1/4,0))
. Here
P^{\uparrow}
is the Borel subgroup, and
w_{+}=1
. We take
\varphi^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the unique
K
-invariant vector such that
\varphi^{\circ}(1)=1
. We assume that
F=\mathbb{Q}
, and denote by
\zeta
the completed zeta function of
\mathbb{Q}
. Using
[
18
]
and the Archimedean computations of
[
49
]
, and taking into account the computation of
\varphi^{\circ}_{P_{0}}
in Lemma
4.3
, we see that for
\lambda=(x,(y,z))\in\mathfrak{a}_{P,\mathbb{C}}^{*}

\mathcal{P}^{P^{\uparrow}}(\varphi^{\circ},\lambda)=\mathrm{vol}([\mathrm{GL}_{1}]_{0})\times\frac{\zeta(x+y+3/2)\zeta(x+z+1)}{\zeta(y-x+3/2)},

where the volume of
[\mathrm{GL}_{1}]_{0}
is taken with respect to the measure of §
3.2.3
. This meromorphic function is well defined for
\lambda=(a+1/4,(-a+1/4,b))\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in general position and for such
\lambda
we get

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi^{\circ},\lambda)=\mathrm{vol}([\mathrm{GL}_{1}]_{0})\times\frac{\zeta(2)\zeta(a+b+5/4)}{\zeta(-b-a+7/4)},

On the other hand,
P^{\downarrow}
has standard Levi
(\mathrm{GL}_{2})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{1}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2})
, and
w_{+}
exchanges the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{1}
blocks on the
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
-side. Because of the factorization of
M(w^{\uparrow},\lambda)
in (
4.7
), we get

\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}(M(w^{\downarrow},\lambda)\varphi^{\circ},w^{\downarrow}\lambda)=\mathrm{vol}([\mathrm{GL}_{2}]_{0})\times\frac{\zeta(-b-a-1/4)}{\zeta(-b-a+7/4)}.

Therefore, Proposition
6.8
amounts to the two equalities

\mathrm{vol}([\mathrm{GL}_{1}]_{0})\zeta(2)=\mathrm{vol}([\mathrm{GL}_{2}]_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta(a+b+5/4)=\zeta(-a-b-1/4).

The first equation is equivalent to the computation of the Tamagawa volume of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
by
[
37
]
, and the second is the functional equation of the
\zeta
function.

6.2.4
Functional equations

We now describe some subsets of the Weyl group
W
of
G
. We first define
W_{\mathbf{M}}(\pi)
. This is the subset of the set of
w=(w_{n},w_{n+1})\in W
that act by blocks on
\mathbf{M}_{P^{\uparrow}}^{\mathrm{std},2}
and correspond to permutations

((\sigma_{+},\sigma_{1,2},\sigma_{-}),(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{1,2},\sigma_{-})),\quad\sigma_{+}\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{+}),\quad\sigma_{1,2}\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{1}+m_{2}-|I_{1}|),\quad\sigma_{-}\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{-}).

This set really depends on
I
and
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
but we drop the reference from the notation. We can also identify any such
w
with an element of
\mathbf{M}_{P^{\uparrow}}^{2}
(by conjugating by
w_{P^{\uparrow}}^{\mathrm{std}}
), and we will again denote it by
w
. In particular, we have the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup
w.P^{\uparrow}
.

We then define
W_{\mathcal{M}}(\pi)
to be the subset of elements acting by blocks on the standard parabolic subgroup
(w^{\uparrow}.P^{\uparrow}_{\pi})\cap\mathcal{M}_{P^{\uparrow,\mathrm{std}}}
. Its elements are identified with couples
(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n+1})\in\mathfrak{S}(m_{c,1}+m_{1})\times\mathfrak{S}(m_{c,2}+m_{2})
. The next result follows from Lemma
6.4
and Proposition
6.8
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. Let
w_{\mathbf{M}}\in W_{\mathbf{M}}(\pi)
, let
w_{\mathcal{M}}\in W_{\mathcal{M}}(\pi)
. Then we have for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in general position

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P^{\uparrow}}\left(M(w_{\mathbf{M}}w_{\mathcal{M}}w^{\uparrow},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}},w_{\mathbf{M}}w_{\mathcal{M}}w^{\uparrow}\lambda\right).

6.2.5
Analytic properties of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}

Consider the map

\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\mathbb{C}\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}\mapsto\left(\lambda(+)_{n},\lambda(1)_{n},\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(2)_{n+1},\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(-)_{n}\right).

It follows from the definition that it is injective. Indeed, for
i\notin I_{1}
we have
\lambda(1)_{n,i}=-\lambda(1)_{n+1,i}
, and for
i\notin I_{2}
we have
\lambda(2)_{n,i}=-\lambda(2)_{n+1,i}
. We set

\left(\lambda(+),\lambda(1),\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(2),\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(-)\right)=\left(\lambda(+)_{n},\lambda(1)_{n},\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(2)_{n+1},\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}},\lambda(-)_{n}\right).

(6.37)

In these coordinates,
-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
is
(0,1/4,0,1/4,0,0)
. We now set

\lambda_{1,2}=(\lambda(1),\lambda(2)).

Let
\pi^{\uparrow}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}})
and
\nu^{\uparrow}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}}^{*}
such that for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
we have
\varphi_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}}\in\mathcal{A}_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow},\pi^{\uparrow},\nu^{\uparrow}}(G)
. If
\mu\in w^{\uparrow}\mathfrak{a}_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}}^{*}
, we write
\mu=\mu_{\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}}+\mu_{\mathbf{P}^{\uparrow}}
for its decomposition with respect to
M_{P^{\uparrow}}^{\mathrm{std}}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\uparrow}}^{\mathrm{std}}\times\mathbf{M}_{P^{\uparrow}}^{\mathrm{std},2}
. Then we have

\displaystyle(w^{\uparrow}(\lambda+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow},n}

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda(1)_{1}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,1)-1}{2},\ldots,\lambda(1)_{m_{1}}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,m_{1})-1}{2},\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}}\right),

\displaystyle(w^{\uparrow}(\lambda+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow},n+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda(2)_{1}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,1)-1}{2},\ldots,\lambda(2)_{m_{2}}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,m_{2})-1}{2},\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}}\right).

With the notation of (
6.26
) and (
6.27
), we set

\displaystyle\pi_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\pi_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\pi_{2,i},

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\sigma_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1,i}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\right)\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\sigma_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}\right),

where we recall that
\pi_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i},\underline{d}(1,i))
and
\pi_{2,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{2,i},\underline{d}(2,i))
. Finally, we set

\displaystyle L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}^{\uparrow}

\displaystyle(\lambda)=\prod_{\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}\pm\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\sigma_{2,j}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{\underline{d}(2,j)-1\pm 1}{2}\right)\prod_{\sigma_{1,i}^{\vee}\simeq\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}+\frac{\underline{d}(1,i)-1\pm 1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\\
i\notin I_{1},j\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\\
i\notin I_{1},j\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}\right).

(6.38)

Proposition 6.11
.

There exists
k>0
such that for every level
J
of
G
there exists
c_{J}>0
such that for every
J
-pair
(P,\pi)
with
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
, and every
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
the meromorphic function

\lambda\mapsto L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}^{\uparrow}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda)

is regular in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}\;\middle|\;(\lambda+t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow})_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}},\;(w^{\uparrow}(\lambda+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}},\;0\leq t\leq 1\right\}.

(6.39)

Remark 6.12
.

We make the following remarks on Proposition
6.11
.

•

The region (
6.39
) is non-empty because
\underline{d}(1,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(1,m_{1})
and
\underline{d}(2,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(2,m_{2})
.

•

The last two factors of (
6.38
) can only be zero if
t
is close to zero.

•

If
\lambda=\mu-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
with
0\leq t\leq 1
, then
(w^{\uparrow}(\mu+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
implies that
(w^{\uparrow}(\lambda+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}}
.

•

The gain of Proposition
6.11
in comparison with Proposition
6.6
(poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
) is that we have more freedom on
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same pattern as Proposition
6.6
so we will be brief. Let
\lambda
be in the region (
6.39
). Write
\lambda=\mu-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
, so that
\mu_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
. By the description of §
6.2.2
, we see that for any
\alpha\in\Delta_{P_{\pi}^{\uparrow}}
,
w^{\uparrow}\alpha<0
implies that
\langle\nu^{\uparrow}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle\geq 0
as long as
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore, the condition
\mu_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}}
implies that
\mu-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}+\nu^{\uparrow}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}(w^{\uparrow})
. By direct computation, we see that we can take

L_{\pi,w^{\uparrow}}(\lambda)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{1,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\pi_{2,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\\
\underline{d}(2,j)\neq 2\\
i\notin I_{1},j\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\\
\underline{d}(1,i)\neq 2\\
i\notin I_{1},j\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}).

Note that the conditions
\underline{d}(2,j)=2
and
\underline{d}(1,i)=2
are superfluous as they would imply
\pi_{1,i}
or
\pi_{2,j}
cuspidal, which is not possible by the definition of
\Pi^{\uparrow}_{H}
. It remains to compute the factor
L_{(w^{\uparrow}\pi^{\uparrow})_{\mathcal{P}},Z}
of (
5.21
). But this is the same calculation as (
6.22
) in the proof of Proposition
6.6
. The only difference is that we a priori see the factor

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}\simeq\pi_{2,j}^{-,\vee}\\
\underline{d}(2,j)=2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\simeq\pi_{2,j}\\
\underline{d}(1,i)=2\end{subarray}}(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}).

But as we just seen this condition is never met. Putting everything together, we obtain Proposition
6.11
.
∎

We can also state a bound for the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
.

Proposition 6.13
.

There exists
k>0
such that for any level
J
and
C>0
there exist
c_{J}>0
,
d>0
,
N>0
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty})
such that for any
J
-pair
(P,\pi)\in\Pi
with
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
we have

\left|L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}^{\uparrow}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\right|\leq(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{-N,X_{i}}.

(6.40)

in the region

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}\;\middle|\;(\lambda+t\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow})_{1,2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi_{1,2},k,c_{J}},\;(w^{\uparrow}(\lambda+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}},k,c_{J}},\;0\leq t\leq 1,\;\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<C\right\}.

(6.41)

Proof.

This is exactly the same proof as Proposition
6.7
.
∎

6.2.6
The
\emptyset
-transformation

We finally explain how to associate to
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})
another element
(I_{\emptyset},P_{\emptyset},\pi_{\emptyset})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
that will be relevant in our proof of the fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period. We recall that this implies that
I_{\emptyset,1}=I_{\emptyset,2}=\emptyset
, hence the notation. As in §
6.2.3
we define an element
w_{\emptyset}\in W(P)
such that
P_{\emptyset}:=w_{\emptyset}.P
satisfies

\underline{n}(P_{\emptyset})=\left((\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(2),\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{1}}(1),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(1),\underline{n}_{I_{1}}(1),\underline{n}(-)),(\underline{n}(+),\underline{n}^{\prime}(1),\underline{n}_{\setminus I_{2}}(2),\underline{n}_{\mathrm{c}}(2),\underline{n}_{I_{2}}(2),\underline{n}(-))\right).

The standard Levi factor is very close to
M_{P^{\downarrow}}
, except that the second and third product of blocks on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
side are put in the appropriate order to match the definition of (
6.25
). We then set

I_{\emptyset}=\left(n_{+},\left(\sum_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m_{1}\}\setminus I_{1}}\underline{n}(1,j)\right),n_{\mathrm{c},1},\left(\sum_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m_{2}\}\setminus I_{2}}\underline{n}(2,j)\right),n_{\mathrm{c},2},\left(\sum_{j\in I_{1}}\underline{n}(1,j)\right)+n_{-}\right).

We have

w_{\emptyset}\left(\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}\right)=\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}.

(6.42)

Note that because
I_{\emptyset,1}=I_{\emptyset,2}=\emptyset
, the element
\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}
actually belongs to
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}^{*}
.

As in Proposition
6.8
we can relate the two regularized periods.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
. For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\pi,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in general position we have

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(M(w_{\emptyset},\lambda)\varphi,w_{\emptyset}\lambda).

Proof.

This can be proved as Proposition
6.8
.
∎

Finally, let us note that
\pi_{\emptyset}
is closely related to
\pi^{\downarrow}
defined in §
6.2.3
. We sum up their relations in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.15
.

We have
\pi^{\downarrow}=(\pi_{\emptyset})^{\downarrow}
. Moreover, let
w_{\emptyset}^{\downarrow}
be the element "
w^{\downarrow}
" built for
\pi_{\emptyset}
in §
6.2.3
. Then we have
w^{\downarrow}=w_{\emptyset}^{\downarrow}w_{\emptyset}
and
w_{\emptyset}^{\downarrow}\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\emptyset}}=\underline{\rho}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}
.

7
Expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period

The goal of this section is to compute the fine spectral expansion of the Rankin–Selberg period of Theorem
1.6
.

7.1
The spectral expansion

We first precisely write the result that we prove in §
7
, and then prove that it is equivalent to that of Theorem
1.6
.

7.1.1
Measures

Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
. We give the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
a Haar measure. Recall that in §
3.2.3
for any algebraic group
G^{\prime}
we have equipped
\mathfrak{a}_{G^{\prime}}
with the Haar measure giving covolume
1
to
\mathrm{Hom}(X^{*}(G^{\prime}),\mathbb{Z})
, and
i\mathfrak{a}_{G^{\prime}}^{*}
with the dual Haar measure. With the notation of §
6.1.1
, let
G^{\prime}
be the algebraic group

G^{\prime}=\prod_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{n}(+,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{d}(1,i)\underline{r}(1,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{d}(2,i)\underline{r}(2,i)}\prod_{i=1}^{m_{-}}\mathrm{GL}_{\underline{n}(-,i)}.

By choosing the canonical basis of
X^{*}(G^{\prime})
as a basis for
\mathfrak{a}_{G^{\prime}}^{*}
, (
6.8
) becomes an isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{G^{\prime}}^{*}
. We equip
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
with the pushforward of the measure from
i\mathfrak{a}_{G^{\prime}}^{*}
. Note that if
m_{+}=m_{-}=0
and if
\pi
is cuspidal, so that
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
, we get back the Haar measure that we equipped
i\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
with in §
3.2.3
.

We also take the opportunity to fix measure for the increasing inducing data. Let
(I,P,\pi,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
be an increasing distinguished datum. Recall that we have defined in §
6.2.3
an associated triple
(I^{\downarrow},P^{\downarrow},\pi^{\downarrow})\in\Pi_{H}
, which is obtained by conjugating by the element
w^{\downarrow}
. We equip
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
with the pushforward of the measure on
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi^{\downarrow}}^{*}
we juste described.

7.1.2
The main result

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem. Recall that for each
(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
we have defined in §
6.1.4
a subset
W(\pi)\subset W(P)
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
J
be a level of
G
. For every
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
be have

\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}}\frac{1}{|W(\pi)|}\int_{\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\langle f,E(\varphi,\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

(7.1)

where this integral is absolutely convergent.

As noted in
[
19
, Remark 3.8.2.1]
, this expansion is independent from the choice of the level
J
.

7.1.3
An alternative version using relative characters

Before starting the proof of Theorem
7.1
, we reformulate it as in Section
1
.

Let
\Pi_{H}^{J}
be the set of
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}
such that
(P,\pi)
is a
J
-pair. Let
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{J}
. For every
F\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
, and
\lambda\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi,k,c_{J}}
(for
k
and
c_{J}
as in Theorem
4.25
), set

\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\rangle_{G}\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}).

(7.2)

The relative character
\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)
is independent of the choice of
J
.

Lemma 7.2
.

The sum in (
7.2
) is absolutely convergent and defines an analytic function on
\lambda
. For fixed level
J
, for all
q>0
there exists a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{J,q}
on
\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
such that for
(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{J}
and every
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
we have

\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)\leq\frac{\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{J,q}}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{q}(1+\Lambda_{\pi}^{2})^{q}},\quad\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}.

(7.3)

Moreover,
\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)
is
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant in the sense that for every
h\in H(\mathbb{A})
we have
\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(R(h)F,\lambda)=\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)
.

Proof.

The bound (
7.3
) follows from Proposition
4.27
and Proposition
6.7
by noting that the zeros of
L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda)
in a neighborhood of
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}
are contained in those of
L_{\pi,0}(-\overline{\lambda})
.

For the second assertion, for fixed
\lambda
away from the zeros of
L_{\pi,\mathcal{P}}(\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})
we know by Proposition
4.27
and Proposition
6.7
again that

\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(R(h)F,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}_{\pi}\left(\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle R(h)F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\rangle_{G}\varphi,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\right),

where the sum converges in
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)^{J}
. But this is
\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)
because
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
is
H(\mathbb{A})
-invariant by Theorem
6.2
. We conclude by analytic continuation.
∎

We can now state the alternative version of Theorem
7.1
.

Theorem 7.3
.

For any
F\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
we have

\int_{[H]}F(h)dh=\sum_{(I,P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{J}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}\mathcal{I}_{(I,P,\pi)}(F,\lambda)d\lambda.

(7.4)

where the integral is absolutely convergent.

Proof.

That the integral is absolutely convergent follows from Lemma
7.2
and (
4.39
). Theorem
7.3
is now simply a reformulation of Theorem
7.1
.
∎

Now let
f\in\mathcal{S}(G(\mathbb{A}))
and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
. Set
F=K_{f}(g,\cdot)
, where
K_{f}
is the kernel function from (
1.1
). By
[
11
, Lemma 2.10.1.1]
, we have
K_{f}(g,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}([G])
. Theorem
1.6
is now exactly Theorem
7.3
up to a change of variables once we realize that

\langle F,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\rangle=E(g,I_{P}(f,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\overline{\varphi},\overline{\lambda}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}),\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J),\quad\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}.

7.2
Unfolding of the Rankin–Selberg period in terms of partial Zeta functions

In the rest of this section, we fix a level
J
. We prove a first spectral expansion for the integral
\int_{[H]}f(h)dh
using Rankin–Selberg unfolding.

7.2.1
Statement of the result

To write our first formula, we need some notation. For every integer
0\leq r\leq n
, let
P_{r}
be the standard parabolic subgroup of
G
with Levi subgroup
M_{r}:=(\mathrm{GL}_{r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n-r})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1-r})
. Note that it is a standard Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup of
G
, so that
P_{r}^{\mathrm{std}}=P_{r}
. We simply write
\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}
for
\mathbf{M}_{P_{r}}^{2}
,
\mathbf{M}_{r}
for
\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}\cap H
and
\mathcal{M}_{r}
for
\mathcal{M}_{P_{r}}
. These groups are respectively isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{r}^{2}
,
\mathrm{GL}_{r}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{n-r}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n+1-r}
. With respect to the decomposition of
M_{r}
given above, set

\underline{z}_{r}=\left((0,1/4),(0,1/4)\right)\in\mathfrak{a}_{P_{r}}^{*}.

(7.5)

Let
I_{r}
be the tuple

I_{r}=(r,0,n-r,0,n+1-r,0).

(7.6)

We have the set of tuples
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
(see §
6.2.1
). Recall that this means that
I_{1}=I_{2}=\emptyset
in the notation of §
6.2.1
. By unfolding the definition,
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
is the datum of a standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
and of
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
such that the following conditions are satisfied.

•

We have
P\subset P_{r}
. We set
\mathcal{P}=P\cap\mathcal{M}_{r}
and
\mathbf{P}^{2}=P\cap\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}
.

•

Under this decomposition, we have
\pi=(\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
, where
\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{\mathbf{P}})
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{\mathcal{P}})
.

Let
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. We have the space
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}
defined in (
6.29
). We can describe it as
\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
where
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}
is anti-diagonally embedded in
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}^{2}}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. We have the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
from (
6.32
). By Proposition
6.8
, this is simply
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
in this case as
P^{\downarrow}=P
. We also have the element
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\in\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
from (
6.30
). It has coordinates
((1/4,0),(1/4,0))
. We write
\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})
for the set of semi-standard parabolic subgroups of
\mathbf{M}_{r}
with semi-standard Levi factor
M_{\mathbf{P}}
, and
\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})
for the those of
\mathcal{M}_{r}
with semi-standard Levi
M_{\mathcal{P}}
. We have the set
W(\pi)
defined in §
6.1.4
and it satisfies

\left|W(\pi)\right|=\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})\right|\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})\right|.

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 7.4
.

There exists
c>0
such that for all
t>c
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
we have

\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}}\frac{1}{|W(\pi)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.7)

Remark 7.5
.

Note that the sum only runs through the
(I_{r},P,\pi)
such that
(P,\pi)
is a
J
-pair. It now follows from Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
6.11
that the integrand in Proposition
7.4
is holomorphic, and it is of rapid decay by Proposition
4.27
and Proposition
6.13
. Therefore, (
7.7
) is well-defined.

7.2.2
Unfolding of the Rankin–Selberg period in terms of partial Zeta integrals

Let
0\leq r\leq n
. If
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
, we can consider the restriction of the constant term
f_{P_{r}}
to
[M_{r}]
. It is a priori an element in
\mathcal{T}([M_{r}])
, but its growth can be more finely controlled.

Lemma 7.6
.

The following assertions hold.

1.

For every
g_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
, the map
m_{r}\in[\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}]\mapsto f_{P_{r}}(m_{r}g_{r})
belongs to
\mathcal{S}([\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}])
.

2.

Let
N>0
. There exists
c_{N}>0
such that for all
t>c_{N}
there exists a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{t}
on
\mathcal{S}([G])
such that

\left|f_{P_{r}}(m_{r}g_{r})\right|\left|\det g_{r}\right|^{-t}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{t}\left\lVert m_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}^{-N}\left\lVert g_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}_{r}}^{-N},\quad m_{r}\in[\mathbf{M}_{r}],\quad g_{r}\in[\mathcal{M}_{r}],\quad f\in\mathcal{S}([G]).

Proof.

The first assertion is a direct consequence of
[
11
, Lemma 2.5.13.1]
. For the second, note that for every
m_{r}\in\mathbf{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
and
g_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
we have

\delta_{P_{r}}^{1/2}(m_{r}g_{r})=\left|m_{r}\right|^{n-r+1/2}\left|g_{r}\right|^{-r},

where we write
\left|\cdot\right|
for
\left|\det(\cdot)\right|
and the determinant is taken in
G
. By
[
11
, Equation (2.4.1.4)]
and
[
11
, Lemma 2.5.13.1]
, there exists
c>0
such that for every
N^{\prime}>0
we have a continuous semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{N^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{S}([G])
such that

\left|f_{P_{r}}(m_{r}g_{r})\right|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{N^{\prime}}\left\lVert m_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}^{-N^{\prime}}\left\lVert g_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}_{r}}^{-N^{\prime}}\left|m_{r}\right|^{-c(n-r+1/2)N^{\prime}}\left|g_{r}\right|^{crN^{\prime}},

(7.8)

for every
m_{r}\in[\mathbf{M}_{r}]
,
g_{r}\in[\mathcal{M}_{r}]
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
. Set
c_{N}=c(n+1/2)N
and take
t>c_{N}
. By applying (
7.8
) to
N^{\prime}=t/{c_{N}}\times N
and to the Schwartz-function
f\left|\cdot\right|^{-c(n-r+1/2)N^{\prime}}
, we get

\left|f_{P_{r}}(m_{r}g_{r})\right|\left|\det g_{r}\right|^{-t}\leq\left\lVert f\left|\cdot\right|^{-c(n-r+1/2)N^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{N^{\prime}}\left\lVert m_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}^{-N}\left\lVert g_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}_{r}}^{-N},

once again for every
m_{r}\in[\mathbf{M}_{r}]
,
g_{r}\in[\mathcal{M}_{r}]
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
. This concludes.
∎

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
, we can therefore consider the twisted partial diagonal period

g_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})\mapsto P_{r}(f)(g_{r}):=\delta_{P_{r}}^{-1/2}(g_{r})\int_{[\mathbf{M}_{r}]}f_{P_{r}}(m_{r}g_{r})\delta_{P_{r,H}}^{-1}(m_{r})dm_{r},

(7.9)

It is absolutely convergent by Lemma
7.6
and
[
14
, Proposition A.1.1.(vi)]
.

We now recall the construction of Zeta integrals on
\mathcal{M}_{r}
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{r}
be the unipotent radical of the standard Borel of upper triangular matrices in
\mathcal{M}_{r}
. Let
\psi
be a generic automorphic character of
N_{0}(\mathbb{A})
trivial on
N_{0,H}(\mathbb{A})
. Denote again by
\psi
its restriction to
\mathcal{N}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
. For
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
N>0
, consider the map

W_{r,f_{r}}:g_{r}\in[\mathcal{M}_{r}]\mapsto\int_{[\mathcal{N}_{r}]}f_{r}(ng_{r})\overline{\psi}(n)dn,\quad f_{r}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\mathcal{M}_{r}]),

and

Z_{r}(f_{r},s):=\int_{\mathcal{N}_{r,H}(\mathbb{A})\backslash\mathcal{M}_{r,H}(\mathbb{A})}W_{r,f_{r}}(h_{r})\left|\det h_{r}\right|^{s}dh_{r},\quad f_{r}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\mathcal{M}_{r}]).

(7.10)

The first is always defined by an absolute convergent integral, while the other may not be. We have the following result from
[
11
]
.

Lemma 7.7

(
[
11
, Lemma 7.1.1.1.]
)
.

Let
N>0
. There exists
c_{N}>0
such that for every
s\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re(s)\geq c_{N}
the integral (
7.10
) is absolutely convergent for every
f_{r}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\mathcal{M}_{r}])
. Moreover, the map
f_{r}\in\mathcal{T}_{N}([\mathcal{M}_{r}])\mapsto Z_{r}(f_{r},s)
is continuous.

We now unfold the Rankin–Selberg integral using the partial Zeta functions. Recall that we write
R
for the action by right-translation on spaces of functions.

Proposition 7.8
.

For every
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
we have

\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\int_{K_{H}}Z_{r}(P_{r}(R(k)f),0)dk.

(7.11)

Proof.

Note that the
Z_{r}(P_{r}(R(k)f),0)
are all well-defined by Lemma
7.6
and Lemma
7.7
. We now claim that (
7.11
) is proved in the course of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 7.2.0.2]
. Indeed,
[
11
]
shows that there exists
c>0
such that for every
\Re(s)\geq c
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])
we have the equality

\int_{[H]}f(h)\left|h\right|^{s}dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{N}_{r,H}(\mathbb{A})\backslash\mathcal{M}_{r,H}(\mathbb{A})}\int_{K_{H}}\int_{[\mathbf{M}_{r}]}\delta_{P_{r,H}}^{-1}(h_{r}m_{r})\left|h_{r}m_{r}\right|^{s}W_{r,R(m_{r}k)f_{P_{r}}}(h_{r})dm_{r}dh_{r}dk,

where the integrals are absolutely convergent. For all
m_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})\cap H(\mathbb{A})
we have
\delta_{P_{r},H}(m_{r})=\delta_{P_{r}}^{1/2}(m_{r})
. Therefore, (
7.11
) follows by applying this result to the Schwartz function
g\mapsto f(g)\left|g\right|^{-c}
. Note that
[
11
]
assumes that
f
belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{\chi}([G])
for some regular cuspidal datum
\chi
(see
[
11
, Section 2.9.7]
). However, this assumption is only used to show that the terms attached to
r>0
vanish in (
7.11
), which we do not claim here.
∎

7.2.3
Sectral unfolding for partial periods of constant terms

We keep
0\leq r\leq n
and write the spectral expansion of the twisted partial diagonal period
P_{r}
from (
7.9
). We consider
\Pi_{r}
the set of couples
(P,\pi)
such that the following conditions are satisfied.

•

P
is a standard parabolic subgroup of
G
contained in
P_{r}
such that
\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}\cap P
is of the form
\mathbf{P}^{2}
. We write
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{M}_{r}\cap P
.

•

\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
decomposes as

\pi=(\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\pi_{\mathcal{P}}.

We embed
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}
diagonally into
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}^{2}}
and further into
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}
. We write
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}
for the anti-diagonal copy of
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}
in
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}^{2}}^{*}
. As in §
7.2.1
, relatively to
\mathfrak{a}_{P}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}^{2}}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
we define

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=((1/4,0),(1/4,0)),\quad\underline{z}_{r}=((0,1/4),(0,1/4)).

Then
\underline{\rho}_{\pi}\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}
.

For every
g_{r}=(g_{r,n},g_{r,n+1})\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
, we have the continuous linear form

\varphi=\varphi_{n}\otimes\varphi_{n+1}\in\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi}(M_{r})\mapsto\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{g_{r}}\right)(\varphi):=\langle\varphi_{n}(\cdot\;g_{r,n}),\overline{\varphi}_{n+1}(\cdot\;g_{r,n+1})\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}},

where the notations
\varphi_{n}(\cdot\;g_{r,n})
and
\varphi_{n+1}(\cdot\;g_{r,n+1})
mean that we consider the restrictions of these automorphic forms to the two copies of
M_{\mathbf{P}}
. For
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi}(M_{r})
, we can consider the automorphic form

\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(\varphi):g_{r}\mapsto\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\otimes\mathrm{ev}_{g_{r}}\right)(\varphi).

It belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathcal{M}_{r})
.

If now
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)
, we set

\varphi_{M}:m\in M_{r}(\mathbb{A})\mapsto\delta_{P_{r}}^{-1/2}(m)\varphi(m).

Then
\varphi_{M}\in\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi}(M_{r})
. By composing with the previous map, we obtain

\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi}(G)\mapsto\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(\varphi_{M})\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathcal{M}_{r})

Lemma 7.9
.

There exists
c>0
such that for every
t>c
and
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
we have

\displaystyle P_{r}(f)(g_{r})=

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{r}}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})\right|^{-1}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})\right|^{-1}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}\left(g_{r},\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(\varphi_{M}),\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\right)d\lambda,

(7.12)

for every
g_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
f=f_{n}\otimes f_{n+1}
as both sides of (
7.9
) define continuous linear forms on
\mathcal{S}([G])
by Lemma
7.6
for the LHS, and Propositions
4.27
and
4.28
as well as (
4.39
) for the RHS. Indeed, because
t
is large the region of integration does not meet the possible singularities of Eisenstein series by Theorem
4.17
. We denote by
\rho_{\mathbf{P}_{r},H}
the restriction of
\rho_{P_{r},H}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}_{r}}
which we identify with an element of
\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}_{r}}^{*}
. Let
g_{r}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{A})
. By the first assertion of Lemma
7.6
and Langlands’ spectral decomposition of the inner product of Schwartz functions (Proposition
4.30
), we have

\displaystyle P_{r}(f)(g_{r})=\delta_{P_{r}}^{-1/2}(g_{r})\sum_{\mathbf{P}\subset\mathbf{P}_{r}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{\mathbf{P}})}\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{P}^{2},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee}}(J)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}+2\rho_{\mathbf{P}_{r},H}}\langle R(g_{r})f_{P_{r}},E^{\mathbf{M}_{r}}(\phi,-\overline{\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}})\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}\langle\phi_{n},\overline{\phi}_{n+1}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}d\lambda_{\mathbf{P}},

where we write again
J
for the level instead of
J\cap\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}
. Note that here we artificially add the Petersson inner product
\langle\phi_{n},\overline{\phi}_{n+1}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}
to sum over
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{P}^{2},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee}}(J)
rather than
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{P},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}}(J)
.

Let
N>0
be such that Langlands’ spectral decomposition holds for functions in
\mathcal{T}_{-N}([\mathcal{M}_{r}])
(Corollary
4.31
). Up to enlarging it, we can also assume that the theorem holds for functions in
\mathcal{T}_{-N}([\mathbf{M}_{r}^{2}])
. Take
c_{N}>0
as in Lemma
7.6
as well as
t>4c_{N}
. For every
\mathbf{P}
,
\pi_{\mathbf{P}}
and
\phi
we can therefore write the spectral expansion of
g_{r}\mapsto\langle R(g_{r})f_{P_{r}},E^{\mathbf{M}_{r}}(\phi,-\overline{\lambda_{\mathbf{P}}})\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}\left|g_{r}\right|^{-t/4}
. By absorbing this twist in the integral, we see that

\displaystyle P_{r}(f)(g_{r})=\delta_{P_{r}}^{-1/2}(g_{r})\sum_{\mathbf{P}\subset\mathbf{P}_{r}}\sum_{\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{\mathbf{P}})}\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{P}^{2},\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee}}(J)}\sum_{\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{P}_{r}}\sum_{\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{\mathcal{P}})}\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P},\pi_{\mathcal{P}}}(J)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}+2\rho_{\mathbf{P}_{r},H}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\langle f_{P_{r}},E^{M_{r}}(\phi\otimes\phi^{\prime},-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}\langle\phi_{n},\overline{\phi}_{n+1}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}(g_{r},\phi^{\prime},\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})d\lambda,

(7.13)

where we write
\phi\otimes\phi^{\prime}
for the product of
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
seen as an automorphic form on
M_{r}
. Note that thanks to our bounds on Eisenstein series from Propositions
4.27
and
4.28
and (
4.39
), we know that this integral is absolutely convergent and therefore that we can switch the order of the sums.

Take
\mathbf{P}
,
\pi_{\mathbf{P}}
,
\mathcal{P}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
as in (
7.2.3
). Set
P=(\mathbf{P}^{2}\times\mathcal{P})N_{P_{r}}
and
\pi=(\pi_{\mathbf{P}}\boxtimes\pi_{\mathbf{P}}^{\vee})\boxtimes\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\in\Pi_{\mathrm{disc}}(M_{P})
. Then
(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{r}
. Take
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}+2\rho_{\mathbf{P}_{r},H}+t\underline{z}_{r}
. By Proposition
4.27
and Lemma
7.6
, we see that the series

f_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi}(J)}\langle f_{P_{r}},E^{M_{r}}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{\mathbf{M}_{r}}\varphi_{\lambda}

(7.14)

is absolutely convergent in
\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}(M_{r})^{J}
. Because
g\in[G]\mapsto(R(g)f)_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{P,\pi_{\lambda},-\rho_{P_{r}}}(G)
, we have

f_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle F,\varphi\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}\varphi_{M,\lambda}.

For fixed
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)
, we check by undolding the integrals, taking into account the twist by
\rho_{P_{r}}
and using (
7.14
), that

\langle F,\varphi\rangle_{P,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle f_{P_{r}},E^{M_{r}}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\rho_{P_{r}})\rangle_{P_{r}}.

It follows that

f_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle f_{P_{r}},E^{M_{r}}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\rho_{P_{r}})\rangle_{P_{r}}\varphi_{M,\lambda}

(7.15)

Because
t\gg 0
, the Eisenstein series over
P_{r}(F)\backslash G(F)
of
E^{M_{r}}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\rho_{P_{r}})
is absolutely convergent. Therefore, by adjunction of the constant term and Eisenstein series, we may rewrite (
7.15
) as

f_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda}+\rho_{P_{r}})\rangle_{G}\varphi_{M,\lambda}

(7.16)

Once again, by Proposition
4.27
this sum is absolutely convergent in
\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}(M_{r})^{J}
. By Proposition
4.28
, we know that the linear form

\mathcal{A}_{P\cap M_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}(M_{r})^{J}\mapsto E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}\left(g_{r},\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(\varphi),\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\right)

is continuous. By comparing the two expressions of
f_{P_{r},\pi_{\lambda}}
obtained in (
7.14
) and (
7.16
), we conclude that (
7.9
) holds once we note that

\underline{\rho}_{\pi}=(\rho_{P_{r}})_{|\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}}-2\rho_{\mathbf{P}_{r},H},

which follows from the definition.
∎

7.2.4
End of the proof of Proposition
7.4

We can now end the proof of Proposition
7.4
. Let
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
. Let
c>0
be given by Lemma
7.9
. By Propositions
4.27
and
4.28
as well as (
4.39
), there exists
N>0
such that for every
t>4c
the integral

\displaystyle g_{r}\in[\mathcal{M}_{r}]\mapsto\left|\det g_{r}\right|^{-t/4}

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{r}}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})\right|^{-1}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})\right|^{-1}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}\left(g_{r},\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(\varphi_{M}),\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\right)d\lambda,

is absolutely convergent in
\mathcal{T}_{-N}(\mathcal{M}_{r})
. Assume moreover that
t>4c_{N}
where
c_{N}
is given by Lemma
7.7
. By Lemma
7.7
, Proposition
7.8
and a change of variables, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{r}}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})\right|^{-1}\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})\right|^{-1}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\int_{K_{H}}Z_{r}(\left(E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}\left(\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(R(k)\varphi_{M}),\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\right),0\right)dkd\lambda.

(7.17)

By Proposition
5.7
, we know that the Zeta integral
Z_{r}
is zero on automorphic representations induced from the residual spectrum. Therefore, the term attached to
(P,\pi)
in (
7.2.4
) is zero as soon as
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\notin\Pi_{\mathrm{cusp}}(M_{\mathcal{P}})
so that the sum takes place over triples
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
.

For
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
, by parabolic descent (Proposition
5.10
) and by the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
in terms of regularized period in (
6.32
), we have for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)
the formula

\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)=\int_{K_{H}}Z_{r}(\left(E^{\mathcal{M}_{r}}\left(\left(\langle\cdot,\overline{\cdot}\rangle_{\mathbf{P},\mathrm{Pet}}\right)(R(k)\varphi_{M}),\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}\right),0\right)dk.

Thanks to the description given in §
7.2.1
, we also know that
i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbf{P}}^{*,-}\oplus i\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
is nothing but
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
and that
\left|W(\pi)\right|=\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathbf{P}})\right|\left|\mathcal{P}(M_{\mathcal{P}})\right|
. This concludes the proof of Proposition
7.4
.

7.3
Additional residues from discrete Eisenstein series

For the rest of the proof of Theorem
7.1
we fix a Schwartz function
f\in\mathcal{S}([G])^{J}
. We now compute a second spectral expansion for
\int_{[H]}f(h)dh
, building on the one from Proposition
7.4
. It is obtained by shifting the contour in the integrals of (
7.7
). Doing so, we will cross some singularities of the integrand which all come from the Eisenstein series
E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})
.

7.3.1
Statement of the result

In this section, we take
0\leq r\leq n
and
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. Our goal is to find a new intermediate expression for

I_{\pi}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.18)

We have the following functional equation for
I_{\pi}
.

Lemma 7.10
.

For any
w\in W(\pi)
, we have
I_{\pi}=I_{w\pi}
.

Proof.

Note that
w
sends
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{w\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{w\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
. The result now follows from the functional equation of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}^{\uparrow}
(Lemma
6.10
) and of Eisenstein series (Theorem
4.17
) using a change of variables in
\varphi
and
\lambda
.
∎

Write
\pi=\pi_{n}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1}
as

\pi_{n}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i},\quad\pi_{n+1}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i},

(7.19)

where the
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
are cuspidal and the
\pi_{+,i}
discrete. We furthermore write
\pi_{+,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{+,i},\underline{d}(+,i))
for each
i
. By Lemma
7.10
, we may assume that
\underline{d}(+,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(+,m_{+})
. We also define
\pi_{+,i}^{+}:=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{+,i},\underline{d}(+,i)+1)
.

In order to state our intermediate formula, we will need some combinatorial gadgets. We denote by
\mathcal{G}(\pi)
the set of undirected simple graphs
\Gamma
such that

•

the vertices of
\Gamma
are
\pi_{+,1},\ldots,\pi_{+,m_{+}}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,1},\ldots,\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,m_{\mathrm{c},1}},\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,1},\ldots,\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,m_{\mathrm{c},2}}
(with multiplicity);

•

the edges of
\Gamma
are of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\}
with
\sigma_{+,i}\simeq\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
, or
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\}
with
\sigma_{+,i}^{\vee}\simeq\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
;

•

each
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
has degree at most one;

•

each
\pi_{+,1},\ldots,\pi_{+,m_{+}}
has degree at most two, and if it is two then the neighbors are some
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
(they can not be
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
or
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
).

For each
i
, we denote by
\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)
(resp.
\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)
) the degree of
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
(resp.
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
), and by
\deg(+,1,i)
(resp.
\deg(+,2,i)
) the number of neighbors of the form
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
(resp.
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
) of
\pi_{+,i}
. These integers are all either zero or one.

If
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
, we can define a discrete representation
\pi_{\Gamma}
of some standard Levi
M_{Q_{\Gamma}}
by

\displaystyle\pi_{\Gamma,n}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\deg(+,1,i)=0\\
\deg(+,2,i)=0\end{subarray}}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\deg(+,2,i)=1\\
\deg(+,1,i)=0\end{subarray}}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}\underset{\deg(+,1,i)=1}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}^{+}\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,i},

(7.20)

\displaystyle\pi_{\Gamma,n+1}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\deg(+,1,i)=0\\
\deg(+,2,i)=0\end{subarray}}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\deg(+,1,i)=1\\
\deg(+,2,i)=0\end{subarray}}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}\underset{\deg(+,2,i)=1}{\boxtimes}\pi_{+,i}^{+,\vee}.\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,i}

(7.21)

Here we impose that the representations appear in the order
i=1,2,\ldots
for
i
in each set.
Set

\displaystyle I_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\deg(+,1,i)=0\\
\deg(+,2,i)=0\end{subarray}}\underline{n}(+,i),\sum_{\deg(+,1,i)=1}(\underline{n}(+,i)+\underline{r}(+,i)),\sum_{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)}\underline{n}(\mathrm{c},1,i),\right.

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{\deg(+,2,i)=1}(\underline{n}(+,i)+\underline{r}(+,i)),\sum_{\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)}\underline{n}(\mathrm{c},2,i),0\right).

Finally, set

I_{\Gamma,1}=I_{\Gamma,2}=\{i\;|\;\deg(+,1,i)=\deg(+,2,i)=1\}

Then up to some evident identifications, we have
(I_{\Gamma},Q_{\Gamma},\pi_{\Gamma},I_{\Gamma,1},I_{\Gamma,2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. This holds because we have assumed that
\underline{d}(+,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(+,m_{+})
, and because the tensor products in (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) are taken with respect to the natural order on the index
i
.

We define
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
to be the image of the map
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)\mapsto(I_{\Gamma},R_{\Gamma},\pi_{\Gamma},I_{\Gamma,1},I_{\Gamma,2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. Note that its fibers can be of cardinal strictly bigger than
1
. Moreover,
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
with the null graph being its sole preimage. If
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
, we can decompose
\tau
as in (
6.26
) and (
6.27
). To differentiate from
\pi
, we write
\tau_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i}(\tau),\underline{d}(\tau,1,i))
,
\tau_{2,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{2,i}(\tau),\underline{d}(\tau,2,i))
and
\tau_{+,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{+,i}(\tau),\underline{d}(\tau,+,i))
. The definition of
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
imposes that the
\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)
and
\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)
are strictly greater than
1
.

Let
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
. We have defined in §
6.2.3
a triple
(I^{\downarrow},Q^{\downarrow},\tau^{\downarrow})\in\Pi_{H}
. We can therefore also consider
W(\tau^{\downarrow})
and denote by
\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)
the stabilizer of
\tau^{\downarrow}
in this set. Because we have fixed the order of the representations in (
7.20
) and (
7.21
), the fiber of the map

(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})\in\Pi^{\uparrow}_{H}(\pi)\mapsto(I^{{}^{\prime},\downarrow},Q^{{}^{\prime},\downarrow},\tau^{{}^{\prime},\downarrow})\in\Pi_{H}

(7.22)

above the set
W(\tau^{\downarrow}).\tau^{\downarrow}
is reduced to
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})
.

We have
M_{Q}\subset(\mathrm{GL}_{n-n_{\mathrm{c},1}^{\prime}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{\mathrm{c},1}^{\prime}})\times(\mathrm{GL}_{n+1-n_{\mathrm{c},2}^{\prime}}\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{\mathrm{c},2}^{\prime}})
. Under this decomposition, let
\underline{z}_{\tau}
be the element

\underline{z}_{\tau}=((0,1/4),(0,1/4))\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}\subset\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*}.

(7.23)

Therefore,
\underline{z}_{\tau}
only lives above the
\boxtimes\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\boxtimes\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
. If
Q=P
, this is
\underline{z}_{r}
. Finally, recall that we have defined some elements
\underline{\rho}_{\tau}
and
\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
in (
6.30
) and (
6.31
) respectively. We can now state our intermediate result.

Proposition 7.11
.

We have

I_{\pi}=\sum_{(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)}\frac{|\mathrm{Stab}(\pi)|}{|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.24)

We will end the proof of Proposition
7.11
in §
7.3.9
.

Remark 7.12
.

The appearance of
2
is somewhat artificial and we may replace it by any real number
t
such that
1<t<3
. It will be proved in the course of the proof that (
7.24
) is well-defined, that is that
\sum_{\varphi}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
is regular and of rapid decay in the region of integration.

7.3.2
A short description of the argument

Before starting the proof of Proposition
7.11
, let us give a short survey of the argument. The idea is to use contour shifting in the integral (
7.18
) defining
I_{\pi}
to bring the domain of integration from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+2\underline{z}_{r}
. This will be done by a step by step process to gradually decrease
t
, which amounts to crossing vertical strips. It takes the form of an induction argument which is the content of §
7.3.3
. Inside each strip we will encounter poles of the integrand. These singularities are described in §
7.3.4
. It turns out that they all come from the Eisenstein series
E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})
. More precisely, they occur when, with the notation of (
7.19
), a segment corresponding to a
\pi_{+,i}
(resp.
\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}
) can be linked with a
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
(resp.
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
). Because the latter is cuspidal, this can only happen if they are juxtaposed. We therefore obtain a new residual representation
\tau
. The residue of our integrand along such singularity is computed in §
7.3.5
: it is a relative character
I_{\tau}
of
f
along the induction of
\tau
to
G
. It then remains to shift the contour in (
7.18
) in a way such that the singularities that our contours of integration cross are always simple. This allows us to carry out the computation only by using the one-dimensional residue theorem. This technical step is done first on the variables coming from the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side in §
7.3.6
and then from the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side in §
7.3.7
.

To keep track of all the residues we catch along the way, we use the graph-theoretic formalism described above. Its meaning is the following: each time we cross a singularity coming from a juxtaposition between
\pi_{+,i}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
(or
\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
), we draw an edge between the two corresponding vertices. The restraints we impose on our graphs
\Gamma
mean that such residue can only occur at most once per
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}
and
\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
, and at most twice per
\pi_{+,i}
/
\pi_{+,i}^{\vee}
, in which case one singularity comes from
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
and the other from
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
. At the level of the associated tuple
(I_{\Gamma},Q_{\Gamma},\pi_{\Gamma},I_{\Gamma,1},I_{\Gamma,2})
, these double residues are remembered in
I_{\Gamma,1}
and
I_{\Gamma,2}
. Using this formalism, we are able to write
I_{\pi}
as a weighted sum of relative characters
I_{\pi_{\Gamma}}
indexed by graphs in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
. It then remains to use a combinatorial argument to rather express it as sum over tuples in
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
. This is the content of §
7.3.8
. Once this is done, the proof of Proposition
7.11
is complete.

7.3.3
The successive changes of contours

The proof of Proposition
7.11
is quite involved and will take the reminder of this section. We will freely use the notation for the coordinate of an element
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
from §
6.2.5
. We start from the definition of
I_{\pi}
given in (
7.18
). The integral takes place in the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
for some
t>0
large enough. In particular,
t/2>\underline{d}(+,i)
for all
1\leq i\leq m_{+}
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. We denote by
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)
the subset of
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
consisting of the tuples
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})
such that we have
\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)>d
and
\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)>d
for all
i
. Proposition
7.11
now follows from the next proposition applied to
d=1
.

Proposition 7.13
.

For all
1\leq d\leq\lceil{t/2}\rceil
,
I_{\pi}
is equal to

\sum_{(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)}\frac{|\mathrm{Stab}(\pi)|}{|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2d\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.25)

We will prove Proposition
7.13
by decreasing induction starting at
d=\lceil{t/2}\rceil
. The proof will take us until the end of the section and will be broken down in several lemmas. It will end in §
7.3.9
. We begin with the initialization step of the induction.

Lemma 7.14
.

Proposition
7.13
holds for
d=\lceil{t/2}\rceil
.

Proof.

By definition, the only element in
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,\lceil{t/2}\rceil)
is
(I_{r},P,\pi)
. To conclude, we have to move the contour from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+t\underline{z}_{r}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi}+2\lceil{t/2}\rceil\underline{z}_{r}
, which is possible by Proposition
7.4
.
∎

In what follows we explain how to prove the induction step. The relevant integral will be the following. For any
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d+1)
, set

I_{\tau,d+1}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.26)

That this integral is well defined will follow from Lemma
7.15
.

7.3.4
Poles

We want to move the contour in the integral (
7.26
). To achieve this, we begin by studying the poles of the integrand. However, it will be useful to do this for a larger class of representations. For now, we let
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)
. We will work with the following region:

\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}:=\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+\nu\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\tau,k,c_{J}},\;\nu\in[2d\underline{z}_{\tau},2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau}]\right\},

(7.27)

where we take
k
and
c_{J}
given by Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
6.11
. Moreover, we may assume that
\mathcal{S}_{\tau,k,c_{J}}\subset\{\lambda\;|\;\left\lVert\Re(\lambda)\right\rVert<\varepsilon\}
for
\varepsilon
small.

Lemma 7.15
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)
, the possible poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
of (
7.27
) are along the zeros of the polynomial

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{+,i}(\tau)\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau,+,j)=d\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{+,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau,+,i)=d\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{2,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)=d+1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{1,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)=d+1\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right).

(7.28)

Moreover, it is of rapid decay in the sense that if we denote by
L
the polynomial in (
7.28
), for all
N>0
there exists
C>0
such that for all
\lambda
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\left|L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\right|\leq\frac{C}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N}}.

(7.29)

In particular, the map
\lambda\mapsto\sum_{\varphi}L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.19
, we investigate the behavior of
L_{\tau,0}(-\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,L_{\tau,0}^{-1}(-\overline{\lambda})E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
, where we recall that
L_{\tau,0}
is the polynomial cutting out the zeros of the Eisenstein series.

We begin with
L_{\tau,0}(-\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
. First, we claim that our region is contained in the set (
6.39
) of Proposition
6.11
. With the notation of §
6.2.5
, we have to show that for any
\nu
as in (
7.27
),
(w^{\uparrow}(-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+\nu)+\nu^{\uparrow}))_{\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}}
is positive. To prove this, say on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side, it is enough to note that the vector

\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,1,1)-1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,1,m_{1})-1}{2},\frac{d+1}{2},\ldots,\frac{d+1}{2}\right)

is positive. This holds because
\underline{d}(\tau,1,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(\tau,1,m_{1})>d
, and the same argument works equally well on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side.

Therefore, the poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
are controlled by
L_{\tau,\mathcal{P}}^{\uparrow}
. By comparing (
4.31
) (definition of
L_{\tau,0}
) and (
6.38
) (definition of
L_{\tau,\mathcal{P}}
), we see that the poles of the product are contained in the zeros of

\displaystyle\prod_{\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}\pm\frac{1}{2}\right)\times\prod_{\sigma_{2,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}}\left(\lambda(2)_{i}+\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}+\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)-1\pm 1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma_{1,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}+\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)-1\pm 1}{2}\right).

We claim that this product is non zero in our region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
. Indeed, for the first factor we always have
\Re(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j})\geq d-2\varepsilon\geq 1-2\varepsilon>0
. For the second, we have
\Re(\lambda(2)_{i}+\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j})\geq d/2+1/4-2\varepsilon\geq 3/4-2\varepsilon
, while
(\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)-1\pm 1)/2\geq-1/2
, and the same argument works for the third. Therefore,
L_{\tau,0}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
is regular in
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
.

We now study
L_{\tau,0}^{-1}(-\lambda)E(\varphi,-\lambda)
. It is enough to investigate the meromorphic functions
L_{\tau_{n},0}^{-1}(-\lambda_{n})E(\varphi_{n},-\lambda_{n})
and
L_{\tau_{n+1},0}^{-1}(-\lambda_{n+1})E(\varphi_{n+1},-\lambda_{n+1})
separately, so that we only explain the first case. Because we always have
\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}-\nu\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{Q}^{*,+}}
, the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,k,c_{J}}
and we can use Corollary
4.19
. It follows that the poles of
L_{\tau_{n},0}^{-1}(-\lambda_{n})E(\varphi_{n},-\lambda_{n})
are controlled by
L_{\tau_{n},E}
.

By going back to the definition in (
4.29
), we see that most factors will immediately be non-zero in our region. Indeed, two cases can be easily excluded. The first is the possible poles coming from isomorphisms
\sigma_{+,i}(\tau)\simeq\sigma_{2,j}(\tau)^{\vee}
. Then the shifted segments associated to the representations
\tau_{+,i}
and
\tau_{2,j}^{-,\vee}
can not be linked for
\lambda
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
in the sense of §
4.4.1
because

\Re(\lambda(+)_{n,i})\approx\Re(\lambda(2)_{n,j})\approx 1/4
(up to
\varepsilon
). It is indeed straightforward that two segments with almost the same mean can not be linked. The other easy situation is
\sigma_{+,i}(\tau)\simeq\sigma_{1,j}(\tau)
or
\sigma_{2,k}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\sigma_{1,j}(\tau)
. Indeed, in both cases we have
\Re(\lambda(+)_{n,i})\approx\Re(\lambda(2)_{n,k})\approx 1/4
and
\Re(\lambda(1)_{n,j})\simeq-1/4
. But it is straightforward that two segments whose means differ by almost
1/2
can not be linked.

Therefore, the only possible poles are contained in the zeros of

\displaystyle\prod_{\sigma_{1,i}(\tau)\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{2,i}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(2)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)}{2}\right)\prod_{\sigma_{+,i}(\tau)\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j}}\left(\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{\underline{d}(\tau,+,i)+1}{2}\right).

(7.30)

Note that the change of signs in the second equation is due to the fact that in our coordinates we have
\lambda(2)_{n,i}=-\lambda(2)_{i}
(see §
6.2.1
). The first product is non-zero in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
because
\Re(\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j})\leq-1/4+(d+1)/2<(\underline{d}(\tau,1,i)+1)/2
. For the second, the same argument shows that the zeros come from the terms with
\underline{d}(\tau,2,i)=d+1
, and for the third from
\underline{d}(\tau,+,i)=d
.

By repeating the argument on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
side, we finally see that the only possible poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}
are indeed along the zeros of the polynomial in (
7.28
).

To conclude the proof of Lemma
7.15
, it remains to prove that
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
is of rapid decay in the sense of (
7.29
). This is a direct consequence of Propositions
4.27
and
6.13
.
∎

7.3.5
Computation of residues

Assume now that
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)
comes from a graph
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
. We now explain how to assign to each affine linear form
\Lambda
appearing in (
7.28
) a graph
\Gamma_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
.

Let us explain the case
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i_{+}}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i_{1}}-(d+1)/2
. By definition of
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
, we have indices
j_{+}
and
j_{1}
such that
\tau_{+,i_{+}}=\pi_{+,j_{+}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i_{1}}=\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j_{1}}
. Note that these indices
j_{+}
and
j_{1}
are uniquely determined if we ask that they correspond exactly to those given by the presentation
\tau
as
\pi(\Gamma)
given in (
7.20
). Let
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
be the graph obtained from
\Gamma
by adding the edge
\{\pi_{+,j_{+}},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j_{1}}\}
. Then we have
\Gamma_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
. For simplicity, we will write
Q_{\Lambda}
instead of
Q_{\Gamma_{\Lambda}}
,
\pi_{\Lambda}
instead of
\pi_{\Gamma_{\Lambda}}
and so on.

For
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i_{+}}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-(d+1)/2
, the graph
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
is built the same way. Let us explain the last case that will be relevant to us which is
\Lambda(\lambda)=-\lambda(1)_{i_{1}}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-(d+1)/2
with
\sigma_{1,i_{1}}(\tau)^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i_{2}}
,
\underline{d}(\tau,1,i_{1})=d+1
and
i_{1}\notin I_{1}
. As before, define
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
such that
\tau_{1,i_{1}}^{-,\vee}=\pi_{+,j_{1}}^{\vee}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i_{2}}=\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j_{2}}
. Then we add the edge
\{\pi_{+,j_{1}},\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j_{2}}\}
. Note that
\pi_{+,j_{1}}
already had degree
1
as it was connected to the cuspidal representation
\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,l}
used to obtain
\tau_{1,i_{1}}
on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side.

We now describe the residues obtained along the zeros of (
7.28
). Note that they are all at most simple and that we may use the naive notion of residue described in §
5.5.1
. Once again, we begin with the case
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i_{+}}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-(d+1)/2
. There exists a unique integer
1\leq k\leq m_{1}(\tau)
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\Lambda,n}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{+}\end{subarray}}^{m_{+}(\tau)}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{2}(\tau)}\tau_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k-1}\tau_{1,i}\boxtimes\pi_{+,j_{+}}^{+}\boxtimes_{i=k}^{m_{1}(\tau)}\tau_{1,i}\right)\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}(\tau)}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i},

\displaystyle\pi_{\Lambda,n+1}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{+}\end{subarray}}^{m_{+}(\tau)}\tau_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{+,j_{+}}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=k\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{1}(\tau)}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\right)\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}(\tau)}\tau_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}(\tau)}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}.

Here we need the integer
k
because we have imposed that the representations in (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) appear in a certain order. Associated to
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
is a regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}
.

Let
w\in W(Q)
such that
w\tau
is the representation with
(w\tau)_{n+1}=\pi_{\Lambda,n+1}
and

(w\tau)_{n}=\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{+}\end{subarray}}^{m_{+}(\tau)}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{2}(\tau)}\tau_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k-1}\tau_{1,i}\boxtimes(\tau_{+,i_{+}}\boxtimes\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i_{1}})\boxtimes_{i=k}^{m_{1}(\tau)}\tau_{1,i}\right)\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}(\tau)}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}.

(7.31)

Note that

w\left(\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap(\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow})\right)=\mathfrak{a}_{\pi_{\Lambda},\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\Lambda}}-\underline{\rho}_{\pi_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}-\nu_{w.Q,\pi_{\Lambda}}^{Q_{\Lambda}},

(7.32)

where
\nu_{w.Q,\pi_{\Lambda}}^{Q_{\Lambda}}
is the twist defined in (
4.5
). Moreover, up to shrinking the constants we have

\lambda\in\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap\mathcal{R}_{\tau,d+1}^{\uparrow}\implies w\lambda+\nu_{w.Q,\pi_{\Lambda}}^{Q_{\Lambda}}\in\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\Lambda},d+1}^{\uparrow}.

(7.33)

If
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i_{+}}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-(d+1)/2
, the construction is exactly the same by on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side. In the case
\Lambda(\lambda)=-\lambda(1)_{i_{1}}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-(d+1)/2
, we have
\pi_{\Lambda,n}=\tau_{n}
and

\pi_{\Lambda,n+1}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}(\tau)}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{i\notin I_{1}\cup\{i_{1}\}}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k-1}\tau_{2,i}\boxtimes\tau_{1,i_{1}}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{i=k}^{m_{2}(\tau)}\tau_{2,i}\right)\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}(\tau)}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i},

for some index
k
. In this case, let
w\in W(R_{n+1})
such that

(w\tau)_{n+1}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}(\tau)}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{i\notin I_{1}\cup\{i_{1}\}}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k-1}\tau_{2,i}\boxtimes\left(\tau_{1,i_{1}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i_{2}}\right)\boxtimes_{i=k}^{m_{2}(\tau)}\tau_{2,i}\right)\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq i_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}(\tau)}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}

Then the relations (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) still hold.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
\Lambda
be any affine linear form appearing in (
7.28
). For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap(\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow})
in general position we have

\underset{\Lambda}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\mu)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\mu})\rangle_{G}\right)(\lambda)=-\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q_{\Lambda},\pi_{\Lambda}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda^{\prime})\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda^{\prime}})\rangle_{G},

(7.34)

where
\lambda^{\prime}=w\lambda+\nu_{w.Q,\pi_{\Lambda}}^{Q_{\Lambda}}
.

Proof.

We only deal with the case
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i_{+}}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i_{1}}-(d+1)/2
, the others being exactly the same. For simplicity, write
R=Q_{\Lambda}
and
\delta=\pi_{\Lambda}
.

That the residue is well-defined in our naive sense follows from Lemma
7.15
. By the unitarity of the intertwining operators and the functional equation of Eisenstein series (Theorem
4.17
), we see using a change of variabless and analytic continuation that for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{Q,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in general position we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{w.Q,w\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}\left(M(w^{-1},w\lambda)\varphi,\lambda\right)\langle f,E(\varphi,-w\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}.

Because we know the poles of
M(w^{-1},w\lambda)
(Proposition
4.11
) and of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
(Proposition
6.11
), we conclude that
\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})
is not contained in any of the singularities of
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}\left(M(w^{-1},w\lambda)\varphi,\lambda\right)
in
\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
. Moreover, by Lemma
7.15
, using
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
we can compute the residue inside the sum
\sum_{\varphi}
and the inner-product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{G}
. It follows that the residue in (
7.34
) is
