\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{w.Q,w\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}\left(M(w^{-1},w\lambda)\varphi,\lambda\right)\langle f,\underset{\overline{\Lambda}}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(E(\varphi,-w\overline{\mu})\right)(\lambda)\rangle_{G}.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{w.Q,w\tau}(G)
. By yet another use of
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
, we see that we have

\underset{\overline{\Lambda}}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(E(\varphi,-w\overline{\mu})\right)(\lambda)=-E\left(E^{R,*}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda^{\prime}}-\nu_{w.Q,\delta}^{R})\right).

where we have by definition of
\lambda^{\prime}

E^{R,*}(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda^{\prime}}-\nu_{w.Q,\delta}^{R})=-\underset{\overline{\Lambda}}{\mathrm{Res}}(E^{R}(\varphi,-w\overline{\mu}))(\lambda).

Note that
(w.Q)_{w\tau}=R_{\delta}
. Let
\sigma_{w\tau}
be the cuspidal representation of
M_{R_{\delta}}
defined in §
4.1.2
, so that there exists
\phi\in\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{w\tau},R_{\delta}}(G)
with
\varphi=E^{w.Q,*}(\phi,-\nu_{w\tau})
. Then by the definition of
E^{R,*}
given in (
4.3
), and because
\nu_{w\tau}+\nu_{w.Q,\delta}^{R}=\nu_{\delta}
, we have

E^{R,*}(\varphi,-\nu_{w.Q,\delta}^{R})=E^{R,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\delta}).

In particular, for every
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{Q,\delta}(G)
by Proposition
4.4
we get

\langle\varphi^{\prime},E^{R,*}(\varphi,-\nu_{w.Q,\delta}^{R})\rangle_{R,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\varphi^{\prime},E^{R,*}(\phi,-\nu_{\delta})\rangle_{R,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\varphi^{\prime}_{R_{\delta}},\phi\rangle_{R_{\delta},\mathrm{Pet}},

but also by transitivity of constant term

\langle\varphi^{\prime}_{w.Q},\varphi\rangle_{w.Q,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\varphi^{\prime}_{w.Q},E^{w.Q,*}(\phi,-\nu_{w\tau})\rangle_{w.Q,\mathrm{Pet}}=\langle\varphi^{\prime}_{R_{\delta}},\phi\rangle_{R_{\delta},\mathrm{Pet}}.

It follows that the regularized Eisenstein series
E^{R,*}
(defined on
\mathcal{A}_{w.Q,w\tau}
) and the constant term along
w.Q
(defined on
\mathcal{A}_{R,\delta}(G)
) are adjoint. This implies that the residue in (
7.34
) is

-\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)}\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}_{\tau}\left(M(w^{-1},\lambda^{\prime})\varphi_{w.Q},w^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}\right)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda^{\prime}})\rangle_{G}.

Let
P_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
,
P_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
and
w_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
,
w_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
respectively be the Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup and element of the Weyl group defined for
\delta
and
\tau
(or rather the associated tuples) in §
6.2.1
and §
6.2.2
. By going back to their definitions, we see that there exist
w_{\mathcal{M}}\in W_{\mathcal{M}}(\tau)
and
w_{\mathbf{M}}\in W_{\mathbf{M}}(\tau)
such that
w_{\mathcal{M}}w_{\mathbf{M}}w^{\uparrow}_{\tau}=w^{\uparrow}_{\delta}w
(see §
6.2.4
) and that moreover
w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{\tau}^{\uparrow}=P_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
. By (
3.6
), the functional equation of regularized period proved in Lemma
6.10
and the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
in (
6.32
), we obtain

\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}_{\tau}\left(M(w^{-1},\lambda^{\prime})\varphi_{w.Q},w^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{P}^{w_{\mathbf{M}}.P_{\tau}^{\uparrow}}\left(M(w_{\delta}^{\uparrow},\lambda)\varphi_{P_{\delta}^{\uparrow}},w_{\delta}^{\uparrow}\lambda^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda^{\prime}).

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

To prove Lemma
7.16
, we crucially use the fact that, up to a change of variabless, our singularities comes from a juxtaposition of two segments of
\tau
(in the sense of §
4.4.1
). In this case, the image spanned by the residues of Eisenstein series is the space of Eisenstein series induced from the discrete automorphic representation obtained by juxtaposing the two corresponding components of
\tau
. For the other singularities, computing the residues is a hard problem (see e.g.
[
26
]
).

7.3.6
Change of contours on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side

We now fix
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d+1)
and assume that it comes from a graph
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
. We begin our change of contours in the integral
I_{\tau,d+1}
of (
7.26
) with the variables on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
-side. We define
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
to be the subset of graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
such that

•

\Gamma
is a subgraph of
\Gamma^{\prime}
;

•

all the edges in
\Gamma^{\prime}
that do not belong to
\Gamma
are of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\}
with
\underline{d}(+,i)=d
.

In words, the graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
are obtained by adding edges to
\Gamma
exactly as we did to describe the residue of
\sum\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
along
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-(d+1)/2
in Lemma
7.16
. For short, we set
\underline{z}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}:=\underline{z}_{\tau_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
and so on.

Recall that our goal is to compute the integral

I_{\tau,d+1}=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

Lemma 7.18
.

We have

I_{\tau,d+1}=\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{*}+\kappa_{d,d+1}(\Gamma^{\prime})}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

(7.35)

where

\kappa_{d,d+1}(\Gamma^{\prime})=-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+2d\underline{z}_{\Gamma^{\prime},n}+2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\Gamma^{\prime},n+1}.

Proof.

Note that the set
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
is finite. For every
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
, let
L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
be the polynomial defined in (
7.28
) which controls the poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}},d+1}^{\uparrow}
of (
7.27
). Denote by
(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime},k,c_{J}}
in (
3.15
), there exist
k^{\prime}>0
and
c>0
such that for all
T>0
we have

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;|\;\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T,\;\max\left|\Re(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq c(1+T)^{-k^{\prime}}\right\}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime},k,c_{J}}.

(7.36)

Note that
k
,
c_{J}
,
k^{\prime}
and
c
can be chosen independently of
\tau^{\prime}
by finiteness.

Let
D
be the maximum of the degrees of the
L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and take
N\geq Dk^{\prime}+1
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. By Lemma
7.15
, there exists
T>0
such that for every
\tau^{\prime}
and every index
i
we have

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}^{\uparrow}\\
\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\geq T\end{subarray}}\left((1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}}(J)}\left|L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\right|\right)\leq\varepsilon,

(7.37)

and moreover

(c/4)^{-D}(2T+1)^{Dk^{\prime}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}\\
\left|\lambda_{i}\right|\geq T\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N}}d\lambda\leq 1,\quad\frac{(c/4)^{-D}(2T+1)^{Dk^{\prime}}}{(1+T^{2})^{N/2}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}\\
\lambda_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N/2}}d\lambda\leq 1.

(7.38)

Let
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
such that all the coordinates of
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau^{\prime}}(+)
are distinct and of absolute value less than
c(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}}/4
, and all the other are zero. Set
\eta=3c(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}}
. Define

\kappa_{\tau^{\prime}}=-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+(2d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n}+2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n+1}.

This point is the middle of the segment
[\kappa_{d,d+1}(\Gamma^{\prime}),-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime}}]
.

Let
(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
be the image of some graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.15
, we can describe the poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region

\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+\nu\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime},k,c_{J}},\begin{array}[]{l}\nu_{n}\in[2d\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n},2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n}],\\
\nu_{n+1}=2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n+1}.\end{array}\right\},

(7.39)

which is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}^{\uparrow}
. Because
\tau
comes from a tuple belonging to
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d+1)
, by the definition of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
we see that we always have
\underline{d}(\tau^{\prime},2,j)>d+1
, so that the only possible poles in the region (
7.39
) are those of the product

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{+,i}(\tau^{\prime})\simeq\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau^{\prime},+,j)=d\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right).

(7.40)

We can also replace
L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
by this product.

We now go back to our initial representation
\tau
. By the previous discussion, we can move the contour in the integral to obtain

I_{\tau,d+1}=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.41)

For any
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}
we have

\left|L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\lambda)\right|\geq(\eta/4)^{D}\geq(c/4)^{D}(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}D}.

(7.42)

This implies that for any
i
we have by (
7.37
) and (
7.38
)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}\\
\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\geq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\left|\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\right|d\lambda\leq\varepsilon,

Therefore, there exists a constant
C
(independent of
\varepsilon
) such that

\left|I_{\tau,d+1}-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda\right|\leq C\varepsilon,

We focus on the second integral. By Fubini’s theorem, we can assume that we are integrating last in the variable
\lambda(+)_{1}
. We change the contour in this last integral, so that all the other variables are fixed. We want to go from
\Re(\lambda(+)_{1})=0
to
\Re(\lambda(+)_{1})=\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{1}
, still with the condition
\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T
. To achieve this we link these two segments with the two additional segments of real part
\Re(\lambda(+)_{1})\in[0,\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{1}]
and imaginary part
\Im(\lambda(+)_{1})=\pm T
. Let us denote this contour by
\gamma
. By the description of (
7.40
), our integrand has no poles inside
\gamma
. Indeed, the key is that with our choice of
\eta
,
\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}
has no coordinates with real part lying in
[-\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{1},\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{1}]
. Moreover, along the two real segments of
\gamma
, as in (
7.42
) we have the estimate
\left|L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\lambda)\right|\geq(c/4)^{D}(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}D}
. By (
7.37
) and (
7.38
), the integrals along these two real segments are bounded by the length of the segments (which can be assumed to be less than
1
) times
\varepsilon
. By repeating this change of contour for each variable
\lambda(+)_{i}
, up to changing
C
we arrive at

\left|I_{\tau,d+1}-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda\right|\leq C\varepsilon.

(7.43)

We denote the integral in (
7.43
) by
I_{\tau,d+1}(T)
.

We now change the contour in the variable
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1}
. We set

\underline{z}_{1}=\left(\underbrace{0,\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots,0}_{m_{+}(\tau)+m_{2}(\tau)-|I_{2}|+m_{1}(\tau)},1/4,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}\right)\in\mathfrak{a}_{Q,n}^{*}.

In words,
\underline{z}_{1}
is the vector in
\mathfrak{a}_{Q,n}^{*}
whose only non-zero coordinates is
1/4
above
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,1}
. We want to move from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}-2\eta\underline{z}_{1}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}
. By Fubini’s theorem, we can integrate in
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1}
last, so that we may consider that all the other variables are fixed. This means that we want to go from an integral on
\left|\Im(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})\right|\leq T
starting from
\Re(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})=(d+1)/2+\eta/4
to
\Re(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})=(d+1)/2-\eta/4
. By the description of (
7.40
) there is no pole in these two regions, or if
2T\geq\left|\Im(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c,1}})\right|>T
. We may therefore consider a contour
\gamma
linking our two segments from above and below, so that non singularities occur along
\gamma
. Note that
\left|L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\lambda)\right|
is bounded from below by
(c/4)^{D}(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}D}
along
\gamma
: along the two imaginary segments it follows from the same argument as in the previous change of contour, while in the two additional curves this condition can easily be met. Therefore, by (
7.37
) and (
7.38
) the two additional integrals in the region
\left|\Im(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c,1}})\right|>T
will be bounded by an absolute constant of
\varepsilon
. The singularities inside
\gamma
are those prescribed by (
7.40
), and they are simple as the coordinates of
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)
are distinct. By the residue theorem and our choices of measures, the integral along
\gamma
is equal to the sum of the residues. Note that in Lemma
7.16
we had computed this residues with respect to linear forms
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},j}-(d+1)/2
. However, here we are shifting the contour in the variable
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1}
so that a
-
sign will occur. By the computation from Lemma
7.16
, we conclude that, up to increasing
C
, we have

\left|I_{\tau,d+1}(T)-\left(\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}-2\eta\underline{z}_{1}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda+\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}}I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(T)\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon.

(7.44)

Here
\Gamma^{\prime}
ranges in the elements of
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
such that there is exactly one edge that does not belong to
\Gamma
and such that it is of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,1}\}
with
\underline{d}(+,i)=d
. In that case, we set for
\tau^{\prime}=\pi_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}=Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(T)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau^{\prime}}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

Note that here
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}
is identified with the element of
\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}
which has
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{1}
as its coordinates in
\lambda(1)_{1}
, and
\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}(+)_{i}
in
\lambda(+)_{i-1}
for
i\geq 2
. However, we claim that we can move the contour of this integral to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau^{\prime}}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau^{\prime}}
. Indeed, this change will take place in the variable
\lambda(1)_{1}
inside the region (
7.39
) and will not cross any poles thanks to (
7.40
), so that in particular
L_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
remains constant. By (
7.37
) and (
7.38
), we conclude that up to increasing the absolute constant
C
we may assume that

\left|I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(T)-I_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}(T)\right|\leq C\varepsilon.

(7.45)

Note that throughout this process the constant
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
.

We can now conclude the proof of Lemma
7.18
. By (
7.44
) and (
7.45
), we see that
I_{\tau,d+1}(T)
is, up to
C\varepsilon
, equal to the same integral shifted to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}+\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}-2\eta\underline{z}_{1}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}
(the absolute values of the imaginary parts still being bounded by
T
), plus a sum of
I_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}(T)
. Let us explain how to deal with each contribution.

For the first integral, we continue to change the contour in each variable
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}
by subtracting
2\eta\underline{z}_{i}
. By reproducing the exact same argument, we see that each steps adds a finite sum of
I_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}(T)
where
\tau^{\prime}
is obtained by adding an edge
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\}
with
\underline{d}(+,i)=d
to
\Gamma
. At the end, our integral takes place along
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}-\eta\underline{z}_{\tau,n}+\underline{\varepsilon}_{\tau}
(with the bound on the imaginary parts). But as in (
7.41
) and (
7.43
), by changes of contours (now generating no residues) we see that this is the integral along
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{d,d+1}(\Gamma)
up to
C\varepsilon
, this time with no bound on the imaginary parts. This is the contribution coming from
\Gamma
in (
7.35
).

We are now left with the remaining
I_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}(T)
. Because the
\tau^{\prime}
s are associated to graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
, their poles are also determined by (
7.40
) in the region (
7.39
) by Lemma
7.15
and we can reproduce the procedure used for
I_{\tau,d+1}(T)
. We conclude by induction because as
m_{\mathrm{c},1}(\tau^{\prime})<m_{\mathrm{c},1}(\tau)
, so that we have less variables to apply change of contour to. Note that at each step we will add an edge of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\}
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
, so that at the end we get a sum indexed by the graphs
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
. Moreover, because we change the contour of the variables in a certain order, each graph can only occur once.

We now see that (
7.35
) holds up to an absolute constant of
\varepsilon
, and we conclude by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
. This ends the proof of Lemma
7.18
.
∎

7.3.7
Changes of contours on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side

We keep our element
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d+1)
coming from the graph
\Gamma
and the notation from §
7.3.6
. We now define
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)
to be the subset of
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
such that

•

\Gamma
is a subgraph of
\Gamma^{\prime}
,

•

all the edges in
\Gamma^{\prime}
that do not belong to
\Gamma
are of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},1,j}\}
or
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\}
with
\underline{d}(+,i)=d
.

By changes of contours on the variables on the
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}
-side, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 7.19
.

We have

I_{\tau,d+1}=\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+2d\underline{z}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.46)

Proof.

The proof follows the same pattern as Lemma
7.18
, up to one major difference. By Lemma
7.18
, we can start from
(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
that comes from a graph
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)
and change the contour in the integral in (
7.35
). We now have to study the poles in the region

\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}+\nu\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\prime},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{\prime},k,c_{J}},\begin{array}[]{l}\nu_{n}=2d\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n},\\
\nu_{n+1}\in[2d\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n+1},2(d+1)\underline{z}_{\tau^{\prime},n+1}].\end{array}\right\},

(7.47)

The difference with Lemma
7.18
is that we can have some
i
such that
\underline{d}(\tau^{\prime},1,i)=d+1
. By Lemma
7.15
, we see that the poles in (
7.47
) are now along the zeros of

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{+,i}(\tau^{\prime})^{\vee}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau^{\prime},+,i)=d\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(+)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{1,i}(\tau^{\prime})^{\vee}\simeq\tau^{\prime}_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\\
\underline{d}(\tau^{\prime},1,i)=d+1\\
i\notin I_{1}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left(-\lambda(1)_{i}-\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{d+1}{2}\right).

We know the residues arising from these singularities thanks Lemma
7.16
. By reproducing the argument in Lemma
7.18
(i.e. truncate the integral to assume that the poles are simple), we see that

I_{\tau^{\prime},d+1}=\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\prime},n+1,d)}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}^{\uparrow}+2d\underline{z}_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}},\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

where
\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\prime},n+1,d)
is the set of graphs
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
and all the edges in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
that do not belong to
\Gamma^{\prime}
are of the form
\{\pi_{+,i},\pi_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\}
with
\underline{d}(+,i)=d
. To conclude, it remains to note that

\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)=\bigsqcup_{\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,n,d)}\mathcal{G}(\Gamma^{\prime},n+1,d).

∎

7.3.8
Counting the contributions

We now end the proof of Proposition
7.13
. We assume that (
7.25
) holds for
d+1\geq 2
. Let
\mathcal{G}(\pi,d)
be the subset of
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi)
such that
(I_{\Gamma},Q_{\Gamma},\pi_{\Gamma},I_{1,\Gamma},I_{2,\Gamma})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)
. We need to sum over elements in
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)
rather than graphs in
\mathcal{G}(\pi,d)
. This is the content of the following combinatorial result.

Lemma 7.20
.

We have

\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi,d+1)}\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)=\mathcal{G}(\pi,d).

Moreover, let
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi,d+1)
and let
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})
be its image. Then the fiber of the map

\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)\mapsto(I_{\Gamma^{\prime}},Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\pi_{\Gamma^{\prime}},I_{1,\Gamma^{\prime}},I_{2,\Gamma^{\prime}})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi,d)

(7.48)

above a point
(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
in the image is of cardinal
|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)||\mathrm{Stab}(\tau^{\prime})|^{-1}
.

Proof.

For the first point, that the union is disjoint follows from the fact that for each
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\pi,d)
there exists a unique graph
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi,d+1)
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained by adding edges to
\Gamma
. We also clearly have the equality as any
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}(\pi,d)
can be obtained by this procedure.

We move to the second assertion. Because there is a single element in the preimage of
W(\tau^{\downarrow}).\tau^{\downarrow}
under the map (
7.22
), we may assume that we have decompositions

\tau_{+}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}\tau_{+,i}^{\boxtimes k_{+,i}}\boxtimes\tau_{+,\neq d},\quad\tau_{\mathrm{c},1}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}^{\boxtimes k_{\mathrm{c},1,i}},\quad\tau_{\mathrm{c},2}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{k}(\sigma_{i}^{\vee})^{\boxtimes k_{\mathrm{c},2,i}},

for
k
some integer, where all the
\sigma_{i}
are mutually non-isomorphic cuspidal representations of some
\mathrm{GL}_{r}
’s, for all
i
we have
\tau_{+,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{i},d)
and
\tau_{+,\neq d}
is a product of Speh representations
\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma,d^{\prime})
with
\sigma
cuspidal and
d^{\prime}\neq d
. Note that we allow the
k_{+,i}
,
k_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
k_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
to be zero.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)
. Let
1\leq i\leq k
. Denote by
k_{1,i}
the number of vertices
\pi_{+,j}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
with
\pi_{+,j}\simeq\tau_{+,i}
,
\deg(+,1,j)=1
and
\deg(+,2,j)=0
,
k_{2,i}
the number of these vertices with
\deg(+,1,j)=0
and
\deg(+,2,j)=1
, and finally
k_{1,2,i}
the number of those with
\deg(+,1,j)=\deg(+,2,j)=1
. It is readily checked that the number of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(\Gamma,d)
with the same image as
\Gamma^{\prime}
under (
7.48
) is

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{k_{\mathrm{c},1,i}!k_{\mathrm{c},2,i}!k_{+,i}!}{(k_{\mathrm{c},1,i}-k_{1,i}-k_{1,2,i})!(k_{\mathrm{c},2,i}-k_{2,i}-k_{1,2,i})!k_{1,i}!k_{2,i}!k_{1,2,i}!(k_{+,i}-k_{1,i}-k_{2,i}-k_{1,2,i})!}\right).

If we denote by
(I^{\prime},Q^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime},I_{2}^{\prime})
the image of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, this is exactly
|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)||\mathrm{Stab}(\tau^{\prime})|^{-1}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

7.3.9
End of the proof of Propositions
7.13
and
7.11

Proposition
7.13
is now a consequence of Lemma
7.19
and Lemma
7.20
. This also concludes the proof of Proposition
7.11
by taking
d=1
.

7.3.10
Conclusion

We can now write our new expression of the Rankin–Selberg period.

Proposition 7.21
.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathrm{Stab}(\pi)|}{|W(\pi)||\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

(7.49)

where
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
is the set of tuples obtained from the null graph built from
w\pi
with
w
being any element in
W(\pi)
such that
\underline{d}(+,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(+,m_{+})
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition
7.4
and Proposition
7.11
. The only thing that we have to check is that the expression in the last line of (
7.49
) is independent from the choice of
w\in W(\pi)
. But this follows from Lemma
7.10
.
∎

7.4
Additional residues from the regularized period

In this section, we continue the computation of the spectral expansion of
\int_{[H]}f(h)dh
by performing a final shift in the contour of the integrals of (
7.49
). In contrast with that of §
7.3
, this wave of shifts will produce additional contributions coming from residues of the regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
.

7.4.1
Statement of the result

Throughout this section, we fix
0\leq r\leq n
and
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. We then fix
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
. Our goal is to compute the integral

I_{\tau}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2\underline{z}_{\tau}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.50)

As in §
7.3.1
, the integral
I_{\tau}
will give rise to several contributions that we will parametrize by graphs. However, the definition is less involved than in §
7.3.1
.

As usual, we can write

\displaystyle\tau_{n}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{2}}\tau_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\tau_{1,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},1}}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i},

(7.51)

\displaystyle\tau_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\tau_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{1}}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\tau_{2,i}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{\mathrm{c},2}}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}.

(7.52)

Because the reference to
\pi
will not be needed in our discussion, we simply write
\tau_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i},\underline{d}(1,i))
and so on. We denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
the set of undirected simple graphs
\Gamma
such that

•

the vertices of
\Gamma
are
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,1},\ldots,\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,m_{1}}
,
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,1},\ldots,\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,m_{2}}
(with multiplicity);

•

the edges of
\Gamma
are of the form
\{\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i},\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\}
for some
i
and
j
with
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}
;

•

the degree of each vertex is
0
or
1
.

For each
i
, we denote by
\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)
(resp.
\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)
) the degree of
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
(resp.
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
). There is a bijection

i\in\{1\leq i\leq m_{\mathrm{c},1}\;|\;\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=1\}\mapsto j(i)\in\{1\leq j\leq m_{\mathrm{c},2}\;|\;\deg(\mathrm{c},2,j)=1\},

(7.53)

such that
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j(i)}
is the only neighbor of
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
. We can then define a representation
\tau_{\Gamma}
of a standard Levi
M_{R_{\Gamma}}
of
G
by

\displaystyle\tau_{\Gamma,n}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\tau_{+,i}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{2}\end{subarray}}^{m_{2}}\tau_{2,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\tau_{1,i}\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=1}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i},

(7.54)

\displaystyle\tau_{\Gamma,n+1}

\displaystyle=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\tau_{+,i}^{\vee}\boxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\notin I_{1}\end{subarray}}^{m_{1}}\tau_{1,i}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{2}}\tau_{2,i}\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=1}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j(i)}.

(7.55)

Associated to the representation
\tau_{\Gamma}
is a natural tuple
J_{\Gamma}=(n_{+},n_{1},n_{\mathrm{c},1},n_{2},n_{\mathrm{c},2},n_{-})
. More precisely, we have
n_{-}=\sum_{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)}n_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
where as usual
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
is a cuspidal representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{n_{\mathrm{c},1,i}}
. We then see that (
7.53
) defines two sets
J_{1,\Gamma}
and
J_{2,\Gamma}
such that
(J_{\Gamma},R_{\Gamma},\tau_{\Gamma},J_{1,\Gamma},J_{2,\Gamma})\in\Pi^{\uparrow}_{H}
. We denote by
\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
the set of tuples obtained this way. Note that
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
because it is the image of the null graph. To keep track of this starting tuple, we will write the elements of
\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
with the letter
J
rather than
I
. We hope that this does not cause confusion with our fixed level (which is also denoted by
J
).

Because
\tau
is fixed, we will typically write
\delta
for the representation
\tau_{\Gamma}
. In this situation, we will have
\delta_{1,i}=\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{1,i}(\delta),\underline{d}(\delta,1,i))
and so on. We will need the element

\underline{z}_{\delta}:=((0,0,0,1/4,0,0),(0,0,0,1/4,0,0))\in\mathfrak{a}_{R}^{*},

(7.56)

where the
1/4
only lives above
\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},1,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\underset{\deg(\mathrm{c},2,i)=0}{\boxtimes}\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,i}
.

Note that the fiber of the map

(J^{\prime},R^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},J_{1}^{\prime},J_{2}^{\prime})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)\mapsto(R^{{}^{\prime},\downarrow},\delta^{{}^{\prime},\downarrow})\in\Pi_{H}

above the set
W(\delta^{\downarrow}).\delta^{\downarrow}
is always reduced to
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})
. As in §
7.3.1
, we write
\mathrm{Stab}(\delta)
for the stabilizer of
\delta^{\downarrow}
in
W(\delta^{\downarrow})
.

The goal of §
7.4
is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 7.22
.

We have

I_{\tau}=\sum_{(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)}\frac{|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)|}{|\mathrm{Stab}(\delta)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R^{\downarrow},\delta^{\downarrow}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

The proof of Proposition
7.22
will run through §
7.4
and end in §
7.4.9
. Once again, it will be proved by changing the contour in several steps. Because most arguments are similar to those used in the course of §
7.3
to prove Proposition
7.11
, we shall give less details when reasonable.

7.4.2
A short description of the argument

As in §
7.3.2
, we give the main ideas behind the proof of Proposition
7.22
. Our goal is to bring the domain of integration in
I_{\tau}
from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+2\underline{z}_{\tau}
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau^{\downarrow}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau^{\downarrow}}
. We do this in three steps.

This first one is to bring our integral to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+\underline{z}_{\tau}
. This is a delicate maneuver as our integrand will have singularities in this region. They are computed in §
7.4.3
and all come from the regularized period
\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}
, and more precisely from isomorphisms
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}
. We determine the associated residues in §
7.4.4
. They can be expressed as relative characters living the induction of
w\tau
for
w
an element in the Weyl group acting by blocks on
\tau
. The idea here is rather simple : the two representations
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}
will be moved from the "
(\mathrm{c},1)
" and "
(\mathrm{c},2)
" parts of
\tau
to the "
-
" one. Once again, to keep track of these residues we use graphs and add an edge between the vertices corresponding to our representations if we cross the relevant singularity. Our constraints mean that this can happen at most once for each
\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,i}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}
, and the representation
\tau_{\Gamma}
described in (
7.54
) and (
7.55
) is the
w\tau
alluded to above.

As noted earlier, our integrand in fact has poles along
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+t\underline{z}_{\tau}
exactly when
t=1
. Our goal is to integrate in this region with
t<1
, thus collecting the residues. The issue is that this makes
-\overline{\lambda}
leave the positive Weyl chamber in which we control
E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})
. To solve this problem, we use the fact that the regions
\mathcal{R}_{\tau,k,c}
described in §
3.4.10
go a little bit beyond this chamber. This allows us to integrate over
i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+(1-\eta)\underline{z}_{\tau}
and
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T
with
\eta
small and
T
large, while controlling the size of the tail. This is done in §
7.4.5
. We end up with a sum of relative characters indexed by graphs
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
, with corresponding
\delta=\tau_{\Gamma}
, integrated along
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}+(1-\eta)\underline{z}_{\delta}
and
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T
.

At that point, we use a change of variabless to replace
\delta
with some
w\delta
in order to bring our integral back in the positive Weyl chamber. Once again, this is possible as the associated
\mathcal{R}
-regions overlap. It turns out that
w\delta
is simply the representation
\delta_{\emptyset}
described in §
6.2.6
. After taking into account our twists, and after an easy manipulation, our integral now takes place in the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-(1-\eta)(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})
with the additional requirement
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T
. We may now shift the contour to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
and lift the requirement on the imaginary parts as our integrand is regular in the corresponding strip. This is the content of §
7.4.6
. It then remains to express our result in terms of
\delta^{\downarrow}
rather than
\delta_{\emptyset}
(§
7.4.7
) and to sum over
\delta^{\downarrow}
rather than graphs in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
(§
7.4.8
) to conclude the proof of Proposition
7.22
.

7.4.3
Poles

Let
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
. Our region of interest will be

\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}:=\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}+\underline{z}_{\delta}\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\delta,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\delta,k,c_{J}}\right\},

(7.57)

where we take
k
and
c_{J}
given by Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
6.11
. In the special case where
\delta=\tau
, we also set

\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow,+}:=\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+\nu\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\tau,k,c_{J}},\;\nu\in[\underline{z}_{\tau},2\underline{z}_{\tau}]\right\},

(7.58)

Lemma 7.23
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)
, the possible poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
(or
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow,+}
) are along the zeros of the polynomial

\prod_{\delta_{\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\delta_{\mathrm{c},2,j}^{\vee}}\left(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},j}-\frac{1}{2}\right)

(7.59)

Moreover, it is of rapid decay in the sense that if we denote by
L
the polynomial in (
7.59
), for all
N>0
there exists
C>0
such that for all
\lambda
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
(or
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow,+}
) we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)}\left|L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\right|\leq\frac{C}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N}}.

(7.60)

In particular, the map
\lambda\mapsto\sum_{\varphi}L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
is holomorphic in these regions.

Proof.

This is proved by reproducing the argument of Lemma
7.15
with
d=0
. The key point is that we always have
\underline{\rho}_{\delta}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta}\in\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{R}^{*,+}}
which lets us use Corollary
4.19
to locate the poles of the Eisenstein series. Note that contrary to the situation of Lemma
7.15
, the poles in (
7.59
) come from the period
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)
rather than the Eisenstein series.
∎

Remark 7.24
.

We emphasize that the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
is an a sense the largest in which we can control the poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
. Indeed, note that in coordinates (with respect to the decomposition of
\delta
given in (
7.54
) and (
7.55
))

\underline{\rho}_{\delta}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta}=\left((1/4,1/4,-1/4,-1/4,-1/4),(1/4,1/4,-1/4,-1/4,-1/4)\right).

In the special case
\delta=\tau
we get for
t\in\mathbb{R}
with the coordinates of (
7.51
) and (
7.52
) instead

\underline{\rho}_{\tau}+\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}-t\underline{z}_{\tau}=\left((1/4,1/4,-1/4,-t/4),(1/4,1/4,-1/4,-t/4)\right),

so that we need
t\geq 1
(i.e.
\nu\geq\underline{z}_{\tau}
) in the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow,+}
given in (
7.58
).

7.4.4
Computation of the residues

We keep
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
and describe the residues obtained along the affine hyperplanes cut out by (
7.59
). We assume that this tuple comes from a graph
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
.

Let
1\leq i_{1}\leq m_{\mathrm{c},1}(\delta)
and
1\leq i_{2}\leq m_{\mathrm{c},2}(\delta)
such that
\delta_{\mathrm{c},1,i_{1}}\simeq\delta_{\mathrm{c},2,i_{2}}
. Let
\Lambda
be the affine linear form
\Lambda(\lambda)=\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},i_{1}}+\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},i_{2}}-1/2
. If we keep track of the order in (
7.51
) and (
7.52
), there exist uniquely determined indices
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
such that
\delta_{\mathrm{c},1,i_{1}}=\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j_{1}}
and
\delta_{\mathrm{c},2,i_{2}}=\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j_{2}}
. We denote by
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
the graph obtained by adding the edge
\{\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,j_{1}},\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j_{2}}\}
to
\Gamma
and by
(J_{\Lambda},R_{\Lambda},\tau_{\Lambda},J_{1,\Gamma},J_{2,\Gamma})
the tuple in
\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
associated to
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
. It gives rise to a regularized period
\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}
.

As in our previous computations of residues from §
7.3.5
, we have a unique element
w
acting by blocks on
M_{R}
such that
w\delta=\tau_{\Lambda}
. Once again, we need to ask that it preserves the order to ensure that it is unique. Moreover, up to choosing appropriately our constants, we have

w\left(\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap(\mathfrak{a}_{\delta,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow})\right)=\mathfrak{a}_{\tau_{\Lambda},\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\Lambda}}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow},

and

w\left(\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}\right)\subset\mathcal{R}_{\tau_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}.

Lemma 7.25
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap(\mathfrak{a}_{\delta,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow})
in general position we have

\underset{\Lambda}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\mu)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\mu})\rangle_{G}\right)(\lambda)=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\Lambda},\tau_{\Lambda}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{\Lambda}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,w\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-w\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we set
R^{\prime}=R_{\Lambda}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\tau_{\Lambda}
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)
. By the proof of Lemma
7.23
, we know that the singularity comes from the period
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\mu)
. By the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
from (
6.32
), we have

\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda):=\mathcal{P}^{R^{\uparrow}}(M(w^{\uparrow},\lambda)\varphi_{R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}},w^{\uparrow}\lambda),

where the parabolic subgroups
R^{\uparrow}
and
R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
as well as the element
w_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
are described in §
6.2.2
. In particular, the intertwining operator
M(w^{\uparrow},\lambda)\varphi_{R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}}
is regular for
\lambda
in general position in the affine hyperplane
\Lambda^{-1}(0)
. We may therefore apply Proposition
5.13
to compute the residues. This proposition gives us a Rankin–Selberg parabolic subgroup
\widetilde{R}
and an element
w^{\prime}
acting by blocks on
w^{\uparrow}.R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
such that for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-1}(\{0\})\cap(\mathfrak{a}_{\delta,\mathbb{C}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow})
in general position we have

\underset{\Lambda}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(\mathcal{P}^{R^{\uparrow}}(M(w^{\uparrow},\mu)\varphi_{R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}},w^{\uparrow}\mu)\right)(\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{R}}\left(M(w^{\prime}w^{\uparrow},\lambda)\varphi_{R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}},w^{\prime}w^{\uparrow}\lambda\right).

Let us denote by
R^{{}^{\prime},\uparrow}
and
w^{{}^{\prime},\uparrow}
the parabolic subgroup and element in the Weyl group built for
\delta^{\prime}
in §
6.2.2
. Then it follows from the description of
w^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{S}
in Proposition
5.13
that there exists
w_{\mathbf{M}}\in W_{\mathbf{M}}(\delta^{\prime})
such that
w^{\prime}w^{\uparrow}=w_{\mathbf{M}}w^{{}^{\prime},\uparrow}w
and
\widetilde{R}=w_{\mathbf{M}}.R^{{}^{\prime},\uparrow}
. With the notation of §
6.2.4
,
w_{\mathbf{M}}
corresponds to a permutation in
\mathfrak{S}(m_{-}(\delta^{\prime}))
whose purpose is to put the components of
\delta^{\prime}_{-}
in the order prescribed by (
7.54
) and (
7.55
). By the functional equation of Lemma
6.10
, we conclude that

\underset{\Lambda}{\mathrm{Res}}\left(\mathcal{P}^{R^{\uparrow}}(M(w^{\uparrow},\mu)\varphi_{R_{\delta}^{\uparrow}},w^{\uparrow}\mu)\right)(\lambda)=P_{\delta^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}(M(w,\lambda)\varphi,w\lambda).

It remains to do a change of variables in the sum over
\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)
and to use the functional equation of Eisenstein series from Theorem
4.17
to conclude.
∎

7.4.5
First change of contours

We can now write the result of the first change of contours of this section. Recall that we have fixed
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
. As in §
7.3.6
, we take
c>0
and
k^{\prime}>0
such that for all
T>0
and
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
we have

\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\delta,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\;|\;\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T,\;\max\left|\Re(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq c(T+1)^{-k^{\prime}}\right\}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\delta,k,c_{J}}.

(7.61)

For any
T>0
, we then set

\eta(T)=4c(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}}.

(7.62)

We begin with the following lemma. Recall that our goal is compute the integral
I_{\tau}
from (
7.50
).

Lemma 7.26
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
T_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for every
T>T_{\varepsilon}
we have

\left|I_{\tau}-\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau_{\Gamma}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma}-\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma}^{\uparrow}+(1-\eta(T))\underline{z}_{\Gamma}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\Gamma},\tau_{\Gamma}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{\Gamma}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda\right|\leq\varepsilon.

(7.63)

Remark 7.27
.

Note that in (
7.63
) the bounds "
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T
" in the domain of integration are necessary. Indeed, if not for them we would leave the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau_{\Gamma}}^{\uparrow}
as
\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma}+\underline{\rho}_{\Gamma}^{\uparrow}-(1-\eta(T))\underline{z}_{\Gamma}
does not belong to
\overline{\mathfrak{a}_{R_{\Gamma}}^{*,+}}
(see Remark
7.24
).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. The argument is very similar to the one used in Lemma
7.18
, so that we only sketch the main steps. For any
T
, let
\underline{\varepsilon}(T)\in\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}
such that
\left\lVert\underline{\varepsilon}(T)\right\rVert<(2T+1)^{-k^{\prime}}/2
,
\underline{\varepsilon}(T)_{n}=0
and all the coordinates of
\underline{\varepsilon}(T)_{n+1}
are zero except those in the variables
\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},1},\ldots,\lambda(2)_{\mathrm{c},m_{\mathrm{c},2}}
which are all different. For any
T
set

\kappa_{\tau}(T)=-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}-\underline{\rho}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}+(1+2\eta(T))\underline{z}_{\tau,n}+(1-\eta(T))\underline{z}_{\tau,n+1}+\underline{\varepsilon}(T).

(7.64)

By the estimates of Lemma
7.23
and change of contour of integration (in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow,+}
), we see that we may choose
T_{0}
so that for any
T>T_{0}
we have

\left|I_{\tau}-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}(T)\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda\right|\leq\varepsilon.

Note that we are now in
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}
.

We focus on the second integral. By Fubini’s theorem, we can integrate last along
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1}
. We consider
\gamma
the contour composed of the two imaginary segments
\left|\Im(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})\right|\leq T
along
\Re(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})=1/4+\eta(T)/2
and
\Re(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})=1/4-\eta(T)/4
, and of two curves in the region
2T\geq\left|\Im(\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1})\right|\geq T
linking their ends. By Lemma
7.23
, there are no singularities along this contour and we can bound the integral along the two additional curves. This holds because we have the upper bound (
7.60
) and also a lower bound for the product (
7.59
), courtesy of our choices of
\eta(T)
and
\underline{\varepsilon}(T)
. By the computation of the residues in Lemma
7.25
and further changes of contours, we end up, for every
T>T_{0}
, with

\left|I_{\tau}-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}+\kappa_{\tau}(T)-3\eta(T)\underline{z}_{1}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{Q,\tau}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda-\sum_{\Gamma}I_{\Gamma}(T)\right|\leq C\varepsilon,

where
C
is an absolute constant independent from
T
,
\underline{z}_{1}
is the element in
\mathfrak{a}_{\tau}^{*}
whose only non zero coordinate is
\lambda(1)_{\mathrm{c},1}=1/4
, the sum ranges over graphs
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
who have only one edge of the form
\{\tau_{\mathrm{c},1,1},\tau_{\mathrm{c},2,j}\}
and

I_{\Gamma}(T)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\tau_{\Gamma}}^{*}+\kappa_{\Gamma}(T)\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\Gamma},\tau_{\Gamma}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\tau_{\Gamma}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

\kappa_{\Gamma}(T)
being defined as in (
7.64
) for
\tau_{\Gamma}
. Note that the region of integration is included in
\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
thanks to (
7.61
). We now conclude the proof of Lemma
7.26
as in Lemma
7.18
, by doing an induction on the number of variables.
∎

7.4.6
Second change of contours

In this section, we fix
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
. Let
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})
be the corresponding tuple in
\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}
. We have the tuple
(J_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset},R_{\emptyset})\in\Pi_{H}
from §
6.2.6
. As in (
7.56
), we set

\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}:=((0,0,0,1/4,0,0),(0,0,0,1/4,0,0))\in\mathfrak{a}_{R}^{*},

where the
1/4
only lives above
\boxtimes_{i}\delta_{\emptyset,\mathrm{c},1,i}\boxtimes_{j}\delta_{\emptyset,\mathrm{c},2,j}
.
As noted in (
6.42
), we have

w_{\emptyset}(\underline{\rho}_{\delta}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta})=\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}.

(7.65)

Note that
\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}\in\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in this case.

We now consider the region

\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}:=\left\{\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}-t(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})\;\middle|\;\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\delta_{\emptyset},k,c_{J}},0\leq t\leq 1\right\},

(7.66)

where once again we ask that
k
and
c_{J}
are those prescribed by Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
6.11
. By (
7.65
) we have
w_{\emptyset}\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}\subset\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
.

Lemma 7.28
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset}}(J)
, the possible poles of
\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
of (
7.66
) are along the zeros of the polynomial

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta_{\emptyset,\mathrm{c},1,i}\simeq\delta^{\vee}_{\emptyset,\mathrm{c},2,j}\end{subarray}}\left(\lambda(1)_{i}+\lambda(2)_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\right)

(7.67)

Moreover, it is of rapid decay in the sense that if we denote by
L
the polynomial in (
7.59
), for all
N>0
there exists
C>0
such that for all
\lambda
in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
we have

\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset}}}\left|L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}\right|\leq\frac{C}{(1+\left\lVert\lambda\right\rVert^{2})^{N}}.

(7.68)

In particular,
\varphi\mapsto\sum_{\varphi}L(\lambda)\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
.

Remark 7.29
.

Note that the polynomial
L
from (
7.67
) is non-zero in the region
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}-t(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})
as soon as
t<1
.

Proof.

This is the same proof as Lemma
7.23
, and relies on Corollary
4.19
(poles of Eisenstein series) and on Proposition
6.11
.
∎

We now set

I_{\delta_{\emptyset}}:=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.69)

This integral is well-defined and absolutely convergent by Lemma
7.28
.

Lemma 7.30
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
T>0
such that

\left|I_{\delta_{\emptyset}}-\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}+(1-\eta(T))\underline{z}_{\delta}\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda\right|\leq\varepsilon.

(7.70)

Proof.

By the bounds and localization of the singularities from Lemma
7.25
, we see that by change of contours, up to increasing
T
we can assume that the integral on the right of (
7.70
) differs in absolute value by a constant times
\varepsilon
from

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta}^{*}+(1-\eta(T))(\underline{z}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta}^{\uparrow})\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda

(7.71)

Indeed, the key point is that this region of integration remains in
\mathcal{R}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
by (
7.61
) and that we have a lower bound for the factor (
7.59
) from Lemma
7.23
, both information being available to us thanks to our choice of
\eta(T)
in (
7.62
). Moreover, this estimate will remain true for any
T
large enough.

By Lemma
6.14
, for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R,\delta}(J)
we have the relation

\mathcal{P}_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)=\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(M(w_{\emptyset},\lambda)\varphi,w_{\emptyset}\lambda),

where
w_{\emptyset}
was defined in §
6.2.6
. By changes of variables and thanks to the relation (
6.42
), we see that (
7.71
) is equal to

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-(1-\eta(T))(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}+\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

Because we are now in the region
\mathcal{R}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
, we can use Lemma
7.28
to conclude by changing yet again the contour that, up to increasing
T
, this integral differs in absolute value by a constant times
\varepsilon
from

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}-(1-\eta(T))(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})\\
\max\left|\Im(\lambda_{i})\right|\leq T\end{subarray}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R_{\emptyset},\delta_{\emptyset}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

It remains to do one last change of contour to bring the region of integration from
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}-(1-\eta(T))(\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{\uparrow}-\underline{z}_{\delta_{\emptyset}})
to
i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
, which is possible by Lemma
7.28
because we do not cross any poles as noted in Remark
7.29
. This yields (
7.70
) and concludes the proof of Lemma
7.30
.
∎

7.4.7
Going back to the non-increasing period

We keep our graph
\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
and
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}
the corresponding tuple. Our goal is to express
I_{\delta_{\emptyset}}
(defined in (
7.69
)) in terms of the regularized Rankin–Selberg period
\mathcal{P}
, defined in Chapter
6
, rather than
\mathcal{P}^{\uparrow}
. To do this, we use the
\delta\mapsto\delta^{\downarrow}\in\Pi_{H}
construction described in §
6.2.3
.

Lemma 7.31
.

We have

I_{\delta_{\emptyset}}=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R^{\downarrow},\delta^{\downarrow}}}\mathcal{P}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.15
and some changes of variables.
∎

7.4.8
Counting the contributions

The last thing that we have to do is to sum over tuples
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
rather than graphs
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)
. This is the content of the next lemma.

Lemma 7.32
.

The fiber of the map

\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{c}}(\tau)\mapsto(J_{\Gamma},R_{\Gamma},\tau_{\Gamma},J_{1,\Gamma},J_{2,\Gamma})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)

above a point in the image
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})
is of cardinal
|\mathrm{Stab}(\tau)||\mathrm{Stab}(\delta)|^{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as that of Lemma
7.20
.
∎

7.4.9
End of the proof of Proposition
7.22

The proposition now follows from Lemmas
7.26
,
7.30
and
7.31
by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
, and by using the combinatorial result of Lemma
7.32
.

7.4.10
Conclusion

We write out explicitly the consequences of our work for the computation of
\int_{[H]}f(h)dh
. It follows from the combination of Proposition
7.21
and Proposition
7.22
.

Proposition 7.33
.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}}\sum_{(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)}\sum_{(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathrm{Stab}(\pi)|}{|W(\pi)||\mathrm{Stab}(\delta)|}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}^{*}-\underline{\rho}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{R^{\downarrow},\delta^{\downarrow}}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\delta^{\downarrow}}(\varphi,\lambda)\langle f,E(\varphi,-\overline{\lambda})\rangle_{G}d\lambda.

(7.72)

where
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
is the set of tuples obtained from the null graph built from
w\pi
with
w
being any element in
W(\pi)
such that
\underline{d}(+,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(+,m_{+})
.

7.5
End of the proof of Theorem
7.1

We can finally conclude the proof of Theorem
7.1
. It is now only a matter of changing the indices in our sums. For the reader’s convenience, we restate the result.

Theorem 7.34
.

We have

\int_{[H]}f(h)dh=\sum_{(P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}}\frac{1}{\left|W(\pi)\right|}\int_{\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{\pi}^{*}}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}_{P,\pi}(J)}\mathcal{P}_{\pi}(\varphi,\lambda-\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\langle f,E(\varphi,\lambda+\underline{\rho}_{\pi})\rangle_{G}d\lambda,

(7.73)

where the integral on the RHS is absolutely convergent.

Proof.

To prove Theorem
7.34
, we need to rewrite Proposition
7.33
. Let
I_{\delta^{\downarrow}}
be the last integral in (
7.72
). By the functional equation of Eisenstein series (Theorem
4.17
) and of
\mathcal{P}_{\pi}
(Corollary
6.5
), for any
w\in W(\delta^{\downarrow})
we have

I_{w\delta^{\downarrow}}=I_{\delta^{\downarrow}}.

(7.74)

Let
0\leq r\leq n
. Let
\Pi_{H,r}^{\uparrow}
be the set of elements
(I_{r},P,\pi)
in
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. We denote by
\Pi_{H,r}^{\uparrow}/W
the quotient of
\Pi_{H,r}^{\uparrow}
by the relation
(I_{r},P_{1},\pi_{1})\sim(I_{r},P_{2},\pi_{2})
if
\pi_{1}=w\pi_{2}
for some
w\in W(\pi_{2})
. Note that because of the shape of
I_{r}
(see (
7.6
)), if
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
and
w\in W(\pi)
, then
(I_{r},w.P,w\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
. Moreover, the set
\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
is independent of the class of
\pi
.

It follows from (
7.74
) that the RHS of (
7.72
) is equal to

\sum_{r=0}^{n}\sum_{(I_{r},\overline{P},\overline{\pi})\in\Pi_{H,r}^{\uparrow}/W}\sum_{(I,R,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\overline{\pi})}\sum_{(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Stab}(\delta)|}I_{\delta^{\downarrow}},

(7.75)

where we write again
(I_{r},\overline{P},\overline{\pi})
for a representative of its class.

We also define an equivalence relation on
\Pi_{H}
by declaring that
\pi_{1}\sim\pi_{2}
if
\pi_{1}=w\pi_{2}
for some
w\in W(\pi_{2})
. Let us denote by
\Pi_{H}/W
the quotient. We claim that we have a bijection

(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\left(\bigcup_{r=0}^{n}\bigcup_{(I_{r},\overline{P},\overline{\pi})\in\Pi_{H,r}^{\uparrow}/W}\bigcup_{(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\overline{\pi})}\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)\right)\mapsto\delta^{\downarrow}\in\Pi_{H}/W.

(7.76)

We explain how to build its inverse. We start from
\overline{\Pi}\in\Pi_{H}/W
. We can choose a representative
\Pi
so that we have the refinements of the decompositions of (
6.2
) and (
6.3
)

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\boxtimes\left(\pi_{1,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}\right)\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}\right),

\displaystyle\Pi_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes\left(\pi_{2,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}\right)\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\left(\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}^{\vee}\right),

where the representations with a "
\mathrm{cusp}
" index are cuspidal, while those with a "
\mathrm{res}
" index are residual. Moreover, we may assume that inside each
\pi_{\ldots}
, the blocks are ordered increasingly. For example, this means that if we write

\pi_{+}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{+,i},\underline{d}(+,i)),

then we ask that
\underline{d}(+,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(+,m_{+})
. We now define
\pi_{n,\mathrm{res}}
and
\pi_{n+1,\mathrm{res}}
to be the unique reordering of the blocks of
\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}
and
\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}^{\vee}
respectively, such that, if we write

\pi_{n,\mathrm{res}}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{n,i},\underline{d}(n,i)),\quad\pi_{n+1,\mathrm{res}}=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{m_{n+1}}\mathrm{Speh}(\sigma_{n+1,i},\underline{d}(n+1,i)),

(7.77)

then
\underline{d}(n,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(n,m_{n})
and
\underline{d}(n,1)\geq\ldots\geq\underline{d}(n,m_{n+1})
, and moreover such that the respective blocks of
\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}
,
\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}
,
\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}
and
\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}^{\vee}
remain in the same order.

It follows from our construction that
\Pi=\delta^{\downarrow}
for
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
which is defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{n}

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{n,\mathrm{res}}\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}},

(7.78)

\displaystyle\delta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1,\mathrm{res}}\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\vee},.

(7.79)

Here
J
is defined in the obvious way and the sets
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
correspond to the indices of the discrete automorphic representations
\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}
and
\pi_{-,\mathrm{res}}^{\vee}
in
\pi_{n,\mathrm{res}}
and
\pi_{n+1,\mathrm{res}}
respectively .

We then have
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\in\Pi_{H,\mathrm{c}}^{\uparrow}(\tau)
where
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})
is also defined by (
7.78
) and (
7.79
), up to grouping
\pi_{1,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}
and
\pi_{2,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\vee}
together.

With the notation of (
7.77
), we set
\sigma_{n}=\boxtimes_{i}\sigma_{n,i}
and
\sigma_{n+1}=\boxtimes_{i}\sigma_{n+1,i}
. Now
(I,Q,\tau,I_{1},I_{2})\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}(\pi)
with
\pi
satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{+}\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{n,\mathrm{res}}^{-}\right)\boxtimes\left(\sigma_{n}\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}\right),

\displaystyle\pi_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\pi_{+}^{\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{1,\mathrm{res}}^{-,\vee}\boxtimes\pi_{n+1,\mathrm{res}}^{-}\right)\boxtimes\left(\sigma_{n+1}\boxtimes\pi_{2,\mathrm{cusp}}\boxtimes\pi_{-,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\vee}\right).

Then
(I_{r},P,\pi)\in\Pi_{H}^{\uparrow}
for some
0\leq r\leq n
and some standard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
, so that the map
\overline{\Pi}\mapsto(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})
lands in the union of (
7.76
). By construction, it is the inverse of
(J,R,\delta,J_{1},J_{2})\mapsto\delta^{\downarrow}
, so that (
7.76
) is indeed a bijection.

Finally, we note that the unions in (
7.76
) are disjoint. This can be proved by similar arguments as the ones we just used. We then see that (
7.73
) follows from (
7.75
) and the orbit-counting theorem.
∎
